<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Mercurius Trismegistus (Hermes) and Esculapius (Asclepius) regarding the nature of the Divine and the "Good." It follows the previous page’s discussion on the hierarchy of beings.</meta>

### MERCURIUS. Folio 11

In common speech, "the Good" <note>original: "bonum"</note> is proclaimed by all. However, what it truly is, is not understood by everyone. For this reason, God is not known by all. Truly, because of ignorance, they call both various gods and even some men "good," yet these can never truly be good, nor even become so. Therefore, all the other immortal gods are honored by the name of God; but God is the Good not merely by way of honor, but by His very nature. For the nature of God is one: namely, the Good itself. Indeed, there is one essence in both names <note>referring to "God" and "The Good"</note>, from which all kinds of things flow. For the Good bestows all things and receives nothing; God offers everything and takes nothing. Therefore, God is the Good, and the Good is God.

The other name for God is "Father," for this reason: because He begets all things. For the duty of a father is to generate.
<margin>The Father.</margin>
For this reason, the procreation of children is considered the most excellent pursuit in life, even among the wise. Indeed, the greatest calamity and impiety happens to the one who departs from this life without children. For this reason, he is punished by <term>demons</term> <note>In this context, "demons" refers to intermediary spirits or "daemons" who act as executors of cosmic justice, rather than purely evil beings.</note> after death. The punishment, finally, is of this kind: that the sterile soul is judged to migrate into a body that has the nature of neither male nor female. This body is indeed one that is raised and bound by the sun. Therefore, O Esculapius, have no commerce with a man who has fathered no children. Yet pity his unhappiness, knowing what evils await him after his passing. Let these things, so many and of such quality, be said to you, O Esculapius, from which a certain foreknowledge of the nature of all things is gathered.

---

<gloss>
The second part discourses on how God is understandable in a rational way to man alone. From the center, the pole, the location, and the forms of things, one "hunts" for God Himself (around whom, in whom, and from whom all things move). He is stable, immobile, and always remains the same as Himself, and nothing in the world can be empty. In truth, those things which seem empty actually emulate the highest fullness, according to true plenitude. Soon, he suggests to Esculapius through his answers that it is easier to say what God is *not* than what He *is*. Nevertheless, God is the absolute Good and the Father, whose duty is to beget all things. This understanding of internal generation—which remains within the very nature of God—brings about the piety of divine religion. The name of "the Good" belongs to God alone.
</gloss>

<summary>Mercurius explains that God and "the Good" are synonymous, defining God as a "Father" who begets all things and warning that childlessness is a spiritual calamity. The text also touches on the "intelligibility" of God through the study of the physical cosmos.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius, The Good, Procreation, Divine Nature, Punishments after death, Fullness vs. Vacuum, Monotheism</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 11, which likely discussed the nature of the soul and the importance of spiritual "offspring" or virtues over physical lineage.</meta>

[[page number: 12]]
[[header: I ii]]

...contends: this harmonizes with the **truth of the Gospel**: <note original: "quid me vocas bonū: nemo bonus niſi ſolus deus">"Why do you call me good? No one is good but God alone."</note> <note>This is a direct reference to Mark 10:18 or Luke 18:19, showing how Renaissance translators sought to align these ancient pagan texts with Christian scripture.</note> And what he adds concerning the extreme calamity of those dying without children is a **veiled speech**. For one should not conceive of this as a bodily birth, but as a birth of the **soul**: consider its children to be the offspring of **virtues**, and masculine **wisdom** for the sake of contemplating God and God's most divine powers. This is what he clearly hints at when he says: "Finally, a punishment of this sort is that a sterile soul migrates into a certain body through judgment, in which there is neither the nature of male nor female." It is as if he were clearly saying: through this, the soul will never perform good works, nor will it ever receive anything of the good. Therefore, you must flee the sterility of the soul with all your might, and produce offspring (which may eventually make you blessed); and in this way, imitate the fecundity of the **Father**—who is always generating—with the fecundity of your own mind, as far as you are able.

### ¶ This is ¶ the Summation of Asclepius.

### ¶ The Sacred Discourse of Hermes. III.
<note>Mercurius is the Latin name for Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary sage to whom these "Hermetic" writings are attributed.</note>

The glory of all things is God. The divine is divine nature. The principle <note>or beginning</note> of the universe: God, Mind, Nature, Act, Necessity, End, and Renewal. For there was an **infinite shadow** in the abyss; water moreover and a thin intellectual spirit were present in **chaos** through divine power. Then a **holy splendor** blossomed, which led the elements forth from beneath the sand and the moist nature. And all the gods chose a seminal nature. When all things were yet indistinct, the light things then flew up into the high region; the heavy things settled beneath the moist sand. When things were distinguished and balanced, which were carried by a **fiery spirit**, the heaven shone out into **seven circles**. <note>These "seven circles" refer to the celestial spheres of the seven classical planets: the Moon, Mercury, Venus, the Sun, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> The gods were seen in the forms <note>or ideas</note> of the stars, along with their signs. The stars were numbered according to the gods who inhabit them. The wider circuit was carried in its course by an airy circle, with the divine spirit breathing together. Each of the gods, out of internal power, fulfilled the work assigned to him. Therefore, living things were born: quadrupeds, reptiles, aquatic creatures, as well as flying things. Likewise, every sowing sprouting through seeds—grass, herbs, and the buds of flowers—each contained the seed of regeneration within. The **generation of men** was for the knowledge of divine works and as a testimony to nature, to rule over all...

[[signature: b iij]]

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on the soul's spiritual "fertility" (virtue) before beginning the 'Sacred Discourse' of Hermes, which provides a cosmogony describing the creation of the universe from chaos into ordered planetary spheres.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius, creation myth, chaos, seven planetary circles, soul fertility, Gospel parallels, Renaissance Hermeticism</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
# MERCURIUS
<note>The heading "MER:" refers to Mercurius Trismegistus (Hermes Trismegistus), a legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the author of the *Hermetica*, texts that blend Greek and Egyptian philosophy.</note>

...those things which are covered by the sky, for the discernment of good things, for the increase of the race, and the propagation of number. And every soul, veiled by the **shadow of the flesh**, <note>original: "carnis umbraculo"</note> is meant to look up at the movement of the celestial gods, at the works of God, and the progress of nature—observing the signs of good things and seeking the knowledge of divine power. A certain portion has been granted for the judgment of good and evil, and for discovering the various arts of good things. Yet, the soul begins to live in these things and to obtain wisdom according to its share in the continuous course of the revolving gods. Furthermore, it shall be dissolved into that state in which great monuments and reminders of the arts will stand upon the earth for these chosen ones through the renewal of time. Herbs and the generations of living flesh—the seeds of fruits having been dissolved, <note>original: "tabefactis," meaning decayed or melted away</note> along with the craftsman of nature and art—will grow young again. Finally, as if by necessity and the renewal of the gods and the circular course of numerous nature, a divine thing arises; namely, the very **universal harmony** <note>original: "conspiratio mundi," literally a "breathing together" or cosmic sympathy</note> of the world, as nature flowers anew. For indeed, nature itself consists within divinity.

> The third source recalls again: that from the bosom of unformed matter—illuminated by forms as if by holy splendors—all things proceeded to their birth in their own order, and that the blessed minds of the heavens fulfill the work committed to them by the highest Father. And that man was born for a **dual duty**: namely, for the contemplation of eternal things, and for the judgment of good and evil, as well as the moderation of human affairs through both the various inventions of the arts and through prudence. For man, the sky, the earth, herbs, buds, the whole harmony of the world, and the most beautiful order of things suggest most abundantly the material for contemplation and for lifting himself up to divine things. For nature is like a **living book** <note>The transcription "liberynus" is likely a corruption of "liber vivus" (living book), a common metaphor where nature is seen as a text written by God.</note> full of divinity and a mirror of divine things. These words clearly explain paganism to the pagans and create a certain foundation for their errors, tending <note>original Greek: "προσ παντα σχεδον την ματαιοτητα" (*pros panta schedon tēn mataiotēta*)</note> **toward almost all vanity**. They lead toward every kind of divination, toward idolatry, the rites of the gods, initiations—or more truly <term>will-worship</term> <note>original: "thelo threscias," from the Greek *ethelothreskeia*, referring to self-imposed or arbitrary religious rites not commanded by God.</note>—magic, <term>theurgies</term>, <note>Rituals intended to invoke the presence of gods or spirits.</note> fate, creator-gods, and many such impure and wicked things. But for us, there is **one God**, who made heaven and earth and all things that are in them: and who changes and transfers all things into different times, just as He wills.

<summary>This page discusses the Hermetic belief that the soul's purpose is to contemplate God through nature's harmony, but concludes with a stern Christian critique, labeling such philosophies as a gateway to pagan idolatry and "will-worship."</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Soul, Universal Harmony, Idolatry, Theurgy, Nature as Mirror, Creation, Paganism vs Christianity</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Mercurius (Hermes Trismegistus) and his son/disciple Tatius. The previous page discussed the soul's duty to contemplate the divine and the "universal harmony" of the world. Here, the focus shifts to the necessity of transcending the physical realm to reach the "Monad" or ultimate Unity.</meta>

# I

They themselves are capable of no action and hinder others; in no other way do those people wander and stray in those processions because of the pleasures of the body. Since these things are so, O Tatius: divine duties ought to come first, and human ones should follow. God is surely beyond blame for the evils that come from us; for we prefer evil things to good ones. 

You see, O son, how we must transcend the celestial bodies, keep away from the choirs of <term>demons</term> <note>original: "demonum." In this context, these are not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense, but intermediary spirits or planetary forces that govern the material world.</note> and the circuits of the stars, and surpass their movements, so that we may strive toward the one and only God. For the Good is insuperable: infinite without end, never beginning in itself, though having a beginning in relation to human knowledge. Yet such knowledge is not the start of the Good itself, but provides for us a starting point for knowing it. Let us therefore embrace this beginning. For when this is known, we shall travel through all things most swiftly. 

But it is difficult for one who leaves behind familiar and present things to turn oneself toward the higher and more powerful things. For those things we see with our eyes delight us too much; things that are hidden produce distrust. Moreover, the things that are visible are certainly evil; the Good is hidden from those who dwell upon manifest things. For it has no form, nor any shape. For this reason, it is like only unto itself, and truly unlike everything else. 

For indeed, the incorporeal cannot appear to the body; such is the difference between the like and the unlike, and the inferiority of the unlike to that which is like. The <term>Monad</term> <note>original: "Monas," meaning "Unity" or "The One." In Hermetic philosophy, it represents the indivisible source of all existence.</note>—that is, Unity—is the <term>principle</term> <note>original: "principium," meaning the fundamental source or first cause.</note>, root, and origin of all things. Without a principle, indeed, there is nothing. A beginning, however, is not a beginning of the principle, but of something else. 

The Monad, therefore, is the principle; it contains every number and is contained by none; it begets every number and is begotten by no number. Whatever is begotten is surely imperfect, divisible, growing, and diminishing. But to that which is perfect, none of these things happen. Indeed, that which increases, increases by the power of the Monad. But it vanishes through its own weakness, when it can no longer contain the Monad.

<summary>Mercurius explains to Tatius that reaching the one God requires transcending material pleasures and celestial influences. He introduces the "Monad" as the perfect, indivisible source of all numbers and existence, contrasting its eternal stability with the decaying nature of physical things.</summary>

<keywords>Monad, Unity, Tatius, Divine vs Human Duty, Principle, Incorporeal, Transcendence, Number Theory, Hermeticism</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and his son Tat, focusing on the distinction between those who possess 'Mind' (Intellect) and those who live only by animal sensation.</meta>

-># HERMES<-

Yet they neglected the proclamation; though they indeed share in speech, they are devoid of Mind <note>original: *mentis*, referring to the higher divine Intellect or spiritual perception</note> and remain ignorant of why and by whom they were created. Furthermore, their senses are like those of irrational animals, entangled in desire; they do not admire those things worthy of contemplation, for they are enslaved to the lusts of the body, believing that man was born for these things alone. But those who are sustained by the nature of God, O Tat, when their works are compared, are considered immortal rather than mortal. Their intelligence encompasses all things on earth, in the sea, and whatever exists beyond the heavens. Thus, they raise themselves up so that they may behold the Good itself. Once they perceive this, they consider the life we lead here to be a kind of misery. Despising all bodily things, they are drawn toward the One alone.

This, O Tat, is how one earns knowledge of divine things; for the contemplation of God is possible because the divine mixing bowl <note>original: *crater*, a central Hermetic metaphor for a vessel filled with Mind/Intellect sent down by God for humans to dip into</note> exists.

**TAT.** Indeed, O father, I desire to be washed in the waters of this mixing bowl.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** <note>The original printer used 'K' instead of 'R' in the name Trismegistus here</note> Unless, O son, you first hate your body, you will not be able to love yourself. But as soon as you love not yourself but God, you will immediately attain the Mind; and having obtained this, you will straightway possess knowledge <note>original: *scientia*, often used here to mean *Gnosis* or spiritual insight</note>.

**TAT.** In what way do you assert these things, O father?

**TRISMEGISTUS.** It is impossible, O son, to attend to both at once—that is, to the mortal and the divine. For since there are only two things in the order of reality, the corporeal and the incorporeal—the one being mortal and the other divine—in choosing one, we lose the other. Whenever the care for one is diminished, the activity of the other is intensified. Therefore, the choice of the better path is honorable for the one who chooses; it not only makes the man who chose it divine <note>original: *deum reddidit*, literally 'rendered him a god'</note> but also demonstrates piety toward God to others. However, the choice of the worse path destroys the man himself; yet it offends nothing against God except this one thing: that such people are like empty processions passing through the middle of the streets <note>The author compares those living purely material lives to street performers or parades—visible and loud, but lacking any real direction or spiritual impact</note>.

<summary>Hermes explains to Tat that humans must choose between the material and the divine, arguing that true 'Mind' is found by looking beyond bodily desires toward the 'Crater' of God. This page emphasizes that spiritual knowledge requires a rejection of the physical self in favor of divine contemplation.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, The Crater, Divine Mind, Immortality, Gnosis, Dualism, Contemplation, Body and Soul</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a philosophical dialogue, likely from the Corpus Hermeticum (Libellus IV), between the sage Hermes Trismegistus and his son Tat (Tatius). It discusses the nature of the "Monad" or Divine Unity.</meta>

# I

[[page number: 13]]

They themselves are indeed incapable of any action, 
while hindering others. In no other way do those men wander and 
stray, following those empty processions <note>The author compares people obsessed with physical pleasures to mindless participants in a parade or street procession.</note>, 
for the sake of bodily pleasures. Since these things are so, 
O Tat <note>Original: "Tati," referring to Tat, the son and pupil of Hermes Trismegistus.</note>: divine duties ought to come first, and 
human affairs should follow. God is certainly free from the 
blame of the evils that come from us; we ourselves are the 
cause. For we prefer evil things over the good. You see, 
O son, how we must transcend the <term>celestial bodies</term> <note>The "celestial bodies" refer to the planets and stars, which in ancient philosophy were thought to influence human destiny.</note>, 
and stay away from the choirs of spirits <note>Original: "demonum." In this context, these are not "demons" in the modern sense, but intermediary spiritual beings.</note>, and move beyond 
the circuit of the stars and their movements, so that we 
may strive toward the one and only God. For He is an 
unsurpassable good: infinite and without end, 
who in Himself never has a beginning, yet to 
human <term>knowledge</term> <note>Original: "cognitione." This refers to "Gnosis" or spiritual insight rather than simple data.</note> 
He appears to have a beginning. However, such knowledge is 
not His own beginning; rather, it provides for us the 
beginning of Him being known. 
Therefore, let us embrace this beginning. For once this is 
known, we shall most swiftly traverse all things. 
But it is difficult for one who is leaving behind familiar 
and present things to turn themselves toward the higher 
and more powerful. For the things we see with our eyes 
delight us too much, while hidden things breed 
distrust. Visible things are indeed evils; the good 
is hidden to those who lean upon things that are 
manifest. For the Good has no form, nor any 
figure. For this reason, it is similar only to itself, 
but unlike all other things. 
Indeed, the <term>incorporeal</term> <note>Something that has no physical body or matter.</note> cannot appear to a body; 
such is the difference between the similar and the 
dissimilar, and the inferiority of the dissimilar 
to that which is similar. 
The <term>Monad</term> <note>From the Greek 'monas,' meaning 'unity' or 'oneness.' In this philosophy, it represents the source of all existence.</note>: that is, Unity, is the 
source, root, and origin of all. Without a source, 
nothing exists. 
The beginning, however, is not the beginning of the source, 
but of something else. The Monad, therefore, is the 
source; it contains every number and is contained by none; 
it generates every number and is generated by no number. 
Whatever is generated is imperfect, divisible, 
subject to growth, and subject to decay. 
But to that which is perfect, none of these things happen. 
Indeed, that which increases, increases by the 
power of the Monad. But it withers away through 
its own weakness, when it can no longer contain the Monad.

<summary>This page explores the Hermetic journey of the soul, arguing that one must transcend the physical stars and spirits to reach the "Monad" or Divine Unity. It defines the Monad as the perfect, indivisible source of all numbers and existence, contrasting it with the imperfect and decaying nature of the material world.</summary>

--- Page 41 ---
->MER.<-

V

These things for you, O Tatius, according to my strength: let the image of God be inscribed <note>original: "subscripta sint"; literally "written underneath," suggesting an internal etching of these truths upon the soul</note> [within you]. If you consider it diligently and recognize it with your inner eyes, believe me, my son, you will find the way up to the heights. The image itself will lead you peacefully; for it offers a certain vision and takes hold of those who burn with the desire to look upon it, and it draws them in the same way as the stone called a **magnet** <note>original: "magnes"; the ancients were fascinated by the magnet's "invisible" power to move physical objects, seeing it as a metaphor for divine attraction</note> draws **iron**.

The **Mixing Vessel** <note>original: "Crater"; a reference to the "Mixing Bowl" or "Font" sent down by God in Hermetic lore, filled with the "Mind" for humans to drink from</note> exhorts us to grasp wisdom; it is the wide-open mixing vessel of the divine mind. Any soul that immerses itself in it becomes a partaker of knowledge and looks up toward the Mind. But indeed, to lift itself to that Mind, the soul must transcend the heavens, cross the circuit of the stars, and leave the choirs of the blessed far below. Then it stretches toward the one and only God: the insurmountable and boundless infinite Good. When the soul gazes upon this, it looks down upon all bodies. It considers this life (which we sustain with light) to be misery, intent only on the desire for the incorporeal good. Yet no one is able to immerse themselves in the divine mixing vessel who has not hated their own body and who has not loved God himself instead of themselves. And those who, neglecting mortal things, have not clung to divine things alone; let them be present to the One alone, meditate on the One alone, and burn for the One. Soon the analogy of unity and numbers raises us to contemplate the true <term>Monad</term> <note>The Monad refers to "the One" or the primary unit from which all numbers and the entire universe originate in Pythagorean and Hermetic philosophy</note> and the true numbers of nature. This is the teaching of the Mixing Vessel.

### ¶ Hermes to Tatius his son: That God is both hidden and manifest at once.

<margin>h</margin>
I will conduct this further discourse with you, O Tatius, so that the most excellent name may not be missing from you, and so that you may not be ignorant of that which seems hidden to many people, yet is actually clear. For if it were not manifest anywhere, it would be nothing at all. Whatever offers itself to sight is "generated" <note>original: "genitum"; referring to things created, born, or brought into existence, which are subject to change</note>. But that which is hidden is eternal. For it does not need to appear, since it never ceases to be. Indeed, it places other things before our eyes, but remains secret itself, as one that enjoys eternal life. For <term>imagination</term> <note>original: "phantasia"; in this context, it refers to the faculty of perceiving sensory appearances, which are considered less "real" than the hidden divine essence</note> deals only with things that are generated. In imagination, nothing exists besides the process of becoming. But that unique "unbegotten" thing is incomprehensible to imagination. Since all things become clear through Him...

<summary>This page discusses the Hermetic concept of the 'Crater' or Mixing Vessel of the Divine Mind, explaining how the soul must transcend the physical world to reach God. It also introduces a discourse on how God is simultaneously hidden from the senses yet visible through the existence of the created world.</summary>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the sage Hermes Trismegistus and his son Tat, focusing on the invisibility of God and how He is revealed through the order of the cosmos.</meta>

[[language: Latin]]
[[page number: 14]]
[[header: I]]

# I

He shines again in all things and through all things. He appears especially to those to whom he has willed to communicate knowledge. Therefore, O my son Tat, first of all, beseech the Lord and Father—the Unique One, the One, and the One from whom the One comes—with pious prayers, so that you may be worthy of his mercy. Only then will you be able to perceive so great a God: if even a single ray of his intelligence shines graciously upon your understanding. For understanding <term>Intellection: the faculty of the mind that perceives spiritual truths beyond the physical senses</term> alone, being hidden, perceives that which is hidden. And so, if you look with the eyes of the mind, O Tat, he will be revealed to you—believe me. God, truly free from all envy <note>The "envy of God" was a common theme in ancient philosophy; here Hermes asserts that God does not hide knowledge out of spite, but offers it freely to the seeking mind.</note>, shines everywhere through every tiny part of the world, and he offers himself to be known so clearly that it is possible not only to understand him, but even (if I may put it this way) to touch him with your very hands. For his image moves before our eyes on every side; it presents and impresses itself upon us. But if he remains hidden within you, how will you find either yourself or him? Finally, when you wish to see God, look up at the order of the stars and the rest of the heavens. Tell me, who preserves their perpetual order? Indeed, all order of number is bounded by the limits of space. The Sun is the most excellent of the celestial gods. The other heavenly beings obey the Sun as their prince and king. The Sun, so great—larger than both land and sea together—nevertheless allows the countless smaller stars to revolve above him. Whom does he fear? Whom does he revere? O son, the motions of the various stars are distinct. Who assigns the measure of movement to each one?

**TAT:** Consider Arcturus <note>original: "Arcturum" - While today a specific star, in this context it refers to the circumpolar constellations (like the Great Bear) that appear to "pull" the heavens in their rotation.</note>, forever turning about the same point and dragging the whole mechanism of the world <note>original: "machina mundi" — the "World Machine," a common Renaissance metaphor for the clockwork-like precision of the universe.</note>.

**TRISMEGISTUS:** Who uses this instrument? Who has marked out the sea within its boundaries? Who holds the weight of the earth steady and balances it in the center? Surely there is someone, O Tat, who is the author and master of these things. For it is impossible to preserve place, number, or measure without the power of an author. Order cannot be created from deformity <note>original: "deformitas" - here meaning chaos or a lack of form.</note>; rather, deformity lacks a master who may provide order.

**Vocabulary:** Tatius, Sun, Arcturus, World-Machine, Trismegistus, Intellection, Order, Deformity

<summary>Hermes Trismegistus explains to his son Tat that although God is invisible, His presence is proven by the perfect order of the stars, the sun, and the physical laws governing the 'World Machine.'</summary>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page appears to be from a Renaissance-era Latin translation of the *Corpus Hermeticum*, specifically a dialogue where Hermes Trismegistus (referred to here as "Mer." for Mercury) instructs his son, Tat, on the nature of God through the evidence of design in the universe and the human body.</meta>

# MERCURY

If only the ability were granted to you, my son, that you might fly into the high regions of the air with the help of wings; and having attained the middle region between heaven and earth, you could behold the solidity of the earth, the spreading of the sea, the flow of the rivers, the vastness of the air, and the swift, consuming force of fire. O what a most happy spectacle, my son! O what a most blessed vision! For in a single movement of your eyes, you would comprehend the entire order of the world; you would see the motionless Creator <note>Latin: *factorem*. The "immobile maker" is a common philosophical concept for God as the Unmoved Mover.</note> in action, and see the hidden one clearly revealed.

But if you also wish to investigate God the Craftsman <note>original: *deum artificem*</note> through those fragile things supported by the earth or hidden in the depths of the water, then come, my son, and consider the workmanship <term>opificium: the act of making or the craftsmanship of a thing</term> of the human body. Through this observation, learn for yourself who the founder of such a beautiful image is. Who is the painter of the eyes? Who fashioned the nostrils and the ears? Who shaped the lips of the mouth? Who stretched and bound the sinews <note>Latin: *nervos*. In early anatomy, this term often referred to both nerves and tendons.</note>? Who watered the veins with blood? Who gathered the solid bones? Who wove the flesh around them with a thin skin? Who distinguished the fingers and the joints? Who extended the foundations of the feet? Who pierced the pores and opened the passages of the body? Who compressed the spleen into its shape? Who impressed the pyramid-like form <note>A common Renaissance anatomical description of the heart's apex.</note> upon the heart? Who covered the fibers of the liver? Who carved the tubes of the lungs? Who gave the belly its capacious size? Who shaped the honorable members of the body in plain view, and who placed the private <note>Latin: *obscena*. Literally "hidden" or "shameful," referring to the reproductive organs in a historical context of modesty.</note> parts in darkness, intending for them to lie secret from the sight of onlookers?

See how many works of divine art they demonstrate in a single material! Each part is beautiful and correctly measured, and each differs in its own specific function. Who fashioned each of these? What mother? What father? Was it not the invisible God alone, who worked all these things by His own will? And since no one would dare to assert that a statue or a painting could be made without a craftsman or a painter, shall we think that the wonderful constitution of this world was established without a Creator?

<gloss>Ability, of wings, with the aid, you would comprehend, workmanship/craftsmanship, founder/creator, all things, own/proper</gloss>

<summary>In this passage, Hermes Trismegistus argues for the existence of an invisible God by pointing to the intricate design of the cosmos and the complex anatomy of the human body as proof of a divine 'Craftsman.' He uses the analogy that just as a statue requires a sculptor, the biological complexity of humans requires a Creator.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, teleological argument, anatomy, divine craftsmanship, microcosm, Renaissance philosophy, Corpus Hermeticum</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue of Mercury (Hermes Trismegistus), shifting from the wonders of creation to a philosophical discourse on the nature of "The Good." Mercury is now addressing Esculapius (Asclepius) rather than Tat.</meta>

### MER

Nothing escapes his yoke, whereby he might be worthily despised and grow angry. Nor is anything wiser than he, such that it might strike rivalry in him. Therefore, since these things do not happen to him, nothing of his nature resists except the Good itself. Indeed, just as none of those things that are evil are in this essence, so the Good is found in nothing else. For in individuals, the rest are individual: both in small things and in great, and even in those which are according to the One, and in that very greatest and most powerful animal of all <note>Mercury refers to the Universe/Cosmos itself as a living, breathing "animal."</note>, which creation certainly fills with passions <term>passio: in this philosophical sense, it refers to "being acted upon" or undergoing change, rather than just emotion</term>. For generation <note>The process of being born or coming into existence.</note> is a certain passion. Where there is passion, there is in no way the Good. Where the Good is, no passion is placed. Where it is day, it is in no way night; where it is night, it is not even day. Therefore, the Good cannot exist in the process of generation. It remains, therefore, in that which is unbegotten. However, just as the participation of all things is granted to matter, so also by the participation of the Good, the world is good. I say "good" insofar as it also effects all things; in which part indeed the world is good; in all other things it is not good. For it is subject to passion and is mobile, and moreover, is the cause of all passions. In man, moreover, the good is defined by comparison to evil. Indeed, that which is not too evil, or even less evil, we call "good" in men. Thus it happens that our "good" is nothing other than the smallest portion of evil. Hence it also follows that a good of this kind cannot be separated from evil. For this good is stained by the admixture of evils; being tainted, it does not remain good any further. If it does not remain, it becomes evil. Therefore, the Good itself exists in God alone. Therefore, only the name of good is present to man, O Esculapius; the nature of the Good is not at all. For the material body cannot contain it, being constructed on all sides and oppressed by depravity, labors, pains, desires, wrath, deceptions, foolish opinions, and trifles. However, I judge the worst of all, O Esculapius, to be that each one of these things I have mentioned is believed to be the "highest good." In the first place, evil must be fled. Gluttony <note>original: *ventris luxus*, literally "the luxury of the belly."</note> is the fuel for all evils; here

<summary>Mercury explains that "The Good" exists only in God and the unbegotten realm, while the material world and humanity only experience "good" as a relative absence of evil or through participation in God's nature.</summary>
<keywords>The Good, God, Esculapius, Passions, Generation, Matter, Evil, Gluttony, Hermeticism</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page concludes a discourse from Hermes (Mercurius) and begins Book VI of the Hermetic corpus, titled "That the Good is in God Alone." It transitions from a mystical hymn of praise to a philosophical investigation of the nature of "The Good."</meta>

# MERCURIUS

...how shall I become anything else? For you are truly whatever I am. You are whatever I do. You are whatever I say. For you are all things, and there is nothing besides you. What is there that you are not? You are everything that has been born. You are the understanding Father; the creator God <note>original: "fabricans deus"</note>; the bringer of good; the one who makes all things good. For the purest air is the substance of matter; the soul is the substance of air; the mind is the substance of soul; and finally, God is the essence of the mind.

<margin>The fifth book teaches that God is both hidden and simultaneously shines through every individual particle of the world. All things (which present themselves to us) are His images. Thus, the wonderful and beautiful order of things clearly declares the Ordainer; and individual things, circumscribed by their certain limits, declare their Lord and author. The sun, the king and prince of the stars, obeys and serves Him; he fears Him and holds Him in awe. In short, the entire structure of the world declares its craftsman. Soon, he offers a more confident judgment concerning the divine internal generation: that it is the essence of God to conceive and make all things. So much so, that it would be impossible for God to exist unless He were always acting upon all things. For the divine <term>Logos|The "Word" or "Reason," representing the divine intellect that organizes the universe</term> is all things in such a way that it is nothing among them all. In this, the corporeal and the incorporeal, being and nothingness, all breathe together and coincide into the same. Then Mercurius, in songs of divine praise, finishes and concludes the dialogue.</margin>

### ¶ That the Good is in God alone, and nowhere else; to Aesculapius. VI

<margin>b</margin> This one thing, O Aesculapius <note>Aesculapius is the Latin name for Asclepius, the Greek god of medicine, who appears here as a student of Hermes.</note>, is placed in nothing except in the one God; or rather, God Himself is the "Good" eternally. For this reason, God must be the <term>essence|The fundamental nature or "soul" of a thing that makes it what it is.</term> of all motion and knowledge. Nothing is deprived of this essence; it possesses within itself a stable power <note>original: "actum," referring to actualized power or activity</note> that lacks nothing, an infinite power extending in great abundance. This is the one principle of the universe: extending the good and pouring forth the good. Whenever I say "The Good," understand that which is always good and which is all good things. This belongs to God alone. For He does not lack anything, such that by desiring to obtain it He would become evil; nor is there ever any loss for Him, so that He might be affected by the pain of damage. For He is greater than any portion of evil. Nor is there anything stronger than Him by which He might be conquered. No injury can fall upon Him, by which He might be provoked or harassed into a rage.

<vocabulary>Mercurius, Esculapius, essence, logos, the good, world structure, king of the stars, whatever I am</vocabulary>

<summary>This page concludes a hymn identifying God as the source and substance of all existence before transitioning to a discourse to Aesculapius regarding the nature of "The Good." It argues that true Goodness is an exclusive attribute of God because only the Divine is self-sufficient and immune to the lack or desire that breeds evil.</summary>

<keywords>Hermeticism, Asclepius, Monotheism, Divine Essence, Logos, Neoplatonism, Nature of Evil, Creator God</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse of Hermes Trismegistus (indicated by the header MER) to his disciple Asclepius. The previous section likely established the absolute nature of God as the only source of the Good.</meta>

# 16
### MER

Nothing escapes his yoke <note>referring to God's sovereignty or the natural law of the Good</note>; therefore, he is not treated with contempt so as to become indignant or angry. Nor is there anything wiser than him that might provoke him to envy. Therefore, since these things do not happen to him, nothing remains in his nature except the Good itself. Just as nothing that is evil is present in this essence, so also the Good is found in nothing else. 

For in individual things, other individual qualities exist—both in the small and the large, and even in those things that are considered as a single unit—and even in that greatest and most powerful animal of all <note>The "Greatest Animal" refers to the Cosmos or the World-Soul, viewed as a living, breathing entity in Hermetic philosophy</note>, which is filled with things born of suffering. For <term>generation: the process of being born, created, or coming into existence</term> is a kind of <term>suffering: "passio" in Latin, meaning to be acted upon or changed by an external force</term>. Where there is suffering, there is in no way the Good. Where the Good is, no suffering is ever placed. Where it is day, it is in no way night; where it is night, it is not even day. 

Therefore, the Good cannot exist in the process of generation. It remains, then, only in that which is unbegotten <note>meaning that which is eternal and was never "born," i.e., God</note>. 

Just as matter is allowed to participate in all things, so also through participation in the Good, the world is good. I say "good" insofar as it also brings all things to effect, and in that respect, the world is good; in all other respects, it is not good. For it is subject to suffering and change, and is, moreover, the cause of all sufferings. 

Furthermore, in man, "good" is only so by comparison to evil. Indeed, that which is not too evil, or is even just "less evil," we call "good" among men. Thus it happens that our "good" is nothing other than the smallest portion of evil. From this it follows that a good of this kind cannot be separated from evil. For this "good" is defiled by the mixing-in of evils. Once tainted, it no longer remains good; and if it does not remain, it becomes evil. 

Therefore, the Good itself exists in **God** alone. Wherefore, O **Asclepius**, man possesses only the *name* of "good," but by no means the *nature* of the Good. For the material body cannot contain it—a body worn out on all sides and oppressed by wickedness, labors, pains, desires, anger, deceptions, foolish opinions, and trifles. Yet I judge this to be the worst of all, **Asclepius**: that each of those things I have mentioned <note>the vices and pains of the body</note> is believed by men to be the "Highest Good." 

Above all, evil must be fled. The excess of lust <note>original: "veneris luxus," literally "the luxury of Venus"</note> is the tinder of all evils. Here...

<summary>Hermes explains to Asclepius that the absolute "Good" exists only in God, because the physical world and human life are defined by "passion" (change and suffering), which is incompatible with the Good.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius, The Good, Evil, Passion, Generation, Materialism, Cosmology, Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues a philosophical dialogue between the sage Hermes Trismegistus and his disciple Asclepius regarding the nature of divinity and the material world. It follows a discussion on how human perception often fails to grasp the true nature of 'the Good.'</meta>

->### MERCURIUS [TRISMEGISTUS] ###<-
->**Page 17**<-

...error: hence the deprivation of the good. Indeed, I give great thanks to God: who, while I was thinking about the nature of the good, infused this certain conviction into me—that the good cannot exist in the world. For indeed the world is a heap of evils. But God is either the source of the good or the Good itself: the exuberant fullness of God. Certainly, there is an excellence of "goods" <note>original: "bonorum"; here referring to divine qualities</note> concerning an essence that shines sincerely and most purely. Nor perhaps are these merely attributes <term>essentiae; the fundamental "being" or "is-ness" of a thing</term> of God. I assert this must be heard, O Asclepius: that the essence of God (if God has any essence at all) is the Good itself. 

Truly, beauty and the good are not to be found in the world, nor in any parts of the world. For whatever things move the senses are merely idols <note>original: "idola"; in this context, false images or fleeting shadows of reality</note> and certain vain wonders. But those things which escape the organs of the senses belong to the beautiful and the good. Just as the sharpness of the eye does not discern God, so it does not recognize beauty and the good. For these are parts of God: belonging to that most whole being, kindred, inseparable, and especially beloved. And as God himself loves these, so God himself is loved by them. 

If you are able to perceive God, you will also perceive beauty and the good, shining through all things and illuminated by God. For beauty is the good without comparison and without imitation. For God admits no comparison or imitation. Just as you would know God as beautiful and good, so indeed it should never be communicated to other living beings <note>This implies that this "secret" knowledge is reserved for those capable of spiritual understanding</note> who cannot be compared with God. 

If you seek God, you seek beauty itself. There is but one way that finally leads to it: piety joined to knowledge <term>gnosis; experiential spiritual knowledge rather than just book-learning</term>. Wherefore ignorant mortals, straying from the path of piety, do not fear to call a human being "good" as well; yet they can have no true knowledge of the good. Instead, they are entangled and ensnared by evils, and they even deem evil to be good. They use these evils incurably, and they fear their loss and deprivation. Finally, they strive with every machination and strength not only so they are not pressed by a single evil, but truly so that the number and magnitude of their evils might be increased. Such things, O Asclepius, are the "ornaments" of humans...

**Vocabulary:**
**Asclepius:** <gloss>The student of Hermes, named after the Greek god of healing.</gloss>
**Essence:** <gloss>The fundamental nature or "being" of God.</gloss>
**The World:** <gloss>The material realm, viewed here as a collection of evils compared to the divine.</gloss>
**Beauty:** <gloss>A divine attribute that cannot be copied or compared to worldly things.</gloss>
**Piety:** <gloss>Religious devotion or reverence.</gloss>
**Knowledge:** <gloss>Spiritual understanding or "Gnosis."</gloss>
**Evil:** <gloss>The absence or deprivation of the Good.</gloss>
**The Good:** <gloss>The supreme divine reality, synonymous with God's essence.</gloss>

<summary>In this passage, Hermes Trismegistus argues that "the Good" and "the Beautiful" are exclusive to the divine essence and cannot be found in the material world, which he describes as a collection of evils. He teaches Asclepius that true spiritual vision requires "piety joined to knowledge" to look past worldly idols and perceive God.</summary>

<keywords>Hermeticism, Asclepius, the Good, divine essence, material world vs spiritual realm, Gnosis, piety, spiritual vision</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding the nature of material things and begins the seventh discourse of the Corpus Hermeticum, a collection of Greco-Egyptian wisdom texts. The text transitions from a summary of the sixth book to a direct exhortation against spiritual ignorance.</meta>

I
[[page number: 17]]

...lesser and good things, which we can neither flee from nor hate. For this is the most difficult task of all: chiefly because it is necessary for us to use and feed upon them, and if we were deprived of them, we could in no way live our lives.

<margin>The sixth discourse <note>original: "Sextus." This refers to the sixth book of the *Pimander* or *Corpus Hermeticum*</note> contends that the Good and the nature of Good (that is, goodness itself) is found in God alone; and likewise the nature of the beautiful, the powerful, and the wise. In the world, however, there is only the *name* of "good," and the text argues that men are blinded by error. It warns us to abhor vices and teaches the way that leads us to goodness and beauty itself—namely, piety joined with knowledge <note>The text refers to *Gnosis*, or spiritual insight</note>. Ignorant mortals, straying from the path of piety, can never reach this. These are the words of the sixth discourse.</margin>

### VII. That the greatest evil for humans is to be ignorant of God.

<margin>q</margin> 
**W**here are you rushing, you drunken mortals? You who have swallowed down the unmixed wine <note>original: "merum," referring to pure, undiluted wine</note> of ignorance; since you cannot bear its weight, vomit it out! Live soberly; look with the eyes of your mind <note>The "eyes of the mind" refers to the *Intellect* or *Nous*, the faculty used to perceive spiritual truths</note>. If you cannot all do this, at least let those who can, do so. The plague of ignorance subverts the whole earth and corrupts the soul while it is imprisoned in the bonds of the body, nor does it allow her to take the path of salvation. Do not allow yourselves to be submerged in the lake of corruption and death. 

Breathe now, breathe! Return to the fountain of life, and take hold of him who will lead you into the sanctuary <note>original: "adytum," the most sacred, innermost chamber of a temple</note> of truth. There is the shining light, unmixed with any darkness. There, no one raves with drunkenness, but all are sober and watchful; and with the eyes of the mind they gaze more keenly upon him who wishes to be seen. He is neither perceived by ears, nor seen by eyes, nor expressed by speech; the mind alone beholds him, the mind alone proclaims him. 

But first you must strip off the garment you wear: the robe of ignorance, the foundation of wickedness, the bond of corruption, the dark veil, the living death, the sentient corpse, the walking tomb, and finally, the thief in your own house. He who hates while he flatters, and envies while he hates—such is the enemy shadow with which you are covered. It drags you down toward itself, lest perhaps you...

<meta>This page continues the Hermetic dialogues (attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, or Mercurius) regarding the nature of the soul and the cosmos. It concludes the themes of the seventh book regarding ignorance and begins the eighth book on the immortality of all things.</meta>

...beholding the beauty of truth and the highest good, you may never perceive the foulness of this scent nor these snares which are constantly plotted against you. This [corporeal nature] dulls and blunts the edge of the internal senses; it chokes them with thick matter and intoxicates them with abominable and loathsome pleasure, so that you may never hear nor clearly see those things which by right ought to be heard and, above all, to be looked upon.

<margin>The seventh [book] complains of the plague of ignorance, which overturns the whole earth and corrupts the soul when it is enclosed in the chains of the body. It exhorts the ignorant to return to their senses and commands us to strip off the mortal garment—the body—by which we are dragged headlong into ignorance and death. It calls us to return to the entrance of the mind, which alone beholds Him who is neither perceived by ears, nor seen by eyes, nor expressed in speech. Thus ends the seventh.</margin>

### ¶ Nothing that exists perishes; rather, they are changes, though deceived men call them destruction. To Tat. VIII

<margin>O son, we must speak concerning the soul and the body: in what way the soul is immortal, and how great its power of action is during the joining together and the breaking apart of the body.</margin>

Death <note>original: *Thanatos*</note> pertains to none of these things. For the term "Death" is a concept of mortal naming; it is either a hollow word or it is formed by the removal of the first letter: "death" <note>original: *thanatos*</note> instead of that which is called "immortal" <note>original: *athanatos*. The author suggests that 'death' is linguistically just 'immortality' with the alpha-privative (the 'not') removed.</note>. For death signifies perishing, but nothing of those things which are in the world perishes. If the world is a <term>second god</term> <note>In Hermetic philosophy, the universe/world is seen as a living, divine being created in the image of the first God.</note> and likewise an immortal living being, it is impossible for any part of an immortal living being to perish. All things that are in the world are members of the world, especially man, the rational animal. The first of all is the truly eternal, immortal, and unbegotten God: the author of all. The second is thereafter, made according to His image: the world, begotten by Him, and by the same preserved, nourished, and endowed with immortality, as if by its own father; it is indeed always living and immortal.

<summary>This page marks the transition into the eighth book of the Pimander, arguing that "death" is a linguistic misunderstanding and that in a divine, immortal universe, nothing truly perishes, but only changes form.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, immortality, Second God, etymology of death, soul and body, Macrocosm, rational animal</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a philosophical discussion regarding the nature of the Father (God), the World (the Second God), and the creation of matter and humanity. It explores the distinctions between eternal existence and immortality granted by a creator.</meta>

# I 18

For He is indeed always living, and always immortal. Indeed, "always living" and "everlasting" <note>original: *sempiternum*</note> differ from one another in this way: the "everlasting" is that which was not made by another. If, however, it was made by itself, it was not made by anyone else, because it exists always. For the "everlasting" is everything insofar as it is everlasting, but that Father is everlasting of Himself.

But truly, the world was made always-living and immortal by the Father. And as much matter as had been subject to the Father, the Father himself—acting upon the corporeal and gathering it into a mass—rendered it all spherical, impressing a quality upon the existing matter that is immortal and possesses an eternal nature.

Full of all ideas, the Father inserted these qualities into the sphere; as if in a circuit, He enclosed it with every quality. He wished to adorn that which exists after Him, supporting the whole body as if with immortality, lest matter—wishing to pull away from this union—should dissolve back into its own deformity. 

For when matter was incorporeal <note>In this context, "incorporeal" refers to matter in a chaotic, unformed state before it was given shape.</note>, O son, it was more deformed. This matter also possesses certain meager qualities, being subject to a revolving nature of both growth and decay, which men call **death**.

This kind of confusion <note>original: *confusio*; here meaning a mixing or lack of stable order</note> exists concerning earthly living things. For the bodies of the heavenly beings preserve one and the same order which they received from their Father at the beginning. This order is preserved by the indissoluble restoration of each part. 

The restoration of the constitution of earthly bodies, however—that very dissolution—restores them into bodies that are indissoluble and immortal. And so, a deprivation of the senses occurs <note>The author explains that "death" is merely the loss of sensory perception, not the destruction of the underlying substance.</note>, not the destruction of the bodies themselves. 

The third living being is **Man**, created in the image of the world. Existing according to the will of the Father, Man—above all other earthly living things—possesses not only a kinship with the <term>Second God</term> <note>The "Second God" is a common Hermetic term for the Cosmos or the physical Universe.</note> but also an intelligence of the First. Sensory perception clearly comprehends the Second God, inasmuch as He is corporeal. The First...

Everlasting (*Sempiternum*), The World (*Mundus*), Matter (*Materia*), Spherical (*Spherica*), Quality (*Qualitas*), Deformity (*Deformitas*), Death (*Mors*), Restoration (*Restitutio*), Dissolution (*Dissolutio*), Man (*Homo*), Second God (*Secundus deus*), Intelligence (*Intelligentia*).

<meta>This page continues a dialogue between the sage Hermes Trismegistus and his son Tat, transitioning into a new discourse addressed to Asclepius. The text is part of the "Corpus Hermeticum," a collection of Greco-Egyptian wisdom texts that were central to Renaissance philosophy.</meta>

...the world truly rises toward God as an incorporeal and good <term>mind</term><note>Latin: *mens*. In this context, it refers to the highest form of intellect or divine consciousness.</note> by means of the mind. **TAT.** Is that <term>living being</term><note>Latin: *animal*. In the Latin of this period, *animal* refers to any "animated" or living entity, including humans and celestial bodies, not just beasts.</note> therefore not destroyed? **TRISMEGISTUS.** Better, better to speak with good omens, O son! Instead, meditate on what God is, what the world is, what an immortal living being is, and what a <term>dissolvable</term><note>Latin: *dissolubile*. This refers to things that can be broken down into their component parts (decay) rather than being utterly annihilated.</note> living being is. Know also that the world exists from God and within God, while man exists from the world and within the world. However, God is the principle, the comprehension, and the constitution of all things.

<gloss>The eighth [treatise]</gloss> teaches primarily that the celestial bodies continually preserve one and the same course which they first received from their father. It teaches that death and destruction first appear among earthly living bodies; but the name of "death" and the "destruction" of bodies is almost meaningless. For the bodies themselves are changed, they do not perish. Instead, immortal <term>matter</term><note>Latin: *materia*. The underlying substance of the universe which remains even when forms change.</note> transforms those very qualities, the <term>images and likenesses of the Ideas</term><note>Latin: *idearum idola simulachraque*. A reference to the Platonic theory that physical things are just shadows or copies of perfect, eternal "Ideas."</note> with which God wished to adorn it. And man is an immortal living being but dissolvable, the bond and the knot of all nature; partly corporeal and partly incorporeal, and capable of a higher mind. He was born in the likeness of the world so that he might understand the <term>sensible world</term><note>The world that can be perceived through the physical senses.</note> through a sensible body, and might rise toward the highest Mind through happy contemplation with his own mind. Thus ends the eighth.

### ¶ That the beautiful and the good exist in God alone, and nowhere else. To Asclepius. VIII.

<margin>Sense</margin>
<gloss>H</gloss> Yesterday, O Asclepius, I delivered a perfect discourse; but for the present, I judge it necessary that we briefly discuss **sensation**. Sensation and motion seem to differ in this most of all: the latter exists according to matter, while the former exists according to essence. To me, however, both seem to coincide, and in humans, they are not distinguished by reason. In <margin>Intelligence</margin> inner living beings, sensation is now unified; but in men, it is **intelligence**. Understanding indeed differs from intelligence, just as God differs from divinity. For divinity proceeds from God, and intelligence proceeds from man. This intelligence is the sister of <term>speech</term><note>Latin: *sermo*. This translates the Greek *Logos*, meaning both the spoken word and the underlying rational principle of the universe.</note>; or rather, both are instruments for one another. For neither is speech pronounced without understanding, nor does understanding come to light without speech. Therefore, sensation...

# I

...and intelligence in man: they work together as if connected. For we are in no way able to understand without the senses, nor to perceive without the mind’s understanding. It is, however, possible to understand intellection <term>Intellection: the act of the mind grasping a truth or concept</term> without the use of the senses, much like those who see phantasms <note>original: "phantasmata," or mental images</note> in their dreams. It appears that both operations occur in the visions of dreams, but the sense is only truly roused from sleep into wakefulness. 

Moreover, the body and the man are united in the soul; whenever both sensory parts agree with one another, then the understanding conceived by the mind is set in order. The Mind conceives all notions <term>Notions: "notiones," here meaning fundamental ideas or concepts</term>—good ones, certainly, whenever the seed is infused by God; but the opposite kind are conceived when seeds are cast by certain demons. 

<margin>Mind</margin>
<margin>Demon</margin>
Indeed, no part of the world is devoid of the presence of demons <note>In this philosophical context, "demons" refers to "daimones," or intermediary spirits that influence human thoughts, rather than purely modern concepts of devils.</note>; their light descends entirely from God himself. Finally, a demon, having been poured into a man, scatters the seeds of its own specific notion. Then the Mind, sown with these seeds and made "pregnant," gives birth to adulteries, debaucheries, homicides, parricides, sacrileges, contempt for the divine, murders, the overturning of cities, plagues upon men, and all other such works of evil demons. 

Clearly, the seeds of God are few, but they are great, beautiful, and good: namely virtue, temperance, and piety. Piety, moreover, is the knowledge <term>Knowledge: "cognitio," often implying a deep, experiential spiritual realization</term> of God. He who recognizes God, being filled with all good things, attains divine notions—notions, I say, which are not at all like those of the many. 

For this reason, if any dedicate themselves to this knowledge, they do not please the common people, nor do the common people please them; they are even thought to be insane and are mocked. Sometimes they are even hated, insulted, and deprived of life. For we have said that wickedness <note>original: "Improbitatem"</note> dwells here, and the earth is its province. I say the "earth," not the whole world <note>original: "mundum," the cosmos</note>, as some impious people wrongly claim. Nevertheless, the man devoted to God, as soon as he has tasted the divine vision, consigns all other things to oblivion; and even those things which other men consider evil...

<meta>This page continues a philosophical dialogue, likely from the Hermetic corpus, regarding the nature of the human mind and its connection to the divine and the material world. It follows a discussion on how the wise man handles good and evil.</meta>

->MER<-
<note>Short for Mercurius, the Latin name for Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary sage to whom these texts are attributed.</note>

...these things are: to this man, good things happen because he consults with **prudence** <note>Practical wisdom or "phronesis"</note> and refers every detail back to **knowledge**. What is truly marvelous is how he completely transforms evil things into good. But let us now return to the discourse on **sense** <note>Perception or "sensus"</note>. It is uniquely human to join sense with **intellect** <note>Higher understanding or "intellectio"</note>. For all people (as I mentioned above) are guided by intellect. However, one is guided according to **matter**, while another according to **essence**. For he who is a servant of depravity is **material**; he has received the seed of understanding from **daemons** <note>original: "demonibus"; in this philosophical context, these are intermediary spirits that influence the physical world, not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense</note> (as we have said). But if there are those who possess goodness of soul, God himself makes use of their nature. For God is the author of all things: by effecting all things, He makes them like unto Himself. Yet some good things, once generated, are sterile in their practical use. Indeed, the **revolution of the world** <note>The rotating motion of the heavens or the cosmos</note>, by agitating the generations, produces certain qualities; it infects and settles some with evil, while defending and purging others with good.

<margin>The World.</margin>

The world, O **Asclepius** <note>The legendary student of Hermes and a figure associated with healing</note>, possesses sense and motion, not in a way similar to human sense and motion, but certainly more powerful and far simpler. For the sense and intelligence of the world is one single thing: namely, to create and dissolve all things. The world is the **instrument of the Divine Will**, constructed primarily for this reason: that, receiving all **seeds** <note>The "logoi spermatikoi" or rational seeds of creation</note> from God and hiding them within its vast bosom, it may bring forth all things by composing them, and again take them all away by dividing them. And like a skilled farmer, it prunes whatever has grown too old, so that at the fixed intervals of time, it may flourish again. Nor is there anything to which the world itself does not grant life; it is simultaneously the place of life and its creator. Indeed, bodies consist of matter in various distinctions: some of these are of earth, some of water, others of air, and many also of fire; all are certainly composite. However, some are more densely packed, while others are simpler. The former are heavy, the latter are light. Moreover, the speed of their movement, through its variety, introduces the generation of quality. For the frequent **breathing** <note>original: "spiratio"; also referring to "pneuma" or spirit</note> existing within the bodies...

<summary>This page discusses the distinction between material and essential intellect, describing how the world (Mundus) acts as a living instrument of God's will to generate, prune, and recycle life through the four elements.</summary>

...also provides together with an abundance of life. God, therefore, is the father of the Cosmos <note>original: "Cosmi", from the Greek "kosmos," meaning both the world and an orderly arrangement</note>, that is, the world. The Cosmos is the father of those things within the Cosmos: and the Cosmos is indeed the son of God. Those things that are in the Cosmos consist under the dominion of the Cosmos; and it is rightly called "Cosmos." For it "cosmifies" <note>original: "cosmei", meaning to arrange, order, or adorn</note> all things through the variety of their generation, as well as through the unceasing operation of life, the perpetual necessity, the speed of the elements, the mixing, and the order of things begotten. Therefore, the Cosmos itself—that is, the "adornment"—is so named by necessity as well as by merit: the sense and understanding of all living things flow into them from the outside, coming from that which contains the inspired things. The Cosmos, however, perpetually preserves whatever it received from God at its very origin. God, certainly—contrary to what it seems to some—is not deprived of sense and mind <note>original: "sensu menteque captus est", literally "captured/taken in sense and mind", meaning senseless or insane</note>. For those oppressed by misery speak wrongly. Whatever things exist, O Asclepius <note>A legendary physician and student of Hermes Trismegistus in this philosophical tradition</note>, are in God and depend on God: some acting through the body, some moving through the essence of the soul <note>original: "essentiam animale"</note>, others giving life through the spirit, and others indeed being the receptacles of the dead. We shall say more correctly that God does not "have" such things: but so that we may express the clear truth, we shall confess that He *is* all things. Nor does He receive those things from the outside; nevertheless, He reaches out to the outermost things. That is the sense and understanding of God. Whence there shall never be a time in which anything that exists vanishes into nothingness. Whenever I say "existent things," I am speaking of God’s treasure. For God Himself embraces all existent things. Outside of Him there is nothing. Outside of nothingness is He Himself. These things, O Asclepius, will seem true to you who understand, but to the ignorant, they will seem incredible. For to understand is itself to believe. But to not believe is, without doubt, to be ignorant. Since my discourse <margin>To believe is to understand.</margin> has run all the way to the truth. The mind also, being broad and led by the discourse to a certain certainty, has touched the truth, finally comprehending all things and finding them in harmony with those things which...

<meta>This page continues a series of philosophical discourses attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary Hellenistic figure who represents a combination of the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth. These texts, known as the Corpus Hermeticum, blend Platonic, Stoic, and esoteric Egyptian thought.</meta>

->MFR.<-

...immediately believed what was interpreted and, in that very faith, rested gracefully. Therefore, regarding what is said of divine matters: once understood, they are believed; yet if not understood, they are denied. Let these words on understanding suffice.

### The Ninth [Chapter] instructs men.
While everyone makes use of their intellect, material men <note>In Hermetic thought, "material" men are those focused entirely on the physical world, as opposed to the "spiritual" or "essential" men.</note> conceive from the seeds of demons, whose presence is never absent from any part of the world. Being pregnant with these, they finally give birth to adultery, debauchery, murder, parricide, and every kind of disgrace. 

But essential and spiritual men conceive every ornament of virtue with God as the sower, and they give birth to every good duty of virtue. For God's seeds are few, but they are indeed great, beautiful, and good, far surpassing the countless multitude of vices. 

Yet those who follow these divine seeds do not please the common people, nor do the common people please them; they are eventually thought to be insane and are mocked, because the earth is the seat and province of wickedness. However, the world was formed by this law: that by receiving seeds from God, the whole might become an organ <note>The word "organum" here implies a functional instrument or tool through which the divine works.</note> worthy of divinity. And all things, in the wonder of a beautiful and orderly arrangement, would proceed to their birth, persevere within it, and finally reach their end. 

This would certainly happen, were it not for the enemy who comes and "sows weeds among the wheat" <note>original: "zizania" — this is a direct allusion to the Parable of the Weeds in the Gospel of Matthew 13:25, showing the influence of Christian terminology on this Latin translation.</note>. Therefore, you who have become spiritual, choose the divine seeds and cautiously flee the seeds of evil demons—who watch to seize us while we are sleeping in material affection. This concludes the ninth.

->### The Key of Hermes Trismegistus to Tat. X.<-

Yesterday, O Asclepius <note>Asclepius and Tat are the primary students or "disciples" to whom Hermes addresses his secret wisdom.</note>, I addressed the discourse to you. Therefore, it is right to dedicate today’s to Tat; it is all the more worthwhile as this present discussion will be a certain concise summary of all we have said before. 

God, the Father, and the Good, O Tat, have the same nature—or rather, the same activity. Indeed, those terms "growth" and "diminution" <note>The Latin "augmenti / diminutionisq" refers to the philosophical concept of things that can increase or decrease in size or quality.</note> pertain to things changeable and unchangeable—that is, human and divine things. He wills each of these to be. Elsewhere, as we have shown in other writings, there is the activity of divine and human things, which must be understood here. The "will" is the activity of this [God]; his essence is to "will" that all things exist. What is God?

->I 21<-
and Father and Good: unless it is the very knot of all these existing things. Furthermore, the very existence belongs to these: that is God, that is the Father, that is the Good, to whom nothing else from all things is applied <note>Meaning God is purely these things and cannot be defined by other attributes</note>. For the World and the Sun are fathers of those things which exist through participation <term>participation: the Neoplatonic idea that physical things exist only because they "take part" in a higher spiritual reality</term>; yet they are not the cause of life and goodness to living things. Because if this is so: the world is acted upon, encompassed by the will of the Good, without which it can never be nor become. Since he is the Father of children—of their procreation as well as their nourishment—receiving this through the desire for the Good alone (for the Good is active), it cannot belong to any other than him. 

Because since he receives nothing: he wills all things to be. I do not say, O Tat, that he *makes* all things. For a maker, failing over a long period of time, is unworthy of God. Indeed, a maker sometimes acts and sometimes stops; such a one is in need of both quantity and quality, sometimes arranging the "of what kind" and "how much," and at other times their opposites. But God is both Father and the Good, by the very fact that he is all things. Such things, therefore, can be prescribed for him. For he wills this to be, and it is. And he is truly himself above all: for all other things are for his sake. Indeed, it is the property of the Good, O Tat, to make itself known. That is the Good, O Tat.

**TAT.** Father of the vision, you have made us partakers of the good and the beautiful. By which the eye of my mind has now been purified by such a vision.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Nor indeed, just as the ray of the sun corrupts the eyes with excessive brightness and makes them dim, so is the sight of the Good itself. For it illuminates and increases the light of the eye all the more, the more one is able to receive the influx of the intelligible splendor <note>intelligible: referring to things understood by the mind/intellect rather than the physical senses</note>. The divine light is swift and sharper to penetrate, filling all things with innocence and immortality. Those who can drink more abundantly of this brightness from the body often fall asleep away from the body <note>This refers to a meditative or ecstatic trance where the soul leaves sensory perception</note> to behold the most beautiful sight, just as Uranus <margin>Uranus <note>Original: Celius</note></margin> and Saturn <margin>Saturn</margin>, our ancestors, did.

<summary>In this passage, Hermes Trismegistus explains that God is not a "maker" who labors, but the "Father" whose very will sustains existence. He describes a divine light that does not blind like the sun but instead purifies and illuminates the mind's eye.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, the Good, participation, intelligible splendor, divine light, Uranus, Saturn, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues a dialogue from the Hermetic corpus, specifically "The Key" (Libellus X), where Hermes Trismegistus instructs his son Tat on the nature of the Divine and the transmigration of souls.</meta>

->MERCURY<-

...spectators rise up. **TAT.** I wish that we could also, father. **TRISMEGISTUS.** I wish it were so, my son. But we are still too weak to behold it. For then we will be able to lift the eyes of the mind and gaze upon that incomprehensible beauty and goodness, when we have said nothing at all about it. Its knowledge is a **divine silence** <note>original: "divinum silentium"; a core Hermetic concept where the soul must be quieted of all sensory input to perceive the Divine.</note>, and the focused application of all our faculties. He who understands this can think of nothing else; he who gazes upon it can gaze upon nothing else but it; he who hears it can hear nothing after it, nor even move the limbs of his own body. Freed indeed from all bodily senses and movements, he remains still. For that which illuminates the whole also irradiates the entire mind from every side, draws the whole soul away from the body, and finally transforms it entirely into the essence of God.

For it is impossible, O son, for the soul of a man lying in the <term>dregs of the body</term> <note>original: "in corporis fece". In this philosophy, the physical body is viewed as a heavy, murky sediment that prevents the soul from rising to its true, light-filled nature.</note> to take on the divine form. Nor is it permitted to behold the beauty of God unless one has first been transformed into God.

**TAT.** In what way do you say this, O father?

**TRISMEGISTUS.** It is the distribution of every soul, my son.

**TAT.** But in what manner do you distribute these changes?

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Have you not heard in what we said generally, how from the one soul of the entire world, all souls flow out through the whole universe as if distributed and running about? The changes of these souls are indeed many: some truly for the better and more fortunate, but others for the contrary. For some of the reptiles <insert>change</insert> into...

<summary>Hermes explains that the vision of God requires a "divine silence" and a total transformation of the soul, which must be freed from the "dregs" of the physical body to regain its divine form. He begins to describe the process of metempsychosis, or how souls flow from a single source and undergo various transformations across the universe.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, divine silence, deification, soul, transmigration, beauty of God, contemplation, Hermetica</keywords>
