[Main Text]
...ties. This is indeed the judgment and punishment for a wicked soul: for the soul's depravity is ignorance. A soul that is ignorant of the nature of things and of the Good, having been blinded, entangles itself in the passions of the body. Such a soul—corrupted by the nature of a <term>cacodemon</term><note>original: *cacodemonis*. From the Greek *kakodaimōn*, meaning an "evil spirit" or "malevolent entity."</note>—is ignorant of itself and serves cast-off bodies and deformed monsters. It carries its own body about like a hostile burden; it does not rule the body but is enslaved by it due to its ignorance. Conversely, the virtue of the soul is knowledge <note>original: *cognitio*. In this context, it refers to *Gnosis*, or spiritual insight into the divine.</note>. For the person who is truly learned is good, pious, and divine.

TAT: Who is such a person, O father?

TRISMEGISTUS: One who neither speaks nor listens to much. For he who focuses on two discourses—whether in speaking or hearing—fights in the darkness. For God, the Father, and the Good are neither pronounced by the tongue nor perceived by the ears. Since these things are so in all things that come from Him—for they cannot be separate from Him—senses are present within them. However, there is a great difference between knowledge and sense. For sense is a movement of that which prevails; knowledge is the boundary of <term>science</term><note>original: *scientie*. Here meaning a structured understanding or wisdom.</note>. Science is the gift of God. Indeed, every science uses the incorporeal mind as its instrument, just as the mind uses the body. Therefore, both return into bodies: both the <term>intelligible</term><note>The realm of pure thought and spirit.</note> and the material. For it is necessary that all things consist of opposition and contrariety; it is impossible for it to be otherwise.

TAT: Who then is this material god?

TRISMEGISTUS: The World <note>original: *Mundus*. Referring to the physical cosmos as a living, divine entity.</note>. It is beautiful indeed, yet it is not "Good." For it is composed of matter and is subject to <term>passions</term><note>original: *passions*. In ancient philosophy, this refers to being acted upon or undergoing change, rather than just human emotions.</note>. It is the first of those things that suffer change, and the second of those things that exist <note>The Hermetic hierarchy usually places God as the First, the World/Cosmos as the Second God, and Man as the Third.</note>. It is needy, moreover, by its own nature; created at some time, yet always existing in a state of <term>generation</term><note>The process of "becoming" or continuous creation.</note>. And having been begotten, it is the parent of all qualities and quantities. For it is mobile; and all material motion ought to be called generation. The state of the intelligible also drives material motion in this way: since the world is a sphere—that is, a head—and nothing material exists above it.

<summary>Hermes explains that ignorance is the soul's punishment while knowledge is its virtue, and identifies the physical Cosmos as a "second god" that is beautiful but subject to change.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, Gnosis, Cacodemon, Material God, Cosmos, Generation, Intelligible Realm</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues a philosophical dialogue attributed to Hermes Trismegistus (Mercurius), likely from Book X of the *Corpus Hermeticum*, exploring the hierarchy of existence between God, the World, and Man.</meta>

HERMES.

Just as nothing beneath our feet is of the intellect <note>original: *intelligibile*; in Hermetic philosophy, this refers to the "intelligible" realm of pure spirit and ideas, as opposed to the "sensible" realm of physical objects</note>, all things there are material. Mind, however, moves the head in a circle—that is, a motion following the natural form of the head. Therefore, whatever parts are adjacent to the membranes of that head—which is truly the soul—are born immortal. It is as if the body were established within the soul; and those things that possess a soul are filled with the body.

Yet, whatever is far from that membrane—even those things that participate more in the soul—exists as a body. But the whole is a living being. Thus, the world is composed of both a material element and an intellectual element. The world is the first living being; man is the second, a living being following the world, but the first among all other living creatures. Whatever gift of soul is granted to others, man also possesses. However, he is considered not only "not good," but even evil, insofar as he is mortal <note>In this context, "evil" refers to the state of being subject to decay, suffering, and the limitations of physical matter, rather than just moral wickedness</note>.

The world is not "good" because it is subject to change and motion; yet it is not "evil" because it is immortal. Man, however, is judged to be evil both because he is subject to change and because he is mortal. The soul of man is carried in this manner: the Mind is within reason; reason is within the soul; the soul is within the spirit <term>spirit: or *pneuma*, an airy, subtle substance thought to act as the intermediary or "glue" connecting the immaterial soul to the physical flesh</term>; and the spirit is within the body.

The spirit, diffused through the veins, arteries, and blood, moves the living being in every part, sustains the heavy mass of the body, and carries it about. For this reason, some were deceived into thinking that the liquid of the blood was the soul; these people were clearly mistaken. For it is necessary first for the spirit to flow into the soul, then for the blood to thicken, and the hollow veins and arteries to expand; finally, the living being is dissolved, and that is the death of the body.

All things depend upon one Principle; the Principle depends upon the One and Only <note>The "One" refers to the ultimate Divine Source or God</note>. And the Principle itself moves so that it may continue to be a Principle. Yet the One itself remains firm and does not depart from its unity. Therefore, there are these three: God the Father (the Good), the World, and Man. God contains the world; the world contains man. The world is the son of God; man is the offspring of the world. For God is not ignorant of man; rather, He takes care of him and wishes to be known by him.

<summary>This text describes the metaphysical hierarchy connecting God, the World, and Humanity, explaining how the soul and spirit inhabit the physical body. It defines man as the "offspring of the world" who, despite his mortal and "evil" material nature, is cared for and known by the Divine.</summary>

<keywords>Hermeticism, Soul, Spirit, Pneuma, Macrocosm and Microcosm, Immortality, Trinity of God-World-Man, Anatomy of the Soul</keywords>

This is the only salvation for man: the knowledge of God. This is the ascent to Olympus <note>original: "olympũ"; here meaning the divine realm or the heavens</note>. From this alone the soul becomes good; not merely good at times and bad at others, but good by necessity.

**TAT.** How do you assert this, O my Trismegistus?

**TRISME.** I assert this: that the soul of a child contemplates itself, not yet having departed from its own nature due to the body (for the body is not yet fully formed), and it sees itself as beautiful in every way. For it is not yet corrupted by the passions of the body, and it still depends upon the Soul of the whole world.

But when the body is fully formed, and it disperses and draws the soul into its own mass, then the soul is subjected to forgetfulness and is deprived of the vision of the Beautiful and the Good. For forgetfulness itself is wickedness <note>The Hermetic tradition views "forgetting" one's divine origin as the source of all vice and suffering</note>. And so it happens to those who depart from the body. For when the soul returns into itself: the spirit <note>original: "spũs" (spiritus), referring here to the vital breath or pneuma that links soul and body</note> is drawn into the blood, and the soul into the spirit. The **Mind** <term>Mens: the highest, divine part of the human intellect, distinct from the lower soul</term>, being free from its veils and divine, having by its own nature obtained a fiery body, wanders through all places and abandons the soul to just judgment and deserved punishment.

**TAT.** In what way do you say this, O father?

**TRISME.** The Mind is separated from the soul, and the soul from the spirit. The garment of the Mind is the soul. The garment of the soul is the spirit. The listener, O son, must understand along with the speaker and be of one mind; the hearing must be sharper than the speaker's voice.

The wrapping of these garments, O son, is completed in the earthly body. For it is utterly impossible for the Mind, naked in itself, to take up residence in an earthly mass. For earthly dregs <note>meaning the heavy, impure matter of the physical world</note> are not able to receive so divine a Mind, nor can such a great divinity sustain a body strengthened by suffering. Therefore, the Mind took the soul as a cloak. Moreover, the soul, being divine, uses the spirit as a vehicle. The spirit permeates the whole living being. And so, as soon as the Mind is released from the earthly body, it immediately dons its own cloak: namely, a fiery body, by which it is safely surrounded so long as it cannot swell into an earthly body. For the earth—

<summary>Hermes explains the hierarchy of the human being, describing how the Mind (Mens) is clothed in the soul and spirit to inhabit the physical body, and how 'forgetfulness' of the divine leads to vice.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, Mind (Mens), Soul, Spirit (Pneuma), forgetfulness, salvation, fiery body, incarnation, afterlife judgment</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes (Mercurius) and his son Tat, focusing on the nature of the elements, the soul's journey after death, and the specific punishments for the impious.</meta>

**HERMES**

...which withstands fire the least: indeed, it would be entirely consumed by a tiny spark. For this reason, moisture is poured around the earthly mass, serving as a kind of barrier against the burning of the fire. Since fire is the sharpest and swiftest of all <term>divine concepts</term><note>The original Latin 'divinorum conceptuum' refers to the primordial ideas or forces used by the Creator.</note>, it encompasses the bodies of every individual element. For indeed, the Architect of the heavens himself uses fire above all else for his construction. The Workman of the whole certainly makes use of all things, but every earthly craftsman uses only those things which are found around the earth. For a human mind deprived of fire is fit only for human arrangements and is unable to construct divine things. 

Indeed, the human soul—not every soul, but the pious and blessed one—is divine. After such a soul has been released from the <term>prison of the body</term><note>A common Orphic and Platonic metaphor ('soma sema'—the body is a tomb) suggesting the physical form restricts the soul's true nature.</note> through death, having submitted to all the commands of virtue and piety, it becomes either Mind or a god. 

However, the contest of religious piety is this: to know God and to do harm to no one. Yet the impious soul remains in its own nature and torments itself excessively; it seeks an earthly and human body to enter. For no body other than a human one can receive a human soul; nor is it right for a rational soul to fall into the body of a creature lacking reason. For divine law prohibits a birth so wicked.

**TAT**

In what way then, O father, is the soul of a man tortured, and what punishment for this soul is more grievous?

**TRISMEGISTUS**

Impiety, my son Tat! For what fire has a more burning flame than that of impiety? What biting wild beast so tears the body as impiety mangles the soul? Do you not see how many evils weigh down the impious mind? 

"Alas!" cries out the impious soul, "I burn! I am consumed! I know not what to do. Evils flowing together from every side devour me, miserable as I am. I see nothing—alas, wretch—I hear nothing!" 

Such are the voices of the afflicted soul, and such is the penalty suited to its nature—not such as you, perhaps, my son, and certain others imagine: those who believe that our soul, after it has cast off the human form... <note>Hermes is about to clarify that the soul does not move into the bodies of beasts, a common misconception of reincarnation.</note>

<summary>Hermes explains that fire is the primary tool of the divine Creator and that a pious soul, after death, ascends to become 'Mind' or 'God.' He emphasizes that the greatest punishment for the wicked is the self-inflicted torment of their own impiety, rather than the common belief in reincarnation into animals.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, elements, divine fire, piety, afterlife, transmigration of souls, impiety, punishment, human mind</keywords>

Henry... I act... also... weeping...</margin> ...to degenerate into the bodies of beasts. For this is a wicked error; <note>The text is refuting the idea of metempsychosis—the belief that human souls can be reborn as animals—calling it a misunderstanding of how the soul is actually purified.</note> rather, the manner of its punishment is different. When the mind indeed becomes a <term>spirit<note>original: "demon" (daemon). In Hermetic philosophy, a daemon is a divine or semi-divine mediator, not an evil entity.</note></term>, it is commanded to take up a fiery body to serve God. From there, it is infused into a wicked soul and lashes it with the whips of sins. Beaten by these, the wicked soul turns itself toward murder, insults, abuse, and various thefts; it falls, finally, into all those things by which men commit offenses. 

But when the mind flows into a holy soul, it raises it to the light of wisdom. Such a soul never thereafter withers in the sluggishness of sleep, but instead offers help to the human race through both words and deeds; it assists in all ways, always imitating its Father. Therefore, O son, we must pray while giving thanks to God that we may be made partakers of a good mind. Indeed, the soul migrates toward the better, but never toward the worse. 

There is also a certain communion of souls: the souls of the gods communicate with the souls of men. God offers Himself in common to each, for He is more excellent than all others. All things, however, are weaker than He: let the world be subject to God, man to the world, and the beast to man. God is above all and around all. The rays of God are his operations; the rays of the world are the forces of nature; and the rays of man are his arts and sciences. 

Operations are carried out through the world, and these descend into man through the natural rays of the world. Nature acts through the elements, and man acts through the arts and sciences. This, indeed, is the administration of the whole world, depending upon the nature of the One and running rightly through one matter, than which nothing is more powerful, nothing more divine, and nothing more unified. This is the communion of men with the gods, and of gods with men. This is the "Good Spirit" <note>original: "bonus demon"</note>. The soul that is full of this is blessed; but the wretched soul is empty.

**TAT.** By what reasoning do you say this, O father?

It describes a cosmic hierarchy where God, the world, and humanity are linked through a system of "rays" consisting of divine acts, natural forces, and human knowledge.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Hermes Trismegistus (Mercurius), likely from the tenth book of the 'Corpus Hermeticum' (known as 'The Key'), focusing on the relationship between the Intellect (Mind) and the Soul, and the exalted status of humanity in the cosmos.</meta>

HERMES TRISMEGISTUS.

Know, my son: that every soul has the Good itself as its mind. For our present discourse is about this mind: not about the servant of the mind <note>original: "ministro huius," referring to the lower or irrational part of the soul</note> of whom we spoke above, who was sent down to the lower realms by judgment. A soul deprived of the presence of the mind is able neither to act nor to speak. 

Indeed, the mind is often outside the soul; at which time the soul neither hears nor sees, but is like a creature lacking reason. So great is the power of the mind: it leaves such a soul entangled in the body and dragged by that body toward the lower realms. Such a soul, my son, has no mind at all; therefore, it is not even right to call this being a human. 

For man is a <term>divine animal</term> <note>The term "animal" here is used in the classical sense of "a living being with a soul" (anima).</note>: he is not to be compared with earthly brutes, but with the celestial gods. Nay, if one must dare to speak the truth: the true human is either superior to the heaven-dwellers <note>original: "celicolis," the inhabitants of the heavens or stars</note> or at least enjoys an equal lot. For whichever of the celestials descends to earth must leave the boundary of heaven; but man ascends into heaven and measures it. Neither do the depths escape him, nor the heights. He diligently investigates all other things. And what is even greater: without even leaving the earth, he is lifted into heaven—so vast is the power of human nature. 

Therefore, one must dare to say: that an earthly human is a **mortal god**, while a celestial god is an **immortal human**. Thus, by the power of these two—namely, man and the world—individual things are governed. Finally, all things are subject to the One.

Let us proceed: He constantly asserts that God is absolutely all things, which individual things contract through a unique participation. Yet God is the Father and the Good by the very fact that he himself is all things. 

Soon he discusses the rapture of the soul in silence, where those things perceived by the eyes of the mind are neither lawful to reveal, nor is the choice to reveal them granted. Moreover, regarding what he adds concerning the <term>transformation of animals</term> <note>This refers to metempsychosis, the idea of souls moving into animal bodies.</note>, the following discourse of the same Mercury declares that one should follow an allegory of the story, rather than a literal fiction of words. 

As he says: "For no other body than the human one can contain a human soul; nor is it lawful for a rational soul to collapse into the body of a creature lacking reason: for divine law prohibits so wicked a generation." And a little after...

<summary>This page discusses the divinity of the human mind, describing man as a 'mortal god' capable of reaching heaven while on earth, and refutes the idea of human souls reincarnating into animals.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Mind and Soul, Divine Humanity, Mortal God, Metempsychosis, Corpus Hermeticum, Alchemy of the Soul</keywords>

### Mind to Mercury. XI
<note>This "Mercury" refers to Hermes Trismegistus. In this dialogue, "Mind" (Latin: *Mens*) is personified as a divine teacher speaking to Hermes.</note>

**MIND.** Stop delaying for so long, O Thrice-Great <term>Trismegistus</term> <note>A title meaning "Thrice-Greatest," attributed to the legendary sage Hermes.</note> Mercury. Be silent now; remember the things said before. Indeed, it will not grieve me to express my thought to you, all the more because many and varied opinions about the world and God wander among mortals. 

**TRISME.** Indeed, to confess the truth of this matter candidly: I do not yet grasp it myself. Therefore, my lord, express the pure truth. For I trust that you alone can illuminate such things. 

**MIND.** Listen to me then, O son. Time, God, and the universe relate to one another in this way: God is eternity; time is <term>generation</term> <note>The process of things being born, growing, and changing.</note>. God produces eternity; eternity produces the world; the world produces time; time produces generation. The essence <note>original: "essentia"</note> of God, as it were, is the Good, the Beautiful, blessedness, wisdom, and eternity. The essence of the world is its order; and the essence of time is change. For generation, there is death and life. The acts of God are the **Mind** and the **Soul**. Eternity is characterized by perseverance and immortality; the world by its institution and the restoration of time; and finally, generation by increase and decrease. 

Eternity, therefore, is in God; the world is in eternity; time is in the world; and generation is in time. Eternity stands around God. The world moves in eternity. Time is bounded within the world; generation is completed within time. Perhaps, then, God is the source of all; but eternity is their essence. The world is the work of eternity—not made at some specific point in time, but made always by the <term>aeon</term> <note>original: "euo" (aevum), referring to an infinite or celestial age.</note>. Since the aeon never ceases, the world will also never cease. And since the world is encompassed by eternity, no part of the world shall perish.

**TRISME.** What then is the wisdom of God? 

**MIND.** It is the Good, the Beautiful, blessedness, all virtue, and the aeon. From which immor[tality]...

If it belongs to both, which has the greater duty? Think of it this way: every living body, whether mortal or immortal, consists of matter and soul. Truly, all living bodies are animated <note>original: *animata*, meaning they possess a soul or *anima*.</note>; however, non-living things are almost entirely bare. Similarly, the soul, in its own nature and close to its parent <note>Referring to God or the Divine Mind as the "parent" of the soul.</note>, is the cause of life. To be the author of all these things is a role that is principal above all others. For there is one soul, one life, and one matter. Who is this author? Who else but the one God? ...generated by him. Come now, contemplate through me the world subject to your sight, and diligently examine its form: <note>The speaker is likely Divine Mind (Poimandres) instructing Hermes Trismegistus.</note> an inviolable body, than which nothing is older, grown strong through all its powers. Look besides at the seven worlds <note>original: "mundos septem"; these are the seven planetary spheres of the ancient cosmos: the Moon, Mercury, Venus, the Sun, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> placed beneath, crafted with wonderful ornament: in eternal order: completing eternity through their different courses, each one full of light; but of fire <note>In this philosophical system, the celestial spheres were often thought to be composed of a "fifth element" or pure light, distinct from the destructive terrestrial fire.</note> there is truly none in them. For the friendship and mixture of contraries and dissimilars established a light illuminated by the act of God—the parent of all goods, the prince of the whole order, and the eternal leader of the seven worlds. Consider the moon running through the others: the organ of nature, transforming the lower matter; and the earth situated in the middle of the world: the seat of this beautiful world, the nourishment of all earthly things, and their nurse. Meditate upon the number of mortal and immortal living beings, with the moon itself circling the middle, as if it were the boundary between both mortals and immortals. The Soul <note>original: "Aia," an abbreviation for "Anima." Here referring to the *Anima Mundi* or World Soul that permeates the universe.</note> again is full of all things, moved by her own motions: partly around the heavens, and partly around the earth. Neither do those things which are on the right move to the left, nor the left to the right. Likewise, neither do the things above move to those below, nor those below back to those above. But that all these things were generated, O my sweetest Mercury <note>original: "Mercuri"; referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary sage to whom these revelations are addressed.</note>: there is no need now for you to learn from my discourse. For they are bodies: they have a soul and they move. But it is impossible for these to come together into one without the power of the one who gathers them. Therefore, there must be someone who is such a person, and entirely one. For since there are many and various motions, and dissimilar bodies, yet one order of speed in all: it is impossible for there to be two or more makers. For a single order would not be maintained among many. Furthermore, the one among them who was weaker would surely envy the more powerful, from which a certain rebellion would arise. Therefore, if there were one creator of mutable animals, he would also desire to beget immortal beings, just as the parent of immortals would strive for mor—

<summary>This page describes the Hermetic vision of the cosmos, detailing the seven planetary spheres, the role of the Moon as a boundary, and the necessity of a single Divine Creator to maintain universal order.</summary>

M.F.R. <note>original: "MFR." likely refers to Marsilio Ficino (Marsilius Ficinus), the famous Renaissance philosopher, or perhaps a section marker for a 'Master' in a dialogue.</note>

...such things, even though there exists but one soul and one matter: to whom among them does the **fashioning** <term>fashioning: the Latin 'fabricatio' refers to the divine craftsmanship or the act of a 'Demiurge' in building the universe</term> of these things most belong? For if it belongs to both, to which of them falls the greater duty? 

Think of it this way: every living body, whether mortal or immortal, consists of matter and soul. Indeed, all living bodies are "ensouled" <note>original: 'animata', meaning possessing an *anima* or soul</note>; whereas things that do not live are, in almost all respects, bare. Likewise, the soul itself, being close to its own parent <note>referring to the divine source or the 'World Soul' from which individual souls emanate</note>, is the cause of life. Yet the cause of life is found in any of the immortal beings. 

In what way, then, do mortal living things differ from other mortals? And likewise, how do immortal things differ from other immortals? Therefore, to be the author of all these things is a distinction that stands above all others. For there is one soul, one life, and also one matter. 

Who is this author? Who else could it be besides the one God? Does it belong to anyone else but God alone to procreate living things? Thus, the one God has acknowledged one world; and...

<summary>This passage explores the metaphysical origins of life, arguing that while matter and soul are the components of all living things, only the single, unique God can be credited as the ultimate creator and fashioner of life.</summary>

...be present; give yourself entirely to me: you will immediately understand the <term>work of God</term> <note>original: *opus dei*. In the Hermetic tradition, the "work" refers to the continuous process of creation and the maintenance of the universe.</note>. It was necessary for this work to be present: so that those things might exist which are made, which were being made, and which will be made in the future. That indeed, O my sweetest Mercury, is life; that indeed is the beautiful, that indeed is the good, and that finally is God. But if you ask that I place these things before your eyes through examples of work: observe what happens to you when you wish to beget <note>original: *generare volenti*. The text uses human procreation as a metaphor for divine creation, though it notes the comparison is limited.</note>. Yet this is not similar to Him. For He is not diffused by pleasure; He has no helper in His work. Efficient by His own nature: working by His own power, He is constantly engaged, Himself always existing in His work, whatever He has ever made. If He should withdraw His influence from anything, all things would collapse into death, for life would fail. Since indeed all things live, and there is one life for all: God is one. Again, if all things are living—both those seen in heaven and those lying on earth—surely one life for all flows from God, and that life itself, and He Himself, is God. All things are made by one Founder. Life truly is the union of mind and soul; death, however, is not the destruction of compounded things, but the <term>dissolution</term> <note>original: *dissolutio*. Death is framed here not as an ending, but as the unbinding of a temporary physical connection.</note> of the union.

Therefore, the image of God is <term>Eternity</term> <note>original: *aevum*—the Aeon, representing timelessness or the eternal duration of the spiritual realm.</note>; the image of Eternity is the world; of the world, the sun; and of the sun, man. But indeed, the common people believe death to be a certain change, for this reason: because the mass of the body is dissolved, and life withdraws itself into <term>the hidden</term> <note>original: *in occultum*. This refers to the invisible, spiritual dimension from which life originates and returns.</note>. Truly, O beloved Mercury, I teach you by this reasoning that the world is indeed changed. Because certain particles of it continually depart into the hidden, yet do not ever think that the world itself perishes. These surely are the <term>passions</term> <note>original: *passiones*. In this context, it refers to the "affections" or states of being of the world, rather than emotional passions.</note> of the world: namely, revolution and hiding. Revolution indeed is conversion; hiding, however, is renewal. Furthermore, the world, which possesses all forms, does not receive forms from outside or as strangers. Instead, it stirs them up within itself. It will also be like the world that contains all forms; but if it had only a single form, it would be inferior to the world in this respect. What then shall we say to these things?

<summary>This page explains that God is the singular source of life and that 'death' is merely the dissolution of a physical union and a retreat into the invisible realm. It establishes a hierarchy of divine images: God, Eternity, the World, the Sun, and finally, Man.</summary>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue of Hermes Trismegistus (Mercurius). The previous text likely established the distinction between the material and the divine. Here, Hermes explains the nature of "Ideas" and the infinite capacity of the soul.</meta>

->MER.<-

I pray you, let us not remain in uncertainty. For that which in divine matters is uncertain <note>original: "ambiguo", meaning doubtful or obscure</note> is not yet understood; it has, therefore, a single Idea. But because the specific Idea of this escapes the sight of our eyes, it is bodiless. And it expresses its shape through individual bodies. Nor should any wonder at all take hold of you because there exists a certain bodiless Idea. For it is like the Form <term>Idea: In this Hermetic and Platonic context, an "Idea" or "Form" is the eternal, perfect pattern of which earthly things are mere shadows.</term> of speech and the marks used in inscriptions. For they seem to stand out prominently, yet by their nature they are clearly weightless. 

But meditate on what will now be said more boldly, and what will be asserted with even greater truth. Just as a human being cannot live without life, so God cannot live unless He produces good things. For this is the life of God, this is His action: to move all individual things and to breathe life into all. Some of the things we have said above, however, require a specific kind of understanding. 

Consider in this example what I most wish to signify. All things are in God, but not as if they were placed in a location. For a "location" is a stationary body, and things placed within it lack motion. Indeed, things are situated differently in a physical body than they are in the imagination <note>original: "phantasia"</note>. Think of Him as containing all things; think that nothing is more spacious than a bodiless nature, nothing faster, nothing stronger, and that it is itself the most spacious, fastest, and most powerful of all. 

So again, beginning from yourself, meditate: and command your soul, and it will fly forth faster than you can give the command. Command it, I say, to cross over into the ocean; it will be there before you have finished the order, without ever departing from where it is now. Command it again to fly into heaven; it will need no wings. Nothing will block its course—not the burning of the sun, nor the vastness of the sky <note>original: "etheris", the upper atmosphere or heavens</note>, nor the spinning of the heavens, nor the bodies of the other stars—but penetrating through all, it may transcend even to the highest body. Moreover, if you wish to pass through all the spheres of the heavens and investigate what lies beyond, that too <note>The sentence ends abruptly, implying the soul's capacity is limitless.</note>

<summary>Hermes explains that God is a bodiless "Idea" whose very life consists of creating good and animating the universe. He uses the human soul’s ability to travel instantly through thought as an analogy for how all things exist within the infinite, non-spatial mind of God.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, incorporeal, divine action, imagination, omnipresence, soul's flight, celestial spheres, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues a dialogue from the previous section where the nature of the divine and its relationship to the visible world is being discussed. The speaker 'MFR' is likely Marsilio Ficino, the Renaissance philosopher who translated these Hermetic texts from Greek into Latin.</meta>

M.F.R. <note>original: "MFR." Likely referring to Marsilio Ficino, the primary translator and commentator of this text.</note>
...nature: because it does not bear within itself some image of divinity. 

->TRISMEGISTUS<-
Is God not invisible? 

->MIND<- <note>original: "MENS." In Hermetic philosophy, "Mens" or Divine Mind is a personified teacher representing the highest form of consciousness.</note>
Speak more reverently, Trismegistus. For who is more radiant than He? Indeed, He crafted all things for this very reason: so that you might perceive Him through each individual thing. This is the goodness of God; this is His power: that He shines through all things. There is nothing, even among incorporeal <note>Incorporeal: existing without a physical body or material form.</note> things, that is invisible. The mind itself is seen through the act of understanding; God, however, is perceived in His handiwork. Let these matters be explained <note>original: "enodata," literally meaning "unknotted."</note> to you thus far, Trismegistus; all other things you shall now examine for yourself, and you shall not be deceived by a false image of the truth.

### The eleventh book concerning the Mind: 
Filled with divine discourse, it declares that eternity comes from God, the world from eternity, time from the world, and change from time; and that life and death depend upon this change. It demonstrates most pleasingly that no power—neither divine, nor human, nor of those above, nor of those below—is comparable to the insurmountable power of God. It shows Him to be the only One, through both reason and analogies. Since He cannot be idle, He is always making all things. You may rightly conceive of this as what is said concerning the eternal generation of the Word <note>original: "verbi." The translator is drawing a parallel between the Hermetic "Word" and the Christian "Logos" found in the Gospel of John.</note>. For it says that it was necessary for the divine work to be present, so that those things which are made, were not made before, and will be made in the future, might exist. That is life; that is the beautiful; that is the good; that is, finally, God and the life of all. 

But what is this, if not what the trumpet of the Evangelist sang more loftily when he said: *In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God. He was in the beginning with God; all things were made through Him, and without Him nothing was made that was made. In Him was life.* <note>The author is quoting the opening of the Gospel of John (John 1:1-4) to show the harmony between Hermetic philosophy and Christian scripture.</note> 

Therefore, the life of all flows from God and is itself God. Thus it happens that true death is not the destruction of things, as is commonly thought, but rather that by which the life of living things, withdrawing itself into hiddenness, leaves the body. Indeed, it uses a marvelous proportion to comprehend Him who escapes all proportion; who, though grasped, remains incomprehensible; and who, unknown to all, presents Himself as knowable in all things. Therefore, all you who are pious, be present here now in your entirety; incline your mind to the discourses of the Mind, so that you might finally know Him who for this reason crafted all things: so that you might perceive Him through each individual thing. Thus far, the Mind.

¶ Mercury to Tat, concerning common matters. XII. <note>This marks the beginning of the twelfth book/chapter, a dialogue between Hermes (Mercury) and his son/disciple Tat.</note>

<meta>This page continues the Hermetic dialogues between Hermes Trismegistus and his son, Tat, focusing on the nature of the Mind (Mens) and its relationship to the Divine Essence.</meta>

I	26 29

<term>Mind</term> <note>Original: "Ens", likely a transcription error for "Mens" (Mind) given the context</note> indeed, O Tat, is born from the very essence of God—if indeed He has an essence. This essence, whatever it may be, alone understands itself in its purity.
Mind, therefore, is by no means divided from the essence of God, but is rather connected to it in the same way that light is connected to the body of the sun.
This Mind is God in humans; and for that reason, some among the number of men are gods, and their humanity is very close to divinity. For indeed, the <term>Good Spirit</term> <note>Original: "bonus demon," referring to Agathos Daimon, a figure of divine wisdom in Hermetic texts</note> calls gods "immortal humans" and humans "mortal gods."
In irrational animals, however, that Mind is simply their nature. For wherever there is a soul, there is also Mind, just as wherever there is life, there is also a soul in living things—though without the discourse of reason. The soul [in animals] is life void of Mind. Mind is indeed the helper of human souls, recalling them to their own proper good.
In animals lacking reason, Mind works together with the nature of each through their natural operations. In the souls of men, however, it sometimes resists and fights back. For the soul, once it is <insert>implanted</insert> <note>Original: "insula," likely a typo for "insita" (implanted) or "inclusa" (enclosed)</note> in the body, is continually corrupted by pleasure and pain.
For from the mixture of the body, pleasure and pain gush forth like certain streams, in which the soaking soul is suffocated.
Therefore, over whichever souls Mind presides, it illuminates them with its own brightness, resisting their habits and their evils.
Just as a skilled physician subjects the body of a sick person to pain—by burning and cutting it for the sake of recovering health—in the same way, Mind afflicts the pleasure-seeking soul to uproot the roots of pleasure. For from this pleasure comes every disease of the soul; the most serious disease of the soul is <term>impiety</term> <note>The lack of reverence for the divine order</note>.
False opinion <note>Original: "opinio", referring to mere belief or sensory illusion versus true knowledge</note> does not entice toward any good at all, but rather toward evil.
But indeed, that resisting Mind provides for the good of the soul, just as the physician provides for the health of the body.
As many human souls as do not have Mind as a strong governor suffer the same things as the souls of brute animals. They are sent...

<summary>This page discusses the divine origin of the Mind (Mens), describing it as an extension of God's essence that acts as a guiding force in humans to combat the corrupting influences of bodily pleasure and pain.</summary>
<keywords>Mind, Tat, Agathos Daimon, soul, divinity, pleasure and pain, impiety, Hermetica</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the sage Hermes Trismegistus and his son Tat, specifically addressing the nature of the Mind (Nous) and its interaction with Fate.</meta>

->MER<-
<note>original: "MER" is likely an abbreviation for Mercurius (Hermes Trismegistus), used here as a running header.</note>

...[the Mind] subjects them to the command of desires; toward the fulfillment of which they are carried by a certain burning impulse, and after the manner of wild beasts, they are immoderate in both anger and longing. And what is worse, they neither put any end to their lust, nor do they find a limit to their evils and passions. To these, God has proposed the law as a sort of lictor <note>A lictor was a Roman officer who executed the sentences of judges, symbolizing the enforcement of divine justice.</note> and judge.

**TAT.** Here, O father, that discussion concerning **fate** (which we left unfinished in the previous sections) revives. For if it is decreed that some are to be adulterers and some perhaps sacrilegious, why is anyone punished for it, since they have sinned compelled by the necessity of fate?

**TRISMEGISTUS.** All things are the works of fate, my son. Without it, nothing of bodily things exists. Nothing good can happen without it, nor can anything evil occur without it. It is decreed that he who has committed something shameful shall suffer. He commits the act for this very reason: so that he may suffer exactly what he does suffer, because he committed the act. However, concerning fate and the punishments for crimes, we have spoken in other places. Our present discourse is about the **Mind**: what the Mind is capable of, and how its instinct differs in humans and in brutes <note>The term 'brutes' refers to non-human animals, which the text views as lacking a rational soul.</note>. And again, how in brutes the Mind itself is not beneficial, but in humans it restrains the impulse of lust and extinguishes the fire of wrath. From this it follows that among men, some exist as irrational and some as rational. 

Truly all men are subject to fate, to birth, and to change; for these two—namely birth and change—are the beginning and the end of fate. And all men indeed suffer that which fate has ordained. But those rational men, whom we said the **Mind** assists as a governor, do not suffer in the same way as the rest. Rather, being far from wickedness and not being evil, they suffer very little evil.

**TAT.** In what way do you say these things again, O father?

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Is not an adulterer evil? Is not a murderer evil? A man who is not an adulterer suffers, but in the same manner as an adulterer. Not...

<summary>In this dialogue, Hermes Trismegistus explains that while Fate governs all physical things and births, humans with a "rational Mind" can control their animalistic passions and avoid the spiritual punishments of wickedness. He addresses the paradox of why people are punished if Fate ordains their actions, suggesting that the Mind acts as a divine governor for the soul.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, Fate, Mind, Nous, Free Will, Divine Law, Rationality, Human Nature vs. Animal Nature</keywords>

murderer: but in what way a murderer? It is impossible to escape the quality of change, just as it is impossible to escape the effect of generation. <note>In Hermetic philosophy, "generation" refers to the process of being born into the physical world, which inherently involves change and decay.</note> However, it is permitted for one who possesses a **mind** to avoid wickedness. For this reason, my son, I have always heard the **Good Spirit** <note>original: "bonum demonem," referring to Agathodaemon, a divine teacher or personification of wisdom</note> dictating; if he had committed his monuments to writing, he would have conferred wonderful benefits upon the human race every day. He alone, my son—as if he were a god born for me—beholding all things, poured out divine oracles upon us. Now indeed I once heard him preaching thus: all things are one, especially the **intelligible** <term>intelligible: things that can only be understood by the mind/intellect, rather than perceived by the physical senses</term> bodies; we live by power, by action, and by eternity. The Mind of this [One] is good, just as its soul is good. Since this is so, nothing of the intelligible is distant from the Intelligible. Furthermore, the Mind, the prince of all things and the soul of God, is able to effect whatever it wills. These are his words. You, therefore, consider these things; and stand ready with your mind and attentive ears for this discourse on **fate** which I shall introduce. If you diligently avoid litigious traps <note>referring to confusing or hair-splitting logical arguments</note>, you will find without doubt that the Mind, the soul of God, dominates all things—even the Law and everything else. Nor is anything of those things which pertain to Fate impossible for the Mind. Therefore, the human spirit is superior to Fate, yet it does not neglect those things which are subject to Fate. And these are the best oracles of the Good Spirit.

**TAT.**
Truly divinely and also aptly have you handled these matters, O father. But I pray you declare this further to me. You said that the Mind operates in brute animals in the manner of nature, cooperating with their impulses. But the impulses of irrational creatures (as I judge) are **passions** <term>passions: from the Latin 'passio' (suffering/undergoing), meaning things the soul experiences or is subjected to by the body</term>; therefore, the mind is a kind of passion, for it is thus shaped by passions.

**TRISMEGISTUS.**
Well done, my good son; you ask nobly. It is right that I should answer you. All incorporeal things that exist within bodies are subject to being acted upon; indeed, they are properly "passions" themselves. Every movement of the body...

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues a dialogue from the Corpus Hermeticum, specifically focusing on the relationship between the mind, the body, and the unique gifts given to humanity. The speaker "MER." refers to Mercurius (Hermes Trismegistus) and "TAT" is his disciple.</meta>

->MERCURY<-

...corporeal <note>The text begins mid-word; likely "corporeus" (corporeal) continuing from the previous page.</note>: the <term>incorporeal</term> <note>That which has no physical substance, like the soul or mind.</note> is also moved by the mind. Movement, however, is a <term>passion</term> <note>original: "passio"; in ancient philosophy, this refers to the state of being acted upon or "undergoing" something, rather than just an emotion.</note>. Therefore, both that which is moved and that which moves undergo something: the one as the ruler, the other as the subject. When it is separated from the body, it is freed from these passions. Indeed, my son, nothing is entirely beyond "undergoing" <note>original: "impatibile"; incapable of being acted upon.</note>; for all things are capable of it. 

But "passion" and the "capability of passion" differ most in this: that one acts, and the other is acted upon. Physical bodies, truly, also act according to their own nature; for they are either immobile or they are moved. Whichever of these occurs, it is an experience of passion. 

Incorporeal things, however, are always operating <note>original: "operantur"; acting or working.</note>; and for that reason, they are capable of passion. Do not let these names disturb you. For "operation" and "passion" are the same thing; yet there is no harm in using a more honorable term <note>The speaker suggests that "operation" sounds more noble than "passion" even if they describe the same interaction.</note>.

->TAT<-

You have given a very clear explanation of these things, O father.

->TRISMEGISTUS<-

Consider this further, O son: that God himself has granted these two things to man alone out of all living creatures: namely, **speech** <note>original: "sermonem"; often translating the Greek "Logos," which means both speech and reason.</note> and **mind**. These are considered to be of the same value as the reward of immortality. If anyone uses these gifts as is proper, he differs in no way from the immortals. Furthermore, once he is released from the shackles of the body, he will be led by both mind and speech into the choir of the blessed and the gods.

->TAT<-

Do not the other living creatures besides man use speech?

->TRISMEGISTUS<-

No, my son; they use **voice**. There is a vast difference between voice and speech. Speech is common to all men; but voice is unique to each individual person, and unique to each kind of animal.

->TAT<-

O father, different kinds of men use different languages.

->TRISMEGISTUS<-

They are different, O son. Yet speech itself is one; when translated from one place to another through interpretation, it is found to be the same Word <note>original: "verbum"; again referring to the concept of the Logos.</note>. This Word is the same among the Egyptians, the Persians, and the Greeks. But you seem, my son, to be ignorant of the power and the greatness of the Word.

<summary>In this passage, Hermes Trismegistus explains that while all things are subject to "passion" (being acted upon), humans are uniquely endowed by God with Mind and Speech (Logos). He argues that these two gifts allow humans to transcend their mortal bodies and achieve the same status as the gods.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, Logos, Mind, Speech, Immortality, Philosophy, Incorporeal, Passion, Human Nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the sage Hermes Trismegistus and his son Tat, common in the Hermetic tradition. The previous context likely established the nature of the cosmos and God, leading into this discussion on whether anything truly "dies."</meta>

HERMES.

**TAT.** Father, do not the living beings <note>original: "animalia" — in this philosophical context, it refers to any being endowed with "anima" or soul, including humans and animals.</note> that are in the world, being parts of the world, perish?

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Speak more correctly, my son. For you err in the term. Nothing in the world dies; instead, composite bodies are dissolved. This dissolution is not death, but rather a breaking down of a mixture. The union is dissolved not so that the things which exist are destroyed, but so that old things may be made young again.

**TAT.** Since life is a kind of operation, is this not also a kind of motion? And therefore, what in the world is motionless?

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Nothing, my son.

**TAT.** Does the earth not seem motionless to you?

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Not at all. Though it is shaken by many movements, it is the only thing that is, in a certain way, stable. Would it not be ridiculous if the very nurse of all things—who conceives and brings forth every individual thing—lacked motion? For it is impossible for anything to give birth without motion. It is no less ridiculous to call that body "sterile," for the name "motionless" signifies nothing other than a kind of sterility. Consider all this, my son: everything in the world that belongs to the realm of <term>generation</term> <note>The process of coming into being, birth, and growth.</note> moves by either growing or decreasing. Whatever moves also lives; however, it is not necessary that all living things be the same. Indeed, the universe as a whole exists all at once; as a whole it is indeed motionless, but its parts are everywhere movable. Yet nothing is subject to <term>corruption</term> <note>original: "corruptioni" — meaning total destruction or ceasing to exist.</note>; instead, certain false names trouble mankind.

For "generation" is not the creation of life, but the unfolding of life that was hidden. Nor is "change" death; rather, it is a concealment. Since these things are so, all things are immortal. The <term>matter</term> of life is spirit; the <term>mind</term> is the matter of the soul. <note>In Hermetic thought, "spirit" (spiritus) is often a subtle physical vapor that connects the divine mind to the physical body.</note> From the Mind, everything living flows forth. Therefore, everything that lives through the Mind remains immortal.

But man is the most immortal of all, for he perceives God and is conformed to the divine essence. For to him alone, out of every kind of living being, God himself draws near: by night through dreams, and by day more frequently through omens, by which all future things are foretold to him. Through birds...

<summary>Hermes Trismegistus explains to Tat that death is merely the dissolution of composite bodies and that everything in the universe, including the Earth, is in constant motion and therefore alive. He argues that nothing truly perishes, but rather changes form, and emphasizes that humans have a unique, immortal connection to the Divine Mind.</summary>

<keywords>Hermeticism, immortality, motion vs. stability, dissolution, generation, divine essence, omens, Hermes Trismegistus, Tat</keywords>

...through the intestines <note>likely referring to haruspicy, the ancient practice of divining the future by inspecting the entrails of sacrificed animals</note>; through the spirit; and through the Sibyl <note>a female prophet in the ancient world</note>. Because of these things, it is truly said that man knows what is, what was, and what is soon to come. But above all, my son, I would have you observe that each kind of living creature inhabits its own province of the world: the moist ones inhabit water, the terrestrial ones the earth, and the winged ones the air.

Man, however, uses all of these: earth, water, air, and fire. He looks up at the heavens and reaches them with his senses. God, however, is around all things and through all things at once. For He is the <term>act|In this philosophical context, 'act' (actus) refers to the realization or expression of power, rather than just a simple deed</term> and the power of all things. Indeed, it is a difficult thing, O son, to perceive God. Whenever you wish to behold Him, observe the order of the world and the beauty of that order. Observe the necessity of those things perceived by the senses, and the providence <note>original: "providentia", the divine care or guidance of God over the universe</note> of all things—both what was made before and what is made daily. Observe the matter full of life, and such a great God walking with all good things—with beautiful gods, <term>daemons|In the ancient sense, 'daemons' are guardian spirits or intermediary beings between gods and humans, not 'demons' in the modern sense</term>, and men.

**TAT:** But these, O father, are certain operations <note>original: "operationes", meaning workings or activities of a divine nature</note>.

**TRISMEGISTUS:** Ah son, from where do these come if not from God himself? Do you not know that just as the sky, water, earth, and air are parts of the world, in the same way, life, immortality, necessity, providence, nature, soul, and mind are the members of God? And the participation in all of these is the Good itself. Neither could anything else exist anywhere, nor has anything been made, where God himself is not present.

**TAT:** So He is in matter then, father?

**TRISMEGISTUS:** Matter, my son, is separate from God, so that a place proper to it may be assigned. What else do you think matter is, but this rude and unordered mass? <note>original: "rudem indigestaque congeriem", a likely reference to Ovid's description of Chaos in the 'Metamorphoses'</note> Do not believe it could exist otherwise unless it were formed. And if it is formed, it is certainly formed by someone. For we have said that the operations of forming are parts of God. By whom, then, is life given to individual living things? By whom do they become immortal, those who enjoy the gift of immortality? By whom [are they]...

<summary>In this dialogue, Hermes Trismegistus explains to his son Tat that God is omnipresent, moving through all elements of the world, and that human life and the order of the cosmos are direct expressions or 'members' of the divine. He describes matter as a chaotic mass that only takes shape through the formative power of God.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, Hermeticism, Providence, Divine Order, Matter and Form, Daemons, Elements, Cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse of Hermes Trismegistus (referred to here as Mercury), likely concluding one book and transitioning into the twelfth. The text focuses on the omnipresence of God within all matter and the distinction between the human mind and the instincts of animals.</meta>

->MER<-
<note>Short for Mercurius, the Latin name for Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary sage to whom these teachings are attributed.</note>

...those things which are changeable—whether you call them matter or body—remember that they are the acts of God. The act of matter is the principle of matter; the bodily act is the principle of bodies; the act of essence is the principle of essence. This is the whole: there is nothing in the universe <note>original: "omni"</note> which is not God Himself. Therefore, neither magnitude, nor place, nor quality, nor shape, nor even time exist as something apart from God, for He is everything. He is all things, surrounding all things, and moving through all things. Cherish and adore this word, O son. The true worship of God is but one thing: to not be evil.

### The Twelfth [Book], concerning the lower mind
(which is a certain image or footprint of the primary mind)

Continuing after the precise definition of the primary mind, this book associates it with individual souls. But in irrational creatures, the mind acts as nature; in humans, however, it is reason. Those humans who are deprived of the benefit of reason are carried away—like wild beasts—by a certain flaming impulse toward the fulfillment of their desires. They set no limit to their lust, nor do they find an end to their evils and passions. By fate and divine decree, they suffer and pay the deserved penalties for their ignorance.

The good, on the contrary—over whom the mind, an image of the good and the beautiful, presides—receive the good things that fate has established for them. You shall by no means escape fate, for fate is indeed divine law. Nevertheless, you can flee from evils and the penalties that follow wickedness.

Mercury, full of oracular power, discusses many things. He often heard the oracle pouring into him. He commends two divine gifts in humans: mind and speech. He opens the oracles of the Good Spirit <note>original: "boni demonis." In this context, a 'daemon' is a benevolent guiding spirit or divine intellect, not an evil entity.</note>, or if you prefer, of the celestial spirit, to those who are divinely inspired. He speaks about God, the Word, the world, and matter. He teaches that nothing in the world is devoid of life; nothing should be called "mortal" (but rather should be understood as something "dissolvable"); and nothing is immobile. He explains that God meets man—alone among dissolvable things—in various ways, and shows how man can rise from the world of the senses <note>original: "sensibilibus"</note> to the perception of God. These things are contained in the twelfth book.

#### Of Mercury to his son Tat

...only one thing then remained for me to learn. You then finally promised that you would reveal it once I had become alienated <note>original: "alienatus"; meaning withdrawn or detached from worldly concerns</note> from the world. See, I am now ready, father. I have shaken the deceptions of the world from my mind. Therefore, keep your promises. Explain the manner of Regeneration <note>The process of spiritual rebirth or transformation into a divine being</note>, whether openly or in secret, as it pleases you. I do not know, O Trismegistus, from what matter or from what seed man is born.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** O son, wisdom is contemplative in silence; its seed is the True Good.

**TAT.** By whom is it sown, father? For this I do not know.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** By the will of God, my son.

**TAT.** And of what nature is he who is born? For he is devoid of the intelligible essence <note>original: "intelligibilis essentie"; the spiritual substance that can only be understood by the mind, not the senses</note>. If it were otherwise, he who is born would be a god, the son of God.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** All in all: he consists of all powers.

**TAT.** You speak in riddles, O father, and you do not speak to your son as you did before.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** This kind of person, my son, is not taught; but whenever God wills, the word is recalled in God’s memory through a certain suggestion.

**TAT.** You suggest impossible and overly violent things. Therefore, I desire to contradict these.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** You differ greatly from your father’s nature, Tat.

**TAT.** Do not begrudge me, father, for I am a legitimate son. Come, I pray: teach me the way of regeneration.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** What shall I say, my son? I have nothing to say beyond this: I see a true vision, offered to me now by God’s kindness. Whence I have been translated into an immortal body; I am no longer who I was before, but have recently become Mind <note>original: "mens"; referring to the Divine Mind or 'Nous', a higher state of consciousness</note>. This mystery is not taught. See this formed element through which one may see, by which my first composite form <note>His physical body</note> was neglected; not that I am colored or have touch or limit. From these things I am currently alienated.

**TAT.** You have stirred me into a fury and a madness of the mind, O father; at present, I scarcely [know] myself...

<summary>This page contains a dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and his son Tat regarding the mystery of spiritual regeneration. Hermes explains that rebirth is achieved through the will of God and results in a transformation from a physical being into a being of pure "Mind."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and his son Tat, focusing on the Hermetic concept of "Regeneration" (spiritual rebirth) and the transcendence of the physical senses to perceive the divine Truth.</meta>

**HERMES**
<note>original: "MER." This is likely an abbreviation for Mercurius, the Latin name for Hermes.</note>
I see. **TRISMEGISTUS:** I wish, dearest son, that you too had passed through yourself and were no longer sleeping, like those who are occupied by sleep and dreams. **TAT:** Come, tell me: who is the author of regeneration? **TRISMEGISTUS:** The son of God, a human <note>original: "Dei filius hõ" (homo). In Hermetic thought, this refers to the "Essential Man" or the spiritual ideal of humanity, rather than the specific Christian figure of Jesus.</note>, by the will of the one God. **TAT:** Now, father, you have made me mute and stunned. I am alienated from my former state of mind, for I perceive the same greatness through the <term>character</term> <note>meaning the outward sign or distinguishing mark</note> of these things, and yet there is a lie within them. For the mortal form is transformed every single day; it turns toward increase and decrease over time, as it is false. What then, Trismegistus, is true? **TRISMEGISTUS:** That which is not disturbed, not limited, not colored, not shaped, not divided, naked, clear, comprehensible by itself, the unchangeable Good, and entirely <term>incorporeal</term> <note>existing without a physical body or material substance</note>. **TAT:** Truly, I am going mad now, my father. For while I had hoped to be made wise by you through such knowledge, my senses have become dull. **TRISMEGISTUS:** It happens so, son. For that which is carried upward like fire, and downward like earth, and wet like water, and breathing like air—how can you perceive the Truth by the senses? It is not hard, not soft, not dense, not penetrable; it is to be considered only by power and act. He who is able, let him pray that he may understand the <term>regeneration</term> <note>original: "generationē" (generationem), used here in the sense of spiritual rebirth or being "born again" into divine knowledge</note> which is in God. **TAT:** I am powerless, father. **TRISMEGISTUS:** Far be it, O son. Return into yourself, and you shall achieve it. Will it, and it shall happen. Purge the senses of the body; free yourself from the irrational <term>tormentors</term> <note>original: "vltoribus" (ultoribus). These are the twelve vices or "punishers" of the material world that bind the soul to the body.</note> of matter itself. **TAT:** Are the tormentors within? **TRISMEGISTUS:** Not a few, O son, but many and horrible. **TAT:** I do not know them, father. **TRISMEGISTUS:** Behold, son: the first tormentor is Ignorance. The second: Sadness. The third: Inconstancy. The fourth: Lust. The fifth: Injustice. The sixth: Luxury. The seventh: Deception. The eighth: Envy. The ninth: Fraud. The tenth: Anger. The eleventh: Rashness. The twelfth: <note>The text cuts off here at "duodecima" (twelfth). In other manuscripts, the twelfth is usually Malice.</note>

<summary>In this dialogue, Hermes explains to Tat that spiritual rebirth is a divine act of will and defines the "True" as that which is incorporeal and unchanging. He instructs Tat to purge the twelve "tormentors" (vices) of the material world to achieve this state of enlightenment.</summary>

...indeed malice. These are twelve in number. Under
these, many others are contained, which, through the prison of the body,
force the enclosed, mournful man to suffer through his senses. But
these are absent from him who relies upon the mercy of God. And
thus the manner and discourse of rebirth <note>original: *regenerationis*</note> is established. Hence-
forth be silent, my son, and praise God by being silent; in that
way, divine mercy will never be absent from us. Rejoice,
henceforth, my son: for by divine power you have been raised into the contemplation
of truth. The knowledge of God descends into us.
With its arrival, ignorance is repelled. The knowledge
of joy descends into us: with this present, sadness completely
flees. And it turns toward those who are prepared to
receive it. I call the power leading to joy "constancy" <term>constancy: spiritual stability or perseverance</term>,
whose force is undoubtedly most sweet. Let us
embrace it most willingly, son: for as soon as
it arrives, it will utterly expel all moral softness <note>original: *mollitiem*, referring to spiritual or character weakness</note>.
Now I call the fourth "continence" <note>self-control or temperance</note>: the force that conquers all
desires. This step, O son, stands as the foundation
of justice. Notice how it has expelled injustice from all
works. We have indeed been made just,
son, once injustice is absent. I call the sixth power
descending into us "communion" <note>sharing or fellowship</note>, namely, against excess.
Finally, as this departs, I invoke Truth. Deception
soon flees; Truth is present. See how the Good is
made perfect when Truth is present. For envy has departed from us.
For the Good is innate to Truth, along with life and
light. No further has the avenging spirit <note>original: *vindex*</note> approached.
Indeed, all the avenging torments were rejected
by a sudden impulse. You have understood, son,
the manner of rebirth itself: with the number ten <note>The ten divine powers</note> present,
the intellectual generation is composed, which rejects the number
twelve <note>The twelve zodiacal torments or vices</note>. And we have observed this from the rebirth itself.
Whoever, therefore, on account of kindness...

The sensible body of nature differs greatly from essential generation <note>original: "generatio." Here meaning the process of spiritual birth or the origin of being.</note>. One is indeed dissoluble; the other is indissoluble. One is mortal; the other is immortal. Or are you truly ignorant that both a god and an only son <note>In Hermetic philosophy, the "son" often refers to the created World or the Logos.</note> have been born? **TAT.** Oh, how I would wish, my father, to have heard from you that analogy through the hymn you said you heard from the Powers <note>Divine celestial beings or emanations.</note> when you yourself were in the <term>Octonary</term> <note>The Eighth Sphere (the realm of fixed stars), which in ancient cosmology represents a state of spiritual enlightenment above the seven planetary spheres.</note>. **TRISMEGISTUS.** The <term>Poimandres</term> <note>Also called "Pimander," the "Divine Mind" or "Shepherd of Men" who initiates Hermes into sacred mysteries.</note> himself sang the Octonary, my son. Therefore, it is right for you to cast off your shadow <note>original: "vmbraculum." Refers to the physical body or the illusions of the material world.</note>; for that Poimandres is pure, the mind of divine power. He revealed no more to me than what is written, believing that I would search out the rest. He urged me toward honorable duties, from which all the powers within me now harmonize. **TAT.** O father, I wish to hear and understand these things. **TRISMEGISTUS.** Be still, my son, be still, I say; you shall now hear the harmonic song, the hymn of <term>regeneration</term> <note>The process of being "born again" into a spiritual, divine nature.</note>, which I would never reveal so easily unless I considered it profitable for you. This is not taught; it is hidden in silence. Look at me, my good son; observe each thing diligently. For it is also fitting for you, while praying under the open sky, to turn your face toward the South when the sun is setting, and toward the East when the sun is rising. Let the entire nature of the world hear this hymn. Hear, O Earth. Hear, O whirlwinds of rain. O forests, be silent: I am about to sing of the creator of all, the whole and the one. Hear, O heavens. Be still, O winds. Let the circle of the immortal God hear this prayer. I sing now of the creator of all, the divider of the lands, the one who balances the sky, commanding the sweet water to flow from the ocean everywhere for the nourishment of men, commanding the fire to shine from above for the actions of men and gods. Let us all give thanks to him with one voice: you who have passed through the heavens and the nature of created things. This is the eye of the mind; he will gladly receive the blessing of the powers. O my powers: praise the one and the all. Harmonize with his pleasure.

<meta>This page continues the "Secret Hymn" from the Hermetic corpus, where the initiate, having undergone a spiritual rebirth, offers a sacrifice of praise to the Creator. It follows the instruction of the teacher (Hermes) to the son (Tat) on how to harmonize with the divine powers.</meta>

all the powers of my soul. Holy knowledge <note>original: "Cognitio sancta," a translation of the Greek "Gnosis."</note>: which shines by your light—through you, singing of the intelligible light <note>Light that is understood by the intellect rather than seen with the eyes.</note>, I exult in the joy of the mind. All powers, sing along with me as one. 

Constancy, sing with me. My Justice: let it sing of the Just through me. My Fellowship: let it praise the Whole itself. Through me, let Truth sing of Truth. 

Our Good: let it sing finally of the Good. O Life, O Light: from you the blessing flows into us. 

I give thanks to you, Father, the act of all powers. I give thanks to you, God, the power of all acts. Your Word <note>original: "Verbum," referring to the divine Logos or creative speech.</note> praises you through me. Through me, the world receives this sacrifice of words. My powers cry out: they sing of the Whole, they fulfill your will. Your will proceeds from you into the Whole. Accept this sacrifice of words from all that I am. 

O Life: save all that is in me. O Light: illuminate the Whole; Spirit, God. It represents the climax of a spiritual rebirth, where the individual identifies their own faculties with the creative powers of God.</summary>

I                                                                                                   26 36

...you will be. **TRISMEGISTUS:** I rejoice, my son, that very many good things have come to you from the truth, which are indeed immortal works. Having learned this from me, proclaim the silence of virtue, communicating the mystery of regeneration <note>The Hermetic "rebirth" into a divine state of mind.</note> to no one, lest we be considered as slanderers. For both of us have meditated sufficiently: I as the teacher, and you as the fellow-learner, since you have known yourself and the Father.

The thirteenth book is about the secret mystery of regeneration, before which no one was saved. The author of this regeneration is the Son of God <note>The editor here is likely identifying the Hermetic "Logos" or "Son of God" with Christ.</note>, man, by the will of the one God. This is a mystery which is not taught; rather, it is venerated with the eyes in silence, and in silence by one's deeds. You, Christian, read the rest. And if you have been reformed in God through the Son of God by a new regeneration (having driven out ignorance, sadness, desire, injustice <note>The original Latin text here reads *iustiticia* (justice), but in the context of the "twelve vices" of the soul described in this chapter, it refers to *iniustitia* (injustice).</note>, luxury, deception, envy, fraud, anger, rashness, and malice—the twelve horrible avengers of darkness—and having attained the ten opposing powers through the piety of God), exult with your whole mind. Remember to give thanks to God the Reformer with all your mental vigor; burn entirely with His love. Having become entirely spirit and reborn of the spirit, sing the hymn of piety together, and keep the purity of regeneration with piety. Turn entirely toward God the Parent and the author of regeneration, His Son, having obtained this fruit from this thirteenth book, or at least having been stirred to think so piously by the goodness of your mind.

¶ **Hermes to Aesculapius: Epilogue.** **XIIII.**

Since my son Tat <note>Original: *Tatius*. Tat is the son and disciple of Hermes in these dialogues.</note> (in your absence) wished to learn the nature of all things, and he himself did not wish me to delay—being a younger man and newly approaching the knowledge of each thing—I was compelled to say many things so that his insight might be easy and ready. I have deemed it worthwhile that, choosing the more important points from many, I should now summarize the preceding discussion for you. For it is permitted to run through certain mysteries with you, as one skilled in the nature of things. All things which are perceived by the senses have been made, and are made daily. They are generated not by themselves, but by another. Many things are generated, each one manifest to the senses; they are different and not at all similar. Whatever things are produced proceed from...

<meta>This page continues a philosophical discourse from the Hermetic tradition, likely a translation by Marsilio Ficino, regarding the necessity of God as the constant creator and the origin of perceived evil in the material world.</meta>

I                                                                 27 37

...in existing as well as in acting. Nothing, however, that is **born** <note>original: "genitum"; referring to anything that comes into being in the physical realm</note> is born of itself. Therefore, it is not made apart from a maker. He who takes away one of these loses the other as well. For one, by its own nature, always regards the other nature. If, therefore, two things are granted—the **effector** and the **effect**—they are indeed united to each other, yet in such a way that one precedes and the other follows. God is surely the preceding effector, while that which follows is the effect. 

Whatever that may be, let no one distrust what we have said, even if terrified by the variety of things, as if the construction of such diverse objects were either too difficult or unworthy of the divine majesty. For the unique glory of God is the constitution of all things; and that is, as it were, the body of God the maker. From the maker himself, surely, comes nothing evil and nothing foul. These "passions" <note>In this context, 'passions' refers to secondary effects or accidental qualities rather than emotions.</note> are things that follow created works, just as rust follows bronze, or as filth <note>original: "limus," literally mud or slime</note> follows living bodies. 

Yet, the blacksmith did not introduce the rust, nor did the parent of a living body introduce the mud and filth. In the same way, God does not cause evil. However, the persistence of **generation** <term>Generation: The process of birth, growth, and constant change in the physical world.</term> causes evil to creep in. For that reason, God instituted **change** in things, acting as a kind of purging of generation itself. 

Furthermore, a painter is permitted to depict the heavens, the earth, the sea, gods, men, animals, trees, and even lifeless things; shall the faculty for doing such things be lacking to God alone? O insane, O blind, and O one inexperienced in all divine knowledge! Nothing is more ridiculous, O **Asclepius**, than what happens to such a man: for while he claims to worship God by "freeing" Him from the business and care of creating, he is utterly ignorant of God himself. And what is worse, he ascribes mortal passions to Him: envy, pride, ignorance, or weakness. For unless He makes all things, He is either proud or rather weak—both of which are wicked thoughts. For God has a unique and proper nature...

<summary>This page argues that God is the inseparable creator of all things and that evil is not a divine creation but a natural byproduct of the material process, similar to rust on bronze. It rebukes those who think God is too "exalted" to be involved in the messy business of creation, calling such views a misunderstanding of divine nature.</summary>
