# The Dialogue of Hermes Trismegistus, Translated by Lucius Apuleius of Madaura, Platonic Philosopher. <note>original: "Mercurij Trismegisti dialogus Lucio Apuleio Madaurensi philosopho platonico Interprete." This text is the *Asclepius*, a famous Hermetic treatise. Historically, it was attributed to the Roman author Apuleius (c. 124–170 AD), though modern scholars debate this attribution.</note>

### I

This Asclepius is a god to me, like the sun. <note>In Hermetic tradition, characters like Asclepius and Ammon are often depicted as disciples of Hermes Trismegistus, representing different levels of spiritual initiation.</note> God has brought you to us, O Asclepius, so that you might take part in a divine discourse, and one of such a nature that, by the merit of all those things previously done by us or inspired in us by divine power, it seems more divine in its religious piety than all others. If you see and understand this, you will be most full in your whole mind of all good things—provided, however, that there truly are many good things, and not just the **One** in which all things are. For one is recognized as being consistent with the other: either all things belong to the One, or the One is all things; for they are so connected to each other that one cannot be separated from the other. 

But regarding the forthcoming discourse: you shall know this through diligent attention. But you, O Asclepius, step forward a little and call for whoever is to be present. Upon entering, Asclepius suggested that **Ammon** also be present. Trismegistus said: "No ill-will forbids Ammon from us, for indeed we remember many things written by us in his name, just as also many things on physics and a great many exotic matters <note>original: "exoticaque." This likely refers to "exoteric" or external teachings meant for a wider audience, as opposed to the "esoteric" or secret teachings discussed here.</note> were written for our most loving and dear son <insert>Tat</insert>." 

"This treatise, however, I shall ascribe to your name. Call no one else besides Ammon, lest this most religious discourse on so great a matter be profaned by the intervention and presence of many coming in. For it is the mark of an irreligious mind to publish to the consciousness of the many a treatise so full of the whole majesty of the divinity." <margin>The four men: Asclepius, Ammon, Mercury [Hermes], and the fourth from chapter 2, Tat.</margin>

When Ammon had also entered the sanctuary, and that holy group of four men was filled with religious awe and the divine presence of God—while with fitting and venerable silence the hearts and minds of each hung upon the words from the mouth of **Hermes**—divine Love <note>original: "Cupido." Here used to signify a divine, longing force for wisdom or the spirit of inspiration.</note> began to speak thus: <margin>Hermu: the Greek genitive [of Hermes].</margin>

**TRISMEGISTUS:** O Asclepius, every human is immortal... <note>The text breaks off here at the start of the primary philosophical instruction.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the 'Asclepius', a foundational Hermetic text, where Hermes Trismegistus prepares to deliver a secret, sacred teaching to a small circle of three disciples: Asclepius, Ammon, and Tat.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius, Ammon, Tat, Hermeticism, Oneness of God, sacred silence, Lucius Apuleius, alchemy of the soul</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the *Asclepius*, a foundational Hermetic text, where Hermes Trismegistus instructs his disciple Asclepius on the nature of God, the soul, and the cosmos.</meta>

is the soul: but not uniformly in all; rather, some in one way, others in another, or at another time. **ASCLEPIUS:** For not every soul, O Trismegistus, is of one and the same quality. **TRISMEGISTUS:** O Asclepius, how quickly you have grasped the true essence of reason! <note>original: "vera rationis continentia" — literally the "true containment of reason," meaning the logical structure of the argument.</note> For I did not say this—that all things are one and one is all—as if all things were simply the same as they were in the Creator before He had created everything. Nor is He undeservedly called "all," for all things are His members. <note>Hermetic philosophy often views the physical universe as the "body" or "members" of God.</note> Therefore, throughout this entire discussion, take care to remember Him who is the One-All, or the Creator of all Himself. All things descend from heaven into the earth, and into the water, and into the air. Only fire, which is carried upwards, is life-giving; that which moves downwards is subservient to it. But indeed, whatever descends from on high is generative; whatever flows upwards is nourishing. The earth alone, standing firm in itself, is the receiver of all things, and the restorer of all kinds which it receives. In this whole, therefore (as you remember), which is "of all" or is "all": the soul and the world, encompassed by nature, are set in motion, thus varied by the multiform equality of all images, so that infinite species of qualities are known at intervals. Joined together, however, for this purpose: that the whole may appear as one, and from the One, all things exist. Therefore, the whole out of which the world is formed consists of four elements: <term>fire, water, earth, and air</term><note>The four classical elements of ancient science.</note>; one world, one soul, and one God. Now, be present with me with all the power of your mind and all the sharpness of your wit. For the reason of divinity—which must be known by a divine focus of the senses—is most like a rushing river, running from the heights downwards with headlong speed. By which it happens that it bypasses the attention not only of those listening but even of those discussing it, with its swift velocity.

<gloss>In the first part, he teaches that all things belong to the One and all things are One. "Of the One" in that all things come from Him; "One" in that all effects are one within their cause. That every human soul is immortal, but that they are diverse...</gloss>

<summary>Hermes explains the unity of the cosmos, arguing that God is "all things" because all things are part of Him, while describing the flow of elements and the nature of the soul. He warns Asclepius that divine logic moves with the speed of a rushing torrent and requires absolute mental focus.</summary>

...perhaps. All things descend from heaven. That which descends provides for generation <note>The process of birth and the creation of physical matter</note>; that which ascends and tends upward provides life alone. This very "One," to whom all things belong and who is all things—namely, the World—moves all the forms of the world. The elements from which the world grew together are fire, air, water, and earth. And just as all bodies constitute the single body of the one world, so too does He <note>The Creator</note> want all the forms of things to constitute one all-encompassing form of the one world, which he calls the Soul of the World <note>original: *animam mundi*</note>. For this reason, the world is "all-bodied" and "all-formed." And these are: one body, one soul, one world, one God. From whom the Divine Reason <note>original: *ratio*; the underlying logic of the universe</note> and the Divine Word <note>original: *verbum*; often identifying with the 'Logos' or the creative speech of God</note>—very much like a rushing river flowing headlong with swift force from the heights to the depths—permeates all things, glides into all things, and flows into all things. And what else, I ask, is this Divine Reason and Divine Word but Divine Wisdom, the craftswoman of all things? She who (as the wise man sings) is "more mobile than all mobile things" <note>A reference to the biblical Book of Wisdom 7:24</note>. And she, though being one, can do all things, and remaining in herself, she makes all things new. This is the first part.

### Chapter II

Heaven, therefore, is the perceptible God, the administrator of all bodies, whose increases and decreases are allotted by the Sun and the Moon. But of Heaven itself, and of its soul, and of all things that are in the world, the Governor is He who is the Maker of all things: God. For from all these aforementioned things—of which He is the Governor of all—a frequent <term>influx</term> <note>original: *influxio*; a term used in astrology and natural philosophy to describe the spiritual or physical "flowing in" of power from the heavens to the earth</note> is carried through the world, and through the soul of all kinds and all species, and through the nature of things.

The world, however, was prepared by God to be the receptacle of all-formed species. By shaping Nature through these species, He brought the world through the four elements up to the heavens. All things are to be pleasing in the sight of God. But all things hanging from above are divided into species in the following way that I am about to describe. The <term>genera</term> <note>original: *genera*; broad categories or "kinds" of things</note> of all things follow their species, such that the totality is the genus and the species is a part of the genus. Thus, there is the genus of gods, the genus of demons <note>original: *demonum*; here referring to "daemons," which in this context are intermediate spirits or guardian entities, not necessarily evil spirits</note>, and likewise of men and of birds. And of all things which the world contains within itself, it generates species similar to itself. There is also another genus of living thing: a genus without senses indeed, yet not lacking a soul <note>Likely referring to plants or vegetative life</note>, from which it enjoys benefits...

<summary>This page outlines the Hermetic view of the universe as a single, divine organism (the World Soul) animated by Divine Wisdom and Reason. It describes how God governs the world through an "influx" of power, categorizing all existence into a hierarchy of genera and species.</summary>

joined to the gods by divine religion, he approaches the demons; he who is joined to them is of a human nature. However, those who are content with the moderation of their own kind and the rest of the species of men will be like those to whose species and kinds they have joined themselves.

In the second chapter. For the help of the present understanding and of certain following points, it should by no means be hidden that Mercury <note>The author refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many mystical and philosophical works were attributed.</note> uses the word **animal** <note>original: "animalis"</note> with a far different meaning than we are accustomed to using it; likewise with the word **soul** <note>original: "anima"</note>. For from the second dialogue of the *Pimander* <note>The *Pimander* (or *Poimandres*) is the first and most famous text in the *Corpus Hermeticum*, a collection of Greco-Egyptian wisdom texts.</note>, he defines "soul" by motion. However, in the following chapter, he defines "animal" by "soul and body." Therefore, whatever has a motor force, by the observation of his own way of speaking, has a soul; whatever has both a soul and a body is an "animal." Therefore, the sky, the world, plants, and the elements are "animals."

But our custom is to call only that an **animal** which is an animated body endowed with sense. And we define "soul" not only by motion, but by life, sense, voluntary motion, or intellect. Therefore, when we hear "animal," let us understand it not according to our way, but according to his.

But now to the dialogue. He argues that the heaven and the celestial body relate to other sensible things as man relates to other animals. Moreover, man is to other animals as an **animate god** <note>original: "animalis deus"</note>; but the heaven is to other sensible things as a **sensible god**. But I would believe that God, the ruler of all and the governor of all things that are in the world, is nothing other than God providing for all and dispensing all things through genera and species, for all of which the world is a receptacle. And to each thing, God imparts some duty as to a fit instrument: like the sun and the moon, which are organs of divinity for the rising and setting of things, and for the increase and decrease of things. And He dispenses men through angels, but beasts through men.

But truly, regarding what he says about demons—that species are not formed without their help, and that some have clung to the divine race and, by proximity and fellowship, were held to be similar to the gods, while others persisted in the quality of their own kind, loving human reason—we know from Sacred Scripture <note>original: "sacris litteris"</note> that the angels who did not keep their own principality left their own home and are reserved in eternal chains under darkness for the judgment of the great God <note>A reference to the biblical Book of Jude 1:6.</note>. Even if these beings pretend to be lovers of men, they love them very little; instead, they drag them toward precipices, which they have done from the beginning. For pretending to love, when they brought death to men, they said: "You shall by no means die, but you shall be like gods, knowing good and evil" <note>A reference to the serpent's temptation of Eve in Genesis 3:4-5.</note>. Therefore, the things he says here about demons do not seem to be able to be drawn to a pious understanding quite conveniently, but...

<summary>This page explains that the philosopher Hermes Trismegistus defines "animal" and "soul" differently than common usage, including the heavens and plants as "animals." It also attempts to reconcile Hermetic teachings about demons with Christian scripture regarding fallen angels.</summary>

HERMES <note>original: "MER.", an abbreviation for Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage to whom these dialogues are attributed.</note>

...to follow the error of the nations. But concerning those men who cling to God, we frankly confess that they escape the divine <note>The text suggests they transcend ordinary human limits to become god-like</note>, while those who are received into the lot of demons are joined to demons, who (as has been said) are reserved in eternal bonds for the judgment of the great God. For we know it has been pronounced from the mouth of God, that he will say in the judgment to those on the left hand: "Depart from me, you cursed ones, into the eternal fire which is prepared for the devil and his angels." <note>This is a direct citation of the Gospel of Matthew 25:41, showing how Renaissance translators often blended Hermetic philosophy with Christian scripture.</note> But who will those be, if not those who adhered to demons in this world? ->Chapter III<-

**P**ecause of this, O Asclepius, **man is a great miracle**, an animal to be adored and honored. For he passes into the nature of God as if he himself were a god. He knows the <term>race of demons</term><note>In this context, 'demons' (daemones) refers to intermediary spirits or supernatural beings, not necessarily evil 'demons' in the modern sense.</note>, inasmuch as he recognizes that he originated from the same source. He despises the part of human nature within himself, placing his confidence in the divinity of his other part. Oh, how happily tempered is the nature of men, and kin to the gods! Joined to divinity, he despises that part of himself by which he is earthly. All other things to which he knows he is necessary by celestial disposition, he binds to himself with a knot of love. Thus he gazes up to heaven. 

Thus he is placed in the happier position of the middle way: so that he may love those things that are below him, and be loved by those who are above him. He tills the earth. He mingles with the elements through his speed; by the sharpness of his mind, he descends into the depths of the sea. All things are clear to him; heaven does not seem too high, for he gazes upon it with the <term>sagacity</term><note>Keen mental discernment and sound judgment.</note> of his soul as if from nearby. No fog of the air confuses the focus of his mind. No density of the earth hinders his work. No deep altitude of the water blunts his downward gaze. He is all things, and he is everywhere the same. 

Of all these kinds of living beings, they have roots reaching from the top downward <note>This refers to the "world-tree" or "heavenly plant" concept, where divine beings or souls are rooted in the heavens and grow toward the earth.</note>. Those living beings of the earth, however, grow wild from the bottom to the top from a single root. Some are nourished by double elements, others by simple ones. There are two types of nourishment—for the soul and for the body—of which these beings consist. The soul of the world is nourished by a restless, constant agitation. The bodily part is nourished from water...

It also links the fate of the soul to its earthly attachments, using Christian-influenced imagery of judgment.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the sage Hermes Trismegistus and his disciple Asclepius, specifically regarding the hierarchy of being and the unique composition of humanity.</meta>

and the earth of the lower world: they grow by nutrients. Spirit <note>original: "Spiritus", here meaning the "pneuma" or life-force that permeates the cosmos</note> (with which all things are full) is mixed with everything: it gives life to all things, with sense added for human understanding, which is the **fifth part** <note>The "fifth part" refers to the "ether" or the "quintessence," a celestial element beyond the four earthly elements (earth, air, fire, water) that enables higher thought.</note> alone granted to man from the ether. But out of all living beings: it adorns, raises up, and supports only the human senses toward the understanding of divine reason. But since I am prompted to speak about sense: a little later I will also explain its logic to you. For it is most holy and great: and not less than that which belongs to divinity itself. 

But now I will set forth for you what I had begun. For I was speaking at the very beginning of things about the communion of the gods: which humans alone (by their favor) enjoy. Truly, those of men whoever have attained such great happiness: that they perceive that divine sense of understanding—which sense is more divine [found] in God alone and in human intelligence. 

**ASCLEPIUS:** For the sense of all men, O Trismegistus, is not uniform. 

**TRISMEGISTUS:** Not everyone, O Asclepius, has attained true understanding. But following an image with a reckless impulse—with no true reason examined—they are deceived. This produces malice in their minds and transforms the best of animals <note>Hermetic texts often refer to man as the "best animal" because of his potential for divinity.</note> into the nature of a wild beast and the character of monsters. However, regarding sense and all similar things: when I speak of spirit, I will then provide you with the whole explanation. 

For man alone is a dual living being. And one part of him is simple, which as the Greeks say is <term>essential</term> <note>original: "ουσιωΔ ησ" (ousiōdēs). In this context, it refers to the spiritual essence that does not change.</note>; or what we call the form of divine likeness. But there is a fourfold part which the Greeks call <term>worldly</term> <note>original: "κοσμικον" (kosmikon). This refers to the material world composed of the four elements.</note>, and we call mundane. From this the body is made: by which that which we have already said is divine in man is covered. In which the divinity of the mind, covered alone, with its kindred—that is, the senses of the pure mind—rests within itself, as if enclosed by the wall of the body.
                                                                                               fi

<summary>Hermes Trismegistus explains that humans are unique because they possess a divine fifth element (intellect) that allows them to commune with the gods, though many fall into beast-like behavior by lacking true understanding. He describes the human as a dual being: one part divine essence and the other a fourfold mundane body that acts as a container for the mind.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius, dualism, divine intellect, ether, ousiōdēs, kosmikon, spirit, pneuma, human nature</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
MER.
The **third chapter** extols the dignity of man. Within this section, the Creator—the author of such great benefits—must be acknowledged above all others and pursued with immense love and "winged" thanksgivings. For it is He who has distinguished humanity with such great and excellent gifts. 

For, as He wills, man was created so that he might pass into a divine nature, having knowledge of the race of angels and demons <note>original: *demonum*, referring here to spirits or intermediary beings rather than necessarily "evil" entities</note>. He is born with the angels, whom he has from the very beginning of his birth as his presiding protectors and guardians. He has obtained a nature akin to the immortals and is marked with the character of God. Man is composed of both a mortal and an immortal part—an earthly and a <term>supramundane|beyond the physical world</term> particle. 

Truly, he who is not ignorant of these gifts of divinity despises his earthly part. Instead, he places his trust in his immortal and supramundane side and sighs toward heaven. As one who belongs there in the better part of himself, he feels all things in that realm to be his kin. 

Yet, placed in the middle of the world, he binds the rest of creation—to which he knows himself to be necessary by divine ordination—to himself in a bond of charity. Thus, by loving those things that are below him, he may in turn be loved by those who are above him.

He cultivates the earth; he mingles with the elements with the speed of thought. With the sharpness of his mind, he descends into the deeps. All things shine clearly for him. Heaven does not seem too high, for he gazes upon it as if from close by through the sagacity of his spirit. The gloom of the air does not confuse his mental focus; the density of the earth does not hinder his work; the profound depth of the water does not dull his downward gaze. 

Out of all living creatures, God adorns, raises, and uplifts only human senses to the understanding of divine reason. Indeed, intelligence alone is the part of man that has obtained an ethereal, immortal, and supramundane nature. 

Yet, while some men have attained this divine sense—and happy indeed are they who are continually carried toward God with a pious intention of mind—others possess only a worldly and sensual image. This is a mere shadow <note>original: *umbratilem*, literally "shadow-like"</note> of true and divine intelligence, and those who follow it wander in error. For this shadowing of divine intelligence breeds malice in the minds of those who follow it; it transforms man—who is by nature the best and a <note>original: *divinum animal*, meaning a "divine living being" or "divine creature"</note> divine animal—into something resembling the nature and habits of beasts. 

This is the summary of the third part.

### Chapter 4

**ASCLEPIUS:** Why then, O Trismegistus, was it necessary for man to be established in the world, and not remain in that realm where God is, since he might have lived there in the highest blessedness?

**TRISMEGISTUS:** You ask rightly, O Asclepius. For we too ask God to grant us the ability to give an account...

It warns that while humans are "divine animals," failing to use true intelligence reduces them to a beast-like state.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Asclepius," a dialogue between the sage Hermes Trismegistus and his disciple Asclepius, focusing on the hierarchy of creation and the unique dual nature of humanity.</meta>

## II 42

...of that [creation]. For since all things depend on his will, then especially those things which are treated concerning the highest summit <note>The "summit" refers to the highest point of divinity or the supreme principle of the universe.</note> which is the reason we seek in the present discussion. Hear then, Asclepius. The Lord and Shaper of all, whom we rightly call God, when he made from himself a second [god] who could be seen and perceived—I call this second one "sensible" <note>original: "sensibilem." In this philosophical context, "sensible" means "capable of being perceived by the senses," referring to the physical Universe as a living, divine being.</note> not because he himself perceives (for whether he perceives or not, we shall discuss at another time), but because he strikes the senses of those who see him. Since, therefore, he made him first from himself, and second from himself, and he appeared beautiful to him, as one who is most full of all goodness: he loved him as a part of his own divinity. 

Therefore, because he was so great and so good, he willed another who would be able to behold that [god] whom he had made from himself; and at the same time, to be an imitator of his reason and care, he made Man. For the will of God is itself the highest perfection, seeing as he completes the willing and the perfecting in one and the same moment of time. When, therefore, God noticed that the essential soul <note>original: "ουσιωΔη" (ousiōdē), meaning substantial or essential nature.</note> could not be mindful of all things unless he covered it with a worldly garment, he clothed it with a bodily home. 

And he commanded all such to be of both natures, blending and mixing them into one, as much as was necessary. Thus he shaped man from the nature of soul and body—that is, from the eternal and the mortal—so that a living being so shaped could satisfy both his origins: both to wonder at and pray to celestial and eternal things, and to cultivate and govern earthly things. Now, however, I call "mortal" things not water and earth—which are two of the four elements nature subjected to humans—but those things which are made by humans either in or from these elements: such as the cultivation of the earth itself, pastures, building, ports, navigation, communication, and mutual services, which is the human...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue of the Asclepius, a foundational text of Hermeticism, focusing on the relationship between God, the world (the "second god"), and the unique role of humanity in maintaining the cosmos.</meta>

MER.

...a very firm bond of unity among themselves. A part of the world consists of water and earth; this earthly part of the world is preserved by the knowledge and use of arts and disciplines, without which God did not wish the world to be perfect. For necessity follows the things that please God, and the effect accompanies his will. For it is not believable that what once pleased God will ever displease him, since he knew long before both that it would exist and that it would please him.

The fourth chapter <note>original: "Vartum caput," an OCR error for "Quartum caput"</note> discusses why God established man in the world rather than in the <term>region of the intelligible</term> <note>original: "intelligibilium regione"; the realm of pure, non-physical ideas or spirits</note>, and the discussion is clear. It also explains why he tempered man from both natures—mortal and immortal—and why he interwove the form of divine likeness (the mind, which he created according to his own likeness and image) with a worldly and corporeal covering. It shows that the will of God is the supreme perfection of things, which necessity follows, while the effect accompanies that necessity. For God completes both the willing and the perfecting at one and the same moment in time. He calls the world a "second god" <note>original: "mundum secundum deum"</note> both here and in the sixth chapter. This is just as if you were to call <term>duality</term> <note>original: "duitatem"</note> a "second unity." For duality is a type of "one"—it is a single pair—but the <term>monad</term> <note>original: "monas"; the ultimate, indivisible One or the Source</note> is absolutely one. Duality, however, is not absolutely one, but is "one" through participation and contraction—a "second one." In the same way, God is one, being absolutely God; but the world is not God, but is "god" through participation, and is the most beautiful footprint of God among sensible things. These points are for the fourth part of the *Asclepius*.

### Chapter V

But O Asclepius, I notice that with a swift desire of mind you hasten to hear how man can have a devotion to or worship of heaven and those things that are in it. Hear then, O Asclepius: the worship of the God of heaven, along with all things within it, is the frequent performance of reverent service. No other animal performs this—neither the divine <note>referring to celestial beings or stars</note> nor the bestial—except man alone. For heaven and the heavenly beings are delighted by the admiration, adorations, praises, and services of men. And not without reason has the chorus of the Muses <note>the nine goddesses of inspiration in arts and sciences</note> been sent down from the highest divinity into the assembly of men, specifically so that the earthly world...

<meta>This page continues the Hermetic dialogue known as the Asclepius. The previous section likely discussed the divine origin of the world and man’s unique position within it. Here, the text transitions from a discussion on the spiritual purpose of music to a hierarchical breakdown of the universe: God, the World, and Man.</meta>

...it would seem more unrefined if it lacked the sweetness of melodies. But rather, so that he who alone is all things—or the Father of all—might be celebrated with the rhythmic songs of men. In this way, through celestial praises, the sweetness of harmony would not be lacking even on earth. For some—indeed, a very few—who are endowed with a pure mind, have been allotted the more venerable task of looking up toward heaven. However, those who, due to the confusion of their dual nature, have had their inner intelligence stifled by the weight of the body, are instead assigned to the care of the elements and these lower realms. 

Therefore, man is a living being <note>original: "animal"</note> who is not lesser simply because he is partly mortal; rather, he is perhaps more fitly and effectively composed for that very reason, appearing to have been granted mortality for a specific purpose. Namely, because he could not have sustained the care of both realms unless he were formed from both natures. He was created from both so that he might have the capacity to cherish both the earthly and the divine. 

Now, Asclepius, I desire you to grasp the reasoning of this treatise, not only with sharp attention but also with a liveliness of soul. For this reasoning is unbelievable to many, yet it is perceived as whole and true by holier minds. And so, here I shall begin.

<gloss>The fifth chapter <note>original: "Quintum caput"</note> teaches that the sweetness of music was granted to humans for singing the praises of God and the celestial beings. We know that the prophets, full of the divine spirit, commanded this when they instructed us to rejoice, to sing psalms to God, and to praise the Lord with the timbrel, in the dance, with strings, and upon the organ. For this is the highest purpose of the Muses and of all music. The literal meaning <note>original: "Littera", referring to the plain sense of the text</note> is clear in itself.</gloss>

### Chapter VI

**The Lord of Eternity, God, is first; the world is second; man is third.** God is the creator of the world and of all things within it, governing all things together, which are arranged with man himself acting as a governor. Man takes up the whole of this duty—that is, the care provided by his own diligence—so that he and the world may each be a beauty and an ornament to the other. Thus, because of this divine composition of man, the world is more rightly called <term>kosmos<note>original Greek: "κοσμοσ". This term implies both the "universe" and "beauty" or "order."</note></term> in the Greek tongue. He knows himself; he knows...

...and the world: namely, that he should remember what is appropriate for his own parts. He should recognize which things he ought to use and which things he ought to serve, offering the greatest praises and thanks to God. He venerates God's image, not unaware that he himself is also the **second image of God**. <note>In Hermetic philosophy, God is the first being, the Universe (Cosmos) is the first image of God, and Man is the second image, or a "microcosm" reflecting the whole.</note> Of God, there are two images: namely, the world and man. From this it follows that, since man is a single composite belonging to Him, he is divine in that part consisting of soul, sense, <term>spirit<note>original: "spiritu"; here referring to 'pneuma', the vital breath or ethereal substance that links the physical body to the rational soul.</note></term>, and reason. Thus, he seems able to ascend into heaven, as if arising from the higher elements.

However, in his worldly part—which consists of fire, water, air, and earth—he remains mortal upon the earth, so that he may eventually leave all worldly things behind, widowed and deserted by his nature. For in this way, **humanity** was made partly divine and partly mortal, while existing in a physical body.

Moreover, the measure of both these parts—that is, of man—is, above all, religion, which is followed by goodness. Goodness finally seems to be perfected only if it is fortified by the virtue of despising all desires for foreign things. For all things possessed through earthly, bodily desire are foreign to all parts of the divine thought. These are rightly called by the name "possessions," because they were not born with us, but were received by us to be possessed at a later time.

Therefore, all things of this kind are foreign to man—even the body. Thus, we should despise both the things we desire and the very part of us from which the vice of desire arises. For as the focus of reason leads the soul, man ought to exist for this purpose: that through the contemplation of divinity, he may despise and look down upon that mortal part joined to him (which he must keep only due to the necessity of serving the <term>lower world<note>original: "mundi interioris"; likely a variation or transcription of 'mundi inferioris', referring to the material, sublunary realm.</note></term>).

For so that man might be most complete in both parts, observe that he was formed from four elements of each part: with two hands and two feet, and other members of the body, by which...

...serves the lower <note>original: "inferiori", referring to the material or earthly realm</note> (that is, the terrestrial) world, but with those four parts: sense, mind, memory, and foresight. By reason of these, he may know and look up to all divine things. Whence it happens that man pursues the diversities, qualities, effects, and quantities of things with a probing investigation. But being delayed by the heavy and excessive vice <note>In this context, "vice" refers to the physical weight or limitations of the material body.</note> of the body, he cannot properly perceive these causes of the nature of things, which truly are. 

Therefore, with man having been thus made and shaped, and placed in charge of such ministry and service by the supreme God—and who, by properly keeping the world clean, by piously worshiping God, and by worthily and competently obeying the will of God in both—with what reward do you believe such a person should be rewarded? 

(For since the world is the work of God, he who preserves and increases its beauty with diligence, and joins his own labor with the will of God—since he arranges the appearance which He formed with divine intention by the aid of his body, with daily work and care) 

—unless it be that reward with which our ancestors were rewarded: by which also we ourselves hope to be rewarded with most pious vows, if it shall please the divine mercy. That is, that having completed our service and being stripped of this worldly guardianship <note>The Latin "mundana custodia" suggests life is a period of "guard duty" or a "watch" that one eventually finishes.</note>, and being released from mortal bonds, He might restore us—pure and holy—to the nature of our higher part, which is the divine part.

**ASCLEPIUS.** You speak justly and truly, O Trismegistus. For this is the reward for those living piously under God and diligently with the world. As this reasoned discourse has proceeded, O Trismegistus, souls are endangered in this worldly life by the hope of future eternity. But to some this seems incredible, to others a fable; to others perhaps it may seem a thing to be mocked. For it is a sweet thing in this bodily life to be captivated by possessions...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus (referred to here as Mercurius) and his disciple Asclepius. The previous page ended with a question about the reward or "fruit" for a human who faithfully serves as the caretaker of the world and a worshiper of the Divine.</meta>

MER.
...the fruit. Wherefore it <note>Referring to the "vice" or material weight of the body mentioned on the previous page.</note> detains the soul by the neck (as the saying goes) <note>original: "obtorto collo," a Latin idiom meaning to drag someone by force or against their will.</note>, so that it clings to that part of itself which is mortal, nor does a malice envying immortality allow the soul to recognize its divine part. For I tell you as if by prophecy: after us, no one shall have that simple delight <note>The Latin "delectum" can mean a choice or a delight; in this context, it refers to the pure pursuit of wisdom.</note> which belongs to philosophy. Philosophy consists solely in a frequent gaze upon the divinity and in holy religion to know it. Yet many shall confound philosophy with manifold reasoning. 

How then do many affect <note>In the sense of "disturb" or "corrupt."</note> this incomprehensible philosophy, or in what way do they confound it with manifold reasoning?

TRISME. O Asclepius, in this way: by mixing it, through cunning commentary, with various disciplines that are not easily understood—Arithmetic, Music, and Geometry. But for one practicing pure philosophy, which depends only on divine religion, it is necessary to attend to these other sciences only so far as to marvel at how the restorations of the stars <note>original: "apocatastases," referring to the astronomical cycles where celestial bodies return to their original positions.</note>, their fixed stations, and the course of their changes consist of numbers. 

As he comes to know the dimensions, qualities, and quantities of the earth, the depths of the sea, the power of fire, and the effects and nature of all these things, let him marvel, adore, and praise the divine art and mind. Truly, to know music is nothing else than to understand the order of all things and what the divine reason has allotted to each. For the order of individual things, brought together into one by a masterly reason, will produce a certain harmony—a divine melody most sweet-sounding and most true.

ASCLE. What then will men be like after us? 

TRISME. Deceived by the cunning of sophists <note>Sophists were teachers who used clever, often deceptive, rhetorical arguments rather than seeking the truth.</note>, they shall be turned away from true, pure, and holy philosophy. For to worship the divinity with a simple mind and soul, to venerate His works, and to give thanks for the will of God (which alone is most full of goodness)—this is philosophy, unviolated by any troublesome curiosity of the mind. And let us have treated these matters thus far.

<summary>Hermes Trismegistus warns Asclepius that true philosophy, defined as the pure contemplation of God, will eventually be corrupted by over-intellectualized sciences and sophistry. He explains that while subjects like music and geometry are useful for admiring the divine order of the cosmos, they should not distract from simple devotion and gratitude toward the Creator.</summary>

          ->The sixth chapter discusses: God is absolutely the first God.<-
<margin>f</margin> The world: the second god, not absolutely but by participation <note>original: "participatione." In Hermetic and Platonic thought, this means the world shares in or reflects the qualities of the Divine without being the source itself.</note>, as it is the first image of the absolute God. Man: the third god by participation in divinity, and the second image of God.
God, however, is not an image, but the Truth of all images. Man: through soul, sense, spirit, and reason—as if by higher elements—is able to ascend into heaven. But because he is composed <note>original: "coalitus," meaning joined together or fused.</note> of fire, air, water, and earth, he is subject to death; he must leave all mundane things to the earth once he is deprived of that divine part. The measure according to which man ought to live is religion, which goodness follows. This is seen to be perfect at last when, fortified by virtue, he despises the desires for all foreign things <note>Meaning things external to the soul, such as material wealth.</note>—possessions, the body, and all those things we crave—and finally even the very sense of longing. For man ought to be called such and exist only so far (as the author's opinion holds) as the intention of reason leads the mind.

It emphasizes that while Man's body is bound to the physical elements and death, his reason and spirit allow him to transcend the material world through "pure philosophy" and religion.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue of Mercurius (Hermes Trismegistus), further detailing the metaphysical nature of God and the World. It follows the previous page’s establishment of a hierarchy (God, World, Man) by defining the concept of "unborn" and the inherent fertility of both Divine and material nature.</meta>

MER.
...but they were already in Him even then: from where they were destined to originate. For those things are not called "unborn" only which are not yet born, but rather those which lack the fertility of generating, so that nothing can be born from them. Therefore, whatever things exist in which the nature of generating is present: these are both "generable" <note>Capable of producing or being produced.</note> and things which can be born from them, even if they themselves were born from their own nature. For there is no doubt that from those things which are self-born, those things from which all things originate can easily be born.

God, therefore, is sempiternal; God is eternal. He cannot be born, nor could He have been. He is, He was, He shall always be. This, then, is the nature of God: that which is entirely of itself. Matter <term>Hyle</term> <note>original: "Hyle." A Greek term used by scholars to describe the fundamental "stuff" or raw substance of the universe before it is given shape.</note>, however—or the nature of the world—and Spirit: although they may seem to have been born from a beginning, they nevertheless possess within themselves the power of being born and procreating, and the nature of fertility. For indeed, the beginning lies in the quality of nature, which possesses within itself the power and nature of both conception and birth. This nature, therefore, is capable of generating on its own, without needing conception from an external source. 

But indeed, those things which have only the power of conceiving through the mingling of another nature must be distinguished in this way: that this "place" of the world, with those things which are within it, appears to be "unborn," for it certainly holds within itself the power of the whole of nature. And I say "place" to mean that in which all things exist. For all these things could not have existed if a space were lacking which could sustain them all. Indeed, for all things that have existed, a place must be provided. For neither qualities, nor quantities, nor positions, nor effects could be identified in things that exist nowhere. 

Thus, even the world—although it was not born in a conventional sense—nevertheless contains within itself the nature of all things, since it provides the most fertile wombs <note>original: "sinus," meaning folds, pockets, or bosoms, used here as a metaphor for the generative capacity of the physical world.</note> for all things to be conceived. This, therefore, is the totality of quality and of matter which is "creatable," even if it has not been created. For just as nature is a fertile source of quality, so too is it equally fertile in producing malice <note>original: "malignitatis." In Hermetic thought, matter is often seen as the source of "evil" or disorder because it is the furthest from the divine spirit.</note>. Nor did I say, oh Asclepius...

<summary>Mercurius explains that God is eternal and self-existent, while Matter (Hyle) and the World possess an inherent, "unborn" fertility that allows for the constant generation of all things. He emphasizes that space or "place" is a necessary prerequisite for any quality or object to exist.</summary>

...Asclepius and Ammon (as is said by many), God could not cut away or turn aside malice <note>The original term "maliciam" refers to evil, vice, or inherent flaws in the material world.</note> from the nature of things. To these people, no answer should be given at all. However, for your sake, I will pursue what I began and provide an explanation. For they themselves say that God ought to have freed the world from malice in every way; for malice is in the world in such a way that it seems to be like a limb of the world itself. 

Yet, provision and caution were taken (as far as could reasonably be done) by the Highest God: at that time when He deigned to gift the minds of humans with sense, learning, and intelligence. By these things, in which we excel other animals, we alone are able to avoid the frauds, deceits, and vices of malice. For he who avoids these things by looking ahead before being entangled in them is a human fortified by divine intelligence and prudence. For the foundation of learning consists in the highest goodness. 

Moreover, all things in the world are served and given life by the Spirit; and like a tool or a machine, the world is subject to the will of the Highest God. Therefore, let us understand that the Highest Being is intelligible <note>Meaning "knowable only through the intellect," rather than the physical senses.</note> by the mind alone: He who is called God, the ruler and governor of the sensible god <note>original: "sensibilis dei." In Hermetic philosophy, the physical Universe/World is often called the "second god" or "sensible god" because it is a visible reflection of the divine.</note>—that is, the one who embraces within himself every place, every substance of things, and the entire matter of things that generate and create, and everything whatever it is, however great it is. 

By the Spirit, however, all species in the world are moved and governed: each one according to its own nature distributed to it by God. Now, Matter <term>Hyle</term> <note>original: "Hyle." The raw, unformed substance of the universe.</note> or the world is the receptacle of all things, and the movement and frequenting of all things; God is the governor of these, dispensing to all worldly things as much as is necessary for each. With Spirit, indeed, He fills all things, according to the quality of each nature. For the world’s roundness is hollow like a sphere: it is its own cause of quality or form. It is entirely invisible in its totality...

<summary>Hermes explains that God did not remove evil from the world but instead gave humans intelligence and prudence to overcome it. He describes the cosmos as a "sensible god" governed by the Highest God through the medium of the Spirit.</summary>

<keywords>problem of evil, malice, divine intelligence, sensible god, Hyle, Spirit, cosmology, Asclepius, providence</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus (Mercurius) and his student Asclepius. Having just discussed how God provides "sense" and "intelligence" to humans to avoid the evils of the material world, Hermes now explains the nature of the "invisible" foundations of the sphere and the specific divine gift of the "Sense" (Sensus) that illuminates the human mind, distinguishing it from the physical sun.</meta>

MER.
If you were to choose a place at the very top to look down, you would not be able to see what is at the bottom from there. Because of this, it is believed in many places to have a likeness and quality only through the shapes of the species <note>The author is discussing the "forms" or "archetypes" that give shape to the material world.</note> whose images appear to be engraved upon it. It is believed to be visible, as if shown in a painting, but in truth, it is always invisible to itself. For this reason, its bottom or part (if it is a place) within the sphere is called **Hades** <note>original Greek: "ades". The text provides a folk etymology, linking Hades to the Greek word for "unseen."</note> in Greek. Since the Greek word for "to see" <note>original Greek: "eidein"</note> means to perceive with sight, the bottom of the sphere lacks this sight. From this, the "forms" or "species" are called **eide** <note>original Greek: "eide", which means both "forms" and "things seen."</note>, because they are forms of the invisible. Therefore, because they are deprived of sight, they are called **Hades** in Greek; and because they are at the bottom of the sphere, they are called the **Underworld** <note>original Latin: "inferi", referring to the lower regions.</note> in Latin. These, then, are the principles—the more ancient things, and the beginnings or heads of all that exist: either within them, through them, or from them.

ASCLE.
Are all these worldly things then (as you say, O Trismegistus) present within every single species just as they are, in their total substance?

TRISME.
The world nourishes bodies; the spirit <note>original: "spiritus". In this context, it refers to the "pneuma" or vital breath that connects soul and body.</note> nourishes souls. But **sense** <note>original: "sensus". Hermes uses this term here not for physical touch or hearing, but for a divine, intuitive faculty of understanding or "Intellect."</note> (a celestial gift by which humanity alone is blessed) nourishes the mind. Not all humans, however, possess this, but only the few whose minds are such that they are capable of receiving so great a benefit. For just as the world is brightened by the sun, so the human mind shines with that light—and even more so. For whatever the sun illuminates is sometimes deprived of its light when the earth or the moon comes between them and night intervenes. But once **sense** has been mixed with the human soul, it becomes a single nature through a well-bonded mixture, so that such minds are never hindered by the errors of darkness. For this reason, they have rightly said that the senses are the souls of the gods. I, however, do not say this of all of them, but only of the greatest and primary ones.

<summary>Hermes explains the etymology of Hades as the "invisible" part of the cosmic sphere and describes "Sense" as a divine light that, unlike the sun, never fails the human mind once it is unified with the soul.</summary>

<keywords>Hades, species, forms, Underworld, Sense, Sensus, Intellect, divine light, sun metaphor, souls of gods, Trismegistus, Asclepius</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of Chapter 7 and begins Chapter 8 of the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and Asclepius. The previous section concluded by defining the "underworld" (Hades) as the invisible foundations of the world. Now, the text summarizes the philosophical nature of matter and the divine "Word," before Hermes begins a deeper revelation regarding the different classes of gods and the "heads" or origins of all things.</meta>

->II                                                                 47<-

**c**       In the seventh section, he contains a veiled philosophy concerning the genesis of the world, concerning **Hyle** <note>original Latin: "Hvle", from the Greek word for "matter."</note>—the world's raw material—the accompanying spirit, and the nature of "place." He asserts that all worldly things exist within the world, and the world exists within God. God, being the container, is what he calls "place." Furthermore, he does not wish the "place" of the world to be considered "born" <note>meaning "created" or "having a beginning."</note>; however, he says the world itself is both born and not born. It is indeed born if you look at the craftsmanship of the creatures within it; but it is not born if you look at the essential truth of that craftsmanship. For **Hyle** and the spirit—that is, the world and a certain divine force flowing through and diffused across all things—were not present in the making of the world when they were not yet "born." But they were (he says) already present even then, from the source where they were destined to be born. But in what, then, did they exist, if not in that **Divine Word** <note>original Latin: "divino illo verbo". This refers to the "Logos," the creative principle through which the universe was ordered.</note> through which all things were made? In him, that which was made was life; and from him, finally, whatever has been made came forth. Matter (**Hyle**) is equally fertile in that quality which holds the nature of good, and equally secondary to malice <note>or "evil."</note>; it is equally capable of both good and evil. Just as the same earth is fertile for both healing and harmful herbs, and the earth possesses this capacity as a part of its own nature, so too does Matter (**Hyle**). Nevertheless, God has provided for humans (as much as was reasonably necessary) sense, discipline, and intelligence to guard against this material nature, so that malice might not occupy our own "matter" like a harmful weed in a deserted, neglected, and uncultivated field. Furthermore, he discusses a few things regarding the spirit diffused into all things, the underworld, and the "Sense"—which is the gliding of higher minds into human minds, like rays of light entering the eyes. This concludes the seventh part.

### Chapter VIII

**a**       ASCLEPIUS: Whom do you call the "heads of things" or the "original beginnings," O Trismegistus? TRISMEGISTUS: I am opening great things to you and baring divine mysteries. I begin this matter with the sought-after favor of heaven. There are many kinds of gods; and of all of them, one part is **intelligible** <note>perceived by the mind/intellect rather than the senses.</note> and another part is **sensible** <note>perceived by the physical senses.</note>. They are called "intelligible" not because they are thought to be beyond the reach of our senses (for we actually feel them *more* than those we call visible), as this discussion will teach, and as you will be able to see clearly if you pay attention. For this sublime reason—and therefore more divine, standing beyond human minds and intentions—will fly past you and flow away, or rather flow back, if you do not receive the words of the speakers with a more attentive obedience of the ears.

<summary>This page summarizes the Hermetic view of matter (Hyle) as a neutral vessel for both good and evil and introduces the distinction between "sensible" gods (visible) and "intelligible" gods (perceived by the mind).</summary>
<keywords>Hyle, Matter, Divine Word, Logos, Good and Evil, Intelligible Gods, Sensible Gods, Asclepius, Hermes Trismegistus</keywords>

...and it shall mix itself with the liquids of the fountain. There are, therefore, gods who are the heads of all species. These are followed by the gods whose prince is **Essence** <note>original: "ουσια" (ousia). In this context, it refers to the fundamental substance or "being" of a thing.</note>. These are perceptible to the senses and resemble both origins; they complete all things through their sensible nature, each one illuminating his own work through the other.

Of heaven—or whatever is contained by that name—the **Ruler of Essence** <term>ousiarches: a Greek term meaning "ruler of substance" or "head of being."</term> is Jupiter. For through heaven, Jupiter provides life to all things. The Ruler of Essence of the sun is **Light**. For the benefit of light is poured into us through the sun's orb.

Concerning the thirty-six <note>Known as the "Decans" in Egyptian and Hermetic astrology, these are 36 spirits who rule over ten-degree segments of the zodiac.</note> (whose name is "the Horoscopes") among the stars that are always fixed in the same place: their Ruler of Essence or prince is the one they call **Pantomorphos** <note>original: "Pantomorphon", meaning "the All-Formed One."</note>, or the "One of All Forms." It is he who creates different forms for different species.

The seven spheres which are called "erratic" <note>The "erratic" stars are the planets (Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, plus the Sun and Moon), so-called because they appear to move against the background of the "fixed" stars.</note> have their own Rulers of Essence—that is, their own princes—whom they call **Fortune** or **Fate** <note>original: "ειμαρμενηρ" (heimarmene). This represents the rigid, cosmic law of cause and effect.</note>, by whom all things are changed by the law of nature with the firmest stability, varied by eternal motion.

**Air**, indeed, is the instrument or the engine of all, through which all things are done. However, the Ruler of Essence of this air is the "second" one. It provides mortal things to mortals and things similar to them.

Because these things are so, moving from the lowest to the highest, all things are naturally connected to one another: so that mortal things are linked to mortals, and things perceived by the senses are linked to the sensible. But the peak of governance obeys that supreme Lord, so that they are not many, but rather **One**. For all things hang from the One, or rather flow from Him; when they are seen from a distance, they are believed to be many separate things. But joined together, they are one, or rather two: the source from which all things are made, and the one by whom they are made. That is, they consist of the **matter** out of which they are made, and the **will** of Him by whose command all other things are brought about.

**ASCLEPIUS:** What then is this reasoning, O Trismegistus?

**TRISMEGISTUS:** It is this, O Asclepius. For God—either the Father, or the Lord...

->II<-                                  48
...the Lord of all, or by whatever other name more holy and religious he may be called by men—which among us, for the sake of our intellect, ought to be held sacred—in our contemplation of such great divinity, we have named him definitely by none of these names. For if a voice is a sound of air struck by breath, declaring every human will or feeling which one might perceive in the mind through the senses—whose name's entire substance is composed of a few syllables, defined and circumscribed, so that there might be in man a necessary exchange of voice and ears, as well as of sense, breath, and air, and all things in and through these—then is the name of God merely this? I do not hope so; for the Maker of all majesty, and the father or lord of all things, cannot be named by a single name composed of many parts. 

Truly, he must be called by one name, or rather by every name (since he is one and is all things); it is necessary either that all things be his name, or that he himself be called by the names of all things. This one, therefore, being all things, and most full of the fecundity <note>Fecundity here refers to the power of being fertile or productive.</note> of both sexes, always pregnant by his own will, gives birth to whatever he has wished to procreate. His will is total goodness. This same goodness belongs to all things. From his divinity, nature is born, so that all things may be as they are, and were, and shall be; and henceforth, nature provides from itself the power of being born. 

Let this reasoning, therefore, O Asclepius, be rendered to you: why and how all things are made of both sexes. 

ASCLEPIUS: Therefore, do you call everything "God," O Trismegistus? 

TRISMEGISTUS: Not only God, O Asclepius, but all living beings and inanimate things. For it is impossible for any of the things that exist to be barren. For if fecundity were taken away from all things that exist, it would be impossible for those things to exist forever. For I say that nature, sense, and the world contain this quality within themselves and preserve all born things within themselves. For by procreation...

<summary>Hermes explains that God is beyond any single human name because He is the source of all things. He introduces the Hermetic concept that God contains both sexes and that all of creation shares in this divine fecundity to ensure the continuity of existence.</summary>
<keywords>naming God, fecundity, divine nature, procreation, duality of sex, Asclepius, Hermes Trismegistus</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and Asclepius from the *Asclepius* (part of the Hermetic Corpus). Hermes is explaining that God contains both genders and that the act of procreation is a divine mystery reflecting the Creator’s own nature.</meta>

HERMES.
Both sexes are full of him, and the connection of both—or (what is truer) their unity—is incomprehensible. You may rightly call this either Desire, or Love, or both. Therefore, once this is perceived by the mind as truer than any truth and more manifest—that from that place of all nature, this ministry of procreation was devised and granted to all things forever by God—to which the highest charity, joy, cheerfulness, desire, and divine love is innate, it would have to be said how great is the power and necessity of this ministry, had it not been possible for it to be known to everyone from their own contemplation and inward sense. 

For if you consider that final moment of time when we reach the ritual <note>The "ritual" here refers to the physical act of sexual union.</note> from the brain <note>original: "ex cerebro". Ancient medical theory, particularly in the Pythagorean and Hippocratic traditions, often held that semen originated in the brain or spinal marrow before traveling through the body.</note>, so that nature pours each offspring into the other, you notice how one eagerly seizes the seed of the other and hides it deep within. Finally, at that time, through the common mingling, even females acquire the strength of males, and males grow faint <note>original: "latescunt", literally "to be hidden" or "to lie concealed," suggesting the male loses his distinctness within the female during union.</note> in the female body. 

Therefore, the effect of this so charming and necessary ministry is performed in secret, lest the divinity of both natures be forced to blush at the mingling of the sexes if the ignorant masses were to mock it; much more so if it were subjected to the sight of irreligious men. However, there are not many—or rather, they are very few—so that the religious might even be counted in the world. Whence it happens that malice remains in many, through a lack of prudence and knowledge of all things that exist. For from the understanding of divine religion, by which all things were established, there is born the contempt for and the remedy of the vices of the whole world. But as long as ignorance and lack of skill persist, all vices grow strong and wound the soul with incurable defects; and the soul, infected and corrupted by these as if by poisons, swells up—except for those whose souls' discipline and intellect are the highest cure. If therefore...

<summary>Hermes explains that God encompasses both male and female natures and that human procreation is a sacred, divine "ministry" that mirrors this celestial unity. He warns that this mystery must be kept secret from the ignorant and irreligious to avoid mockery, noting that true religious understanding is the only cure for the world's vices.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius, divine gender, procreation, sacred mystery, origins of vice, spiritual healing, Hermeticism</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues from the discussion of the divine nature of procreation. Hermes is now transitioning to explain why only a few humans possess true understanding and how the dual nature of humanity—composed of both the divine and the material—places them in a unique position relative to the gods.</meta>

                                II                                49

Since this will benefit only a few, it is worthy to pursue and explain this treatise: why, indeed, the divinity has deigned to impart his intelligence and teaching to humans alone. Hear, therefore. When God, the father and lord, made humans after the gods <note>In Hermetic thought, "gods" usually refers to the celestial bodies (stars and planets) which were considered immortal, sentient beings created before humanity.</note>, he fashioned them from the more corrupt part of the world and from the divine part, weighing them with an equal balance. It happened that the vices of the world remained mixed in their bodies, and other [vices arose] because of the food and sustenance which we necessarily have in common with all animals. From these things, it is inevitable that the longings of desires and the remaining vices of the mind fall upon human souls. 

To the gods, however—as they were made from the cleanest part of nature and need no supports of reason or teaching (since their immortality and the vigor of a single, eternal age is itself their prudence and teaching)—he nevertheless established an order written by the law of necessity, by eternal law, so that because of the unity of reason, they would not be strangers to teaching and intellect. 

Acknowledging man among all animals for his capacity for reason and teaching alone—through which humans might have been able to turn away and distance themselves from the vices of bodies—God held out to them the striving and hope of immortality. Finally, he composed the "good man" <note>original: "bonum hominem". This refers to the archetypal human or the human potential for moral excellence.</note> who could be immortal from both natures: the divine and the mortal. And man, being thus composed by the will of God, was established to be better even than the gods (who are formed from only immortal nature) and all other mortals. 

Because of this, man is joined to the gods by kinship; he venerates them with religion and a holy mind. And the gods also, with pious affection, look upon and guard all human affairs. But this is said only of those few humans endowed with a pious mind. Regarding the vicious, indeed, nothing must be said, lest this most holy discourse be violated by the consideration of them.
                                                                gi

<summary>Hermes explains that humanity is a unique blend of divine spirit and corruptible matter, a duality that grants humans the potential for immortality and a status even greater than the purely immortal gods. This relationship creates a reciprocal bond where pious humans venerate the gods, and the gods guard human affairs.</summary>
<keywords>dual nature of man, divinity, immortality, vices of the world, reason, gods vs humans, piety, Hermeticism</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary and commentary on the Hermetic dialogues, moving from the general nature of the human soul and its struggle with the material body into the specific cosmology of the "usiarchs" or celestial rulers. It transitions from the theological significance of procreation to the controversial Hermetic claim that humans are "makers of gods."</meta>

MER

<margin>e</margin> In the eighth chapter, he encompasses the usiarchs <term>usiarchs<note>From the Greek 'ousiarchēs', meaning a ruler of essence or substance; a governing principle or "archon" of a particular realm of reality.</note></term> and substantial principles, whom he calls gods. He calls Jupiter the usiarch of heaven, and Light the usiarch of the sun. He posits thirty-six "horoscopes"—that is, watchers of the hours—of the stars always fixed in the same place; and the prince of these he calls <term>Pantomorphos<note>original: "Pantomorphon", meaning "all-formed" or "taking all shapes." This refers to a cosmic entity representing the totality of the zodiac or the material universe.</note></term>. He posits the seven principles of the wandering spheres <note>The seven classical planets: Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, the Sun, and the Moon.</note>: Fortune and Fate. He also posits an usiarch of the air. But these seem to be the figments of demons, and certain vain veils leading toward pagan errors, which compel those who dwell too long upon them to fade away rather than to be perfected.

From here, he returns to the contemplation of the supreme God, who is ineffable and unnamable, or who is named by the names of all things, being most full of fecundity. From Him, all things have their existence, so that nothing can be unfruitful. Hence comes that love of procreating and the great striving of nature—having in itself nothing at all foul or disgraceful, provided it is thought of religiously, and such discourse is held regarding it, and it is treated no less religiously. For this has been granted as an emulation of divine fecundity.

Truly, because the greatest part of men are ignorant of the mysteries, they profane the divine laws and, in the manner of beasts, they treat those matters insanely, irreverently, and as if they were raging and out of their minds. Therefore, out of shame, one should now hardly dare to think or speak anything of such people: because that disorder, and that irreverence of men toward the venerable ordination of God, thrusts itself into our senses and thoughts, rather than that beautiful, holy, and uncontaminated ordination of God. And because it is granted to few to have pure senses, I judge that one should think and speak of such matters only to a few.

He soon adds: the god of the sun has imparted intelligence and discipline to men, by which his immortal and divine part might be able to avoid vices—vices which it would otherwise easily incur through the admixture of the bodily mass. For by reason of the body and the dissolvable part, it is inevitable that the longings of desires and the remaining vices of the mind fall upon the souls of men. This is the eighth part.

### Chapter IX

<margin>On Idolatry.</margin> <margin>e</margin> And since our discourse is directed toward the kinship and fellowship of men and gods: recognize, O Asclepius, the power and strength of man. The Lord
<margin>The fall of Mercury.</margin> and Father, or (that which is highest) God, just as he is the maker of the celestial gods, so man is the maker of the gods who are in temples, being content with human proximity. And not—

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous section where Hermes Trismegistus was discussing the unique status of humanity. It moves into one of the most famous and controversial sections of the "Asclepius," regarding the "making of gods" (the animation of statues) and the spiritual destiny of Egypt.</meta>

II 50

[not only] are they illuminated: but they also illuminate. Nor does it only profit him: but it even confirms the gods.

Do you wonder, O Asclepius? Or do you perhaps distrust this, as many do?

ASCLEPIUS. I am confounded, O Trismegistus; but having willingly assented to your words, I judge the man most happy who has attained such great happiness.

TRISMEGISTUS. And not undeservedly is he worthy of wonder: who is the greatest of all <note>The text suggests that the man who understands his divine nature is greater than even the celestial gods.</note>. For the genus of all [the gods] is manifest without confusion, propagated from the purest part of nature. And their signs are as if they are only heads instead of all [limbs] <note>This likely refers to the celestial bodies or planets, which appear as "heads" or spheres in the sky rather than having human bodies.</note>. But the species of gods which humanity shapes is formed from both natures: from the divine, which is prior and much more divine; and from that which is within humans—that is, from the matter out of which they were fabricated. And they are configured not only with heads alone: but with all members and the whole body. Thus humanity, mindful of its nature and origin, perseveres in that imitation of divinity. Just as the Father and Lord made eternal gods to be like Himself, so humanity figures its own gods in the likeness of its own face. 

ASCLEPIUS. Do you mean statues, O Trismegistus?

TRISMEGISTUS. Statues, O Asclepius. Do you see how far you yourself distrust? Animated statues, full of sense and spirit, doing such great and wondrous things. Statues that foreknow the future and predict it by lot, by prophets, by dreams, and many other things; bringing illnesses to men and curing them, and bringing sadness or joy according to their merits. 

<margin>
THE 

FALL 

OF 

HERMES 
<note>original: "LAPSUS HERMETIS". This marginal note was likely added by a Christian editor or commentator to signal that the following passage—defending the creation of "gods" through statues—is a theological error or a "fall" into idolatry.</note>
</margin>

Or are you ignorant, O Asclepius, that Egypt is the image of heaven? Or, to speak more truly, it is the transfer and descent of all things that are governed and exercised in heaven. And if the truth must be told: our land is the temple of the whole world. And yet, since it befits the prudent to foreknow all things, it is not right for you to be ignorant of this: a time is coming when it will appear that the Egyptians have in vain, with a pious mind...

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the prophetic "Lament of Hermes" from the Asclepius. In the previous section, Hermes described how humans create gods in the form of animated statues. Here, the tone shifts dramatically to a mournful prophecy about the future of Egypt, predicting a time when the gods will abandon the land and its ancient traditions will be forgotten or forbidden.</meta>

HERMES.
...to have preserved divinity with diligent religion. And all their holy veneration: failing into vanity, will be frustrated. For divinity is about to return from the earth to heaven. Egypt will be abandoned: and the land (which was the seat of divinity) widowed of religion, will be destitute of the presence of the gods. For when foreigners fill this region and land, not only will there be a neglect of religions: but (what is harder) as if by laws, a prohibition with a proscribed penalty will be established against religion, piety, and divine worship <note>Hermes is predicting a time when the traditional Egyptian rites would be made illegal, likely reflecting the historical transition to Roman or Christian rule.</note>. Then this land, the most holy seat of shrines and temples: will be full of tombs and of the dead. O Egypt, Egypt: of your religions only stories will survive, and equally incredible to your descendants, and only words carved in stones will remain, telling of your pious deeds <note>This is a hauntingly accurate description of how ancient Egyptian hieroglyphs were viewed for centuries—as mysterious "stories" on stone—before they were deciphered.</note>. And the Scythian or the Indian or some such—that is, barbarian neighbors—will inhabit Egypt <term>Scythian or Indian: generic terms used in antiquity to describe people from the furthest known reaches of the world, symbolizing total outsiders.</term>. For divinity will seek heaven again; abandoned men will all die, and thus Egypt, widowed of god and man, will be deserted. But you I call upon, most holy river <note>The Nile.</note>, and to you I predict the future. Full of a torrent of blood: you will burst forth even to the banks. And the divine waves will not only overflow with blood: but will be totally defiled. And the number of the buried will be much greater than the living. And he who survives will be known as an Egyptian by his tongue alone, but in his actions he will seem a stranger...

<summary>This famous passage, known as the "Lament of Hermes," foretells the spiritual desolation of Egypt, the departure of the gods to heaven, and the prohibition of ancient religious practices by foreign rulers.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Egypt, prophecy, Lament of Hermes, Nile, divinity, religious prohibition, ancient ruins</keywords>
