<margin>work</margin>...immutable; a glorious construction; a good composed of a multiform variety of images; the engine of the will of God <note>original: "machina voluntatis dei." This refers to the universe as a complex instrument designed by the Divine</note>, who supports his work without envy—a multiform collection gathered into one of all things that can be venerated, praised, and finally loved by those who see it. For darkness will be preferred to light, and death will be judged more useful than life. No one will look up to heaven. The religious man will be thought insane, and the irreligious man will be thought wise; the madman will be thought brave, and the worst person will be held as good. For the soul and all things concerning it—by which it is either born mortal or presumes it will attain immortality (according to what I have explained to you)—will be thought not only a joke, but even a vanity. 

But believe me: even a capital danger <note>Meaning a crime punishable by death</note> will be established for him who dedicates himself to the religion of the mind <term>religion of the mind: "religio mentis," the Hermetic practice of seeking God through intellectual and spiritual contemplation</term>. New laws will be established; a new law. Nothing holy, nothing religious, nor anything worthy of heaven or the celestial beings will be heard or believed in the mind. A sorrowful departure of the gods from humans takes place; only harmful angels remain <note>original: "nocentes angeli." In this context, these are "daemons" or spirits that lead humanity toward destructive behavior</note>, who, mixed with humanity, will compel the wretched ones (by laying hands on them) toward every evil of audacity: into wars, into robberies, into frauds, and into all things that are contrary to the nature of souls. 

Then the earth will not stand firm, nor will the sea be sailed. Neither will the heaven remain with the courses of the stars, nor will the courses of the stars stand firm in the heaven. Every divine voice will grow silent by a necessary stillness. The fruits of the earth will be corrupted, nor will the soil be fertile. And the air itself will languish with a mournful torpor. This and such is the old age that will come: the irreligion of the world, and its disorder, and the irrationality of all good things. 

When all these things have happened, O Asclepius, then that Lord and Father, God the first-powerful and governor of the one world, looking into the customs and voluntary deeds, by his own will (which is the kindness of God), resisting the vices and the corruption of everything...

<summary>This page describes the "Old Age of the World," a prophetic collapse where morality is inverted, the soul's immortality is mocked, and nature itself falls into decay. It concludes with the promise that God will intervene to stop this total corruption.</summary>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Lament of Hermes." The previous page concluded with a dire prophecy of the world's descent into darkness, where spiritual life is mocked. This page marks the transition to the "Restoration," describing how God will intervene to purify and renew the cosmos.</meta>

MER.
<note>Short for Mercurius/Hermes Trismegistus</note> ...recalling every error, either washing away all wickedness with a flood, or consuming it with fire, or by pestilential diseases scattered everywhere through various places: He will finally bring the world back to its ancient face. Thus the world itself will be seen as worthy of adoration and wonder; and God, the creator and restorer of so great a work, will be celebrated by all who exist then with frequent proclamations of praise and blessings. For this is the rebirth of the world: a reformation of all good things, and a most holy and religious restoration of nature itself, within the preordained course of time—a nature which is and was eternal, without beginning. For the will of God lacks a beginning; it remains the same and is everywhere eternal. 

ASCLE. 
<note>Asclepius, the student of Hermes</note> For the nature of God is the counsel of His will; the highest goodness is that counsel, O Trismegistus. 

TRISME. 
O Asclepius, the will is born from counsel, and the act of willing itself comes from the will. For He does not desire anything out of necessity <note>original: "impense." This suggests God does not strive or suffer need like a human, but acts from perfect fullness</note>, since He is the most full of all things; and He possesses all those things which He wills. He wills all good things, and He has all that He wills. Indeed, He both thinks and wills all good things. This is what God is: and the world is the image of that Good. 

ASCLE. 
Is the world good, O Trismegistus? 

TRISME. 
It is good, O Asclepius, as I shall teach you. For just as God is the distributor and dispenser of good things—namely, consciousness <note>original: "sensus"</note>, soul, and life—to all species and kinds within the world, so too the world is the provider and giver of all things that seem "good" to mortals: namely, the changing of the seasons, the fruits of the earth, birth, growth, maturity, and similar things. Because of this, God, dwelling above the summit of the highest heaven, is everywhere and watches over all things. For there is a place beyond heaven, without stars, far removed from all physical <note>original: "corpulentis," referring to dense, bodily matter</note> things. The Governor <note>original: "dispensator"</note> (who is between heaven and earth) occupies a place we call Jupiter <note>original: "Iouem." In this context, Jupiter represents a cosmic administrator or the spirit of the planetary sphere that manages the physical world</note>.

<summary>Hermes describes the divine restoration of the world through a cleansing of evil and the return of the cosmos to its original, holy state. He explains the relationship between God’s will and the goodness of the world, identifying the world as an image of God's inherent goodness.</summary>
<keywords>Restoration of the world, Divine Will, Goodness, Jupiter as Governor, rebirth of nature, Hermeticism</keywords>

This section moves into the specific distribution of divine powers over the material realm and includes a critical commentary referencing St. Augustine.</meta>

II                                                                                                   52

...over the earth and the sea: Plutonian Jupiter <note>original: "iupiter plutonius." This refers to a terrestrial or underworld aspect of Jupiter who governs the physical, material realm</note> rules, and he is the nourisher of mortal living things and fruit-bearing plants. By the powers of all these, fruits, trees, and the earth are quickened; the powers and effects of others, however, are distributed through all things that exist. Indeed, those who rule the earth are distributed: and they shall be settled in a city at the furthest border of Egypt, which shall be founded on the side of the setting sun <note>Referring to the West</note>, toward which the entire mortal race shall hasten by land and sea.

**ASCLEPIUS.** But at this present time, where are they, O Trismegistus? 

**TRISMEGISTUS.** They are settled in a great city on the Libyan mountain <note>Likely referring to the desert hills west of the Nile</note>; and let these things be told thus far.

<margin>Augustine, Book Eight, Chapter 23.</margin>
Among us, the whole of this <insert>teaching</insert> is profane: which Augustine also rightly refutes in the book *The City of God*. Hermes approves of idolatry, extols it with wonderful praises, and preaches it; he even laments its fall. He places a certain God above the summit of heaven, looking down upon all things; but within that place which is contained by heaven and earth, he places Jupiter as the dispenser, and Plutonian Jupiter as the master of the earth...

<summary>Hermes describes the "Plutonian Jupiter" as the ruler of the material world and mentions a sacred city in Egypt where divine powers reside. The commentator notes that St. Augustine condemned these passages as profane idolatry in his work The City of God.</summary>

<keywords>Plutonian Jupiter, Egypt, Libyan mountain, St. Augustine, City of God, idolatry, terrestrial gods, Hermes Trismegistus</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Hermes Trismegistus’s views on "animated statues" (idols) by referencing St. Augustine’s "City of God." It transitions from a theological debate on idolatry to Chapter X of the Asclepius, which discusses the nature of death and the soul's journey after leaving the body.</meta>

MER.
...of the statues and their souls; we recognize that those souls, senses, and spirits that cause infirmities in men and then cure them are undoubtedly evil spirits. We know with unwavering faith that they are those of whom the prophet speaks with a truthful mouth: "All the gods of the nations are demons." <note>original: "omnes dij gentiū:demonia." A quotation from Psalm 96:5 (or 95:5 in the Vulgate), used here to argue that the "gods" Hermes claims to inhabit statues are actually fallen angels.</note> Let these few things be sufficient against the impurity of Mercurius and of this ninth section. For we address our discourse to those who are imbued with the knowledge of the true God—whom to know (as the wise man says) is perfected justice, and to know His justice and power is the root of immortality. <note>This refers to the Wisdom of Solomon 15:3.</note> Lazzarelli <note>Ludovico Lazzarelli (1440–1500), a poet and Hermetic philosopher who attempted to reconcile Hermes with Christian doctrine.</note> draws this passage toward an analogy, as if the "idols" were the apostles; the "maker" is man; "Christ" is the power instilled from above; and the "spirits" are the Holy Spirit. "Egypt" represents the darkness of the nations and the persecution of the disciples, apostles, and martyrs. The "inscribed stones" narrate pious deeds, while the hearts of posterity retain not the works but only the words of faith. These things are piously devised, but perhaps forced in the literal sense. Regarding the "lapse" of Hermes, I agree with Augustine both here and in the thirteenth chapter. For the prophets of the nations (such as Balaam or the Sibyls) seem to have this trait: they endure both light and darkness, experiencing bright and obscure intervals in their prophecies—sometimes pure, and at other times impure. These things suffice for chapter nine.

<margin>Wisdom 14.</margin>

## ¶ Chapter X

<margin>The soul after death.</margin>

Now we must discuss the immortal and the mortal. For the hope and fear of death torment many who are ignorant of true reason. Death is caused by the dissolution of the body, which has been exhausted by labor, and by the completion of the number <note>A Pythagorean/Platonic concept suggesting the body has a mathematical or harmonic lifespan.</note> by which the limbs of the body are fitted into one machine for vital uses. For the body dies when it has ceased to be able to carry the vital parts of man. This, therefore, is death: the dissolution of the body and the destruction of bodily sensation. Concern over this is superfluous. But there is another necessary thing, which human ignorance or unbelief despises.

**ASCLEPIUS.** What is it, O Trismegistus, that they are either ignorant of or doubt can exist?

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Hear then, O Asclepius: when the departure of the soul from the body has taken place, then the judgment and examination of its merit will pass into the power of the highest demon. <note>original: "summi demonis." In the Hermetic tradition, a "daemon" is a spiritual guardian or intermediary, not necessarily an evil spirit. Here, it refers to a judge of the afterlife.</note> And he, when he sees that the soul has been pious and just, permits it to remain in its appropriate places...

<summary>This page concludes a Christian critique of Hermes's defense of idols, characterizing his inspirations as a mix of divine light and "impure" darkness. It then begins Chapter X, defining death as a natural mechanical dissolution of the body and introducing the post-mortem judgment of the soul by a spiritual authority.</summary>

<keywords>idolatry, St. Augustine, Ludovico Lazzarelli, Sibyls, Balaam, death, dissolution of the body, post-mortem judgment, daemon, immortality</keywords>

But if he <note>Referring to the "Great Judge" or the Divine mind overseeing the soul's departure.</note> sees the soul smeared with the stains of sins and defiled by vices, he will hurl it from above down to the depths, handing it over to the storms and whirlwinds of the air, fire, and water, which are often in discord. Snatched away and tossed forever by the waves of the world in different directions between heaven and earth, the soul will be driven by eternal punishments. Thus, the soul’s very eternity works against it, for it is subjugated by an immortal sentence and an eternal judgment. Therefore, we must recognize that we must revere, fear, and be careful lest we be entangled in these things. For those who do not believe now will be forced to believe after their sins—not by words but by examples, and not by threats but by the very suffering of punishments.

**ASCLEPIUS:** Then, Trismegistus, are the sins of men not punished by human law alone?

**TRISMEGISTUS:** First, O Asclepius, all earthly things that exist are mortal. <note>In this context, "mortal" implies they are subject to change and physical decay, unlike the eternal soul.</note> Furthermore, those things that live by a bodily principle and then fail to live by that same bodily law are all liable to punishments according to the merits or sins of their lives. After death, they are subjected to punishments that are all the more severe if they were perhaps zealous in their vices while they lived. For since the Divinity has foreknowledge of all things, punishments are rendered precisely according to the qualities of the sins.

**ASCLEPIUS:** Who are worthy of the greatest punishments, O Trismegistus?

**TRISMEGISTUS:** Those who, having been condemned by human laws, lose their lives violently; <note>In Hermetic philosophy, a violent death—especially an execution for crimes—was thought to reflect a profound spiritual failure, as the soul did not depart naturally.</note> so that they seem not to have paid back the soul owed to nature, but to have paid a penalty for their deeds. In contrast, for the just man, there is a defense in the <term>religion of God</term> <note>original: "dei religione." Here, "religion" refers to a lived, spiritual devotion and knowledge of God rather than just institutional practice.</note> and in the highest piety. For God protects such people from all evils. Indeed, the Father and Lord of all, who alone is all things, willingly shows Himself to everyone. He is not shown in a specific place, nor by a specific quality, nor by a specific quantity; rather, He illuminates man by the intelligence of the mind alone. Having shaken the darkness of errors from the soul and perceived the clarity of truth, the man who commits himself with his whole sense of intelligence...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the *Asclepius*, specifically where Hermes (Mercurius) explains the distinction between the pious and the impious, moving into a discourse on the divinity of the Sun and the immortality of the Cosmos as a living organism.</meta>

                                        MERCURIUS

...mingles with the divine <note>original: "divine commiscet"</note>; by whose love he is freed from that part of nature by which he is mortal, and he conceives the confidence of future immortality. This, therefore, will be the distinction between the good and the evil. For each individual shines forth through piety, religion, prudence, and the worship and veneration of God, as if an eye had perceived with true reason. And the confidence of his belief <note>In this context, "credulitas" refers to a deep, trusting faith or conviction rather than "gullibility."</note> stands out as much among men as the sun excels the other stars by its light. 

For the sun itself illuminates the other stars, not so much by the magnitude of its power as by its divinity and holiness. Believe this sun to be the second god <note>The "Secundus Deus" in Hermetic thought is the visible sun, which acts as a demiurge or governor of the physical world under the Supreme God.</note>, O Asclepius, governing all other things and illuminating all the world's creatures, whether they be animate or inanimate. For if the world is a living being <note>original: "animal"; the text views the Cosmos as a "Great Animal" or living organism.</note> and is always alive—and was, and is, and will be—then nothing in the world is mortal. For in every single part of that which is always living, which exists in the world just as in one and the same perpetually living creature, there is no place for mortality. Therefore, the eternity of life... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence: "ergo vite/ eterint," likely leading into a discussion on eternal life or the eternal nature of the soul.</note>

II                                                                                                   54

...consists of a twofold effect. For the world itself is brought to life from the outside by eternity; and it brings to life all things that are within it, differing according to the numbers and times established and prefixed. All things are governed through the effect of the sun and the wandering of the stars <note>The author refers to the planets, which were called "wandering stars" in antiquity.</note>, all temporal things being recorded by reason and divine law. Moreover, earthly time is distinguished by the quality of the air and the variety of heat and cold; but celestial time is distinguished by the return of the stars to the same places as they recur in their temporal revolution. And the world is the receptacle of time, by whose course and agitation it is nourished. Time, however, is maintained by order. Order and time, through alternation, bring about the renewal of all things that are in the world.

**T**enth [partition], concerning death. Concerning the examination of the soul when it has departed from the body. That those whose life was taken by just human laws, because of the greatness of their crimes, are punished more after death than the just; may God be our protection. Concerning the divinity of the sun and the world. He posits the world as the first sensible god <note>A "sensible god" is one that can be perceived by the senses, unlike the supreme, hidden God.</note>, and the sun as the second. He claims the world will never be at rest and will never be destroyed. We, however, do not attribute the incommunicable name of God <note>The "incommunicable name" refers to the Tetragrammaton or the unique essence of the Creator that cannot be shared with created things.</note> to either the sun or the world. We believe that the world must one day be dissolved. Therefore, these sayings of Hermes, if understood simply as they sound, carry the error of the Gentiles <note>The translator is warning the reader that Hermes (as a pagan/Gentile) might sound heretical, but his words can be interpreted in a Christian-friendly way.</note>. Nevertheless, we attribute divinity to them through their participation in them as excellent works of God. This is the tenth partition.

->**Chapter XI**<-

**S**ince all things are thus, nothing is stable, nothing is fixed, nothing is immobile among those things being born, nor among celestial things, nor among earthly things. For God alone—and deservedly He alone—is entirely full and perfect in Himself, by Himself, and around Himself. And He is His own firm stability; He cannot be moved by any impulse nor by any place, since all things are in Him and He alone is in all things. Unless someone should dare to say that His own motion exists within eternity; but even eternity itself is more truly immobile, into which [all motion flows].

<summary>This page discusses the relationship between time, the cosmos, and the sun, while providing a theological summary of the tenth book of the Asclepius. It concludes by contrasting the constant motion and instability of the created world with the absolute stability and perfection of God.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius, divinity of the sun, eternity, cosmic order, stability of God, Gentile error, sensible god, celestial time, renewal of things</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse from the previous section of the *Asclepius*. Having established the world as a vessel for time and order, the text now explores the metaphysical relationship between God, Eternity, and Time, specifically addressing the paradox of how an immobile God can be the source of all movement.</meta>

MER

the movement of all times returns, and it is from this that the movement of all times takes its beginning. God, therefore, was stable: and eternity always stood similarly with him, having within himself the unborn world (which we rightly call <term>sensible<note>original: "sensibilem"; referring to the physical world perceivable by the five senses.</note></term>), which is the image of this God. Hence the world was made an imitator of eternity. Moreover, time has the force and nature of its own stability (even though it is always in motion) by that very necessity of returning into itself. Therefore, although eternity is stable, immobile, and fixed, nevertheless, because movement is always called back into eternity by the mobility of time, and that mobility turns according to the logic of time, it is brought about that eternity itself—though indeed immobile—seems to move only through the time in which it exists, and in which all movement is found. 

Thus it is brought about that even the stability of eternity moves, and the mobility of time becomes stable by the fixed law of its course. In this way, it is even believable that God moves within himself by that same immobility. For in the magnitude of his very stability, there is an immobile movement; for the law of his magnitude is itself immobile. 

This, therefore, which is such—which is not subjected to the physical senses—is infinite, incomprehensible, and inestimable. It can neither be sustained, nor carried, nor investigated. For from where, and to where, and where, or how, or what kind it is, is uncertain. For he is borne in the highest stability, and within him is his own stability, whether it be God, or eternity, or both, or one within the other, or both within each other. 

Because of this, eternity is without the <term>definition<note>original: "diffinitione"; here meaning "limitation" or "boundary."</note></term> of time. But time, which can be defined either by number or by alternation, or by returning through the circuit of another, is eternal. Both, therefore, appear infinite; both appear eternal. For stability, being fixed so that it can sustain those things which are moveable, by the benefit of its firmness, deservedly holds the primacy. For of all things which...

<summary>This page explores the Hermetic paradox of the "immobile mover," explaining how God and Eternity remain stable while generating the constant movement of time and the physical world. It defines the world as a "sensible" image of an incomprehensible God, where time eventually returns to the stability of eternity.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, God, Time, Sensible World, Immobility, Movement, Infinity, Divine Law, Asclepius, Hermeticism</keywords>

# II 55

...are: the primary principles are God and eternity. The world, however, since it is mobile, does not have primacy. For its mobility precedes its own stability within the law of eternal agitation, by possessing an immobile firmness. Therefore, every <term>sense of divinity<note>original: "sensus divinitatis"; here meaning a divine consciousness or way of perceiving that is inherent to God's nature.</note></term> is similar and immobile: it moves itself within its own stability. It is holy, incorruptible, and eternal—and whatever better name it might be called. Eternity, which consists in the very truth of the highest God, is most full of all <term>sensible things<note>original: "sensibilium"; things that can be perceived by the senses.</note></term> and of all knowledge, existing (if I may say so) with God.

But the mundane sense is the receptacle of all sensible things, species, and disciplines. Human sense, however, arises from the tenacity of memory, in that it is mindful of all things that have been done. For the sense of divinity descended as far as the human animal. God did not wish for the highest and divine sense to be mixed with all living things, lest it be shamed by the commingling with other animals. For the intelligence of human sense—of whatever kind or magnitude it may be—exists entirely in the memory of things past. Through this tenacity of memory, man was made the governor of the earth.

Moreover, the intellect of the nature and quality of the world's sense can be perceived through all things that are sensible in the world. Eternity, which is the second principle, is recognized through the sense and quality given by the sensible world. But the understanding of the quality and the quality of the sense of the highest God is Truth alone. Of this Truth, not even the furthest line or shadow is recognized in the world. For where anything is known by the measurement of time, there are lies, there are <term>physical births<note>original: "geniture"; referring to the process of being born into the physical world of change and decay, as opposed to eternal existence.</note></term>, and there errors are seen.

You see, therefore, O Asclepius, in what things we are established, and what matters we treat or what we dare to touch upon. But to you, highest God, I give thanks: you who have illuminated me with the light of seeing your divinity. And you, O Tat and Asclepius and Ammon:

<summary>This page defines the metaphysical hierarchy between God, Eternity, and the World, explaining that while God possesses a pure "divine sense," humans possess a "mundane sense" rooted in memory that allows them to govern the earth.</summary>
<keywords>God, Eternity, human intelligence, memory, Truth vs. Time, Asclepius, Tat, Ammon, sensible world</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Hermetic dialogue regarding the nature of perception. It concludes the discussion on how the human intellect perceives the world and the divine, then transitions into a rejection of the concept of the "void" or "vacuum" within the universe.</meta>

HERMES:
Keep the divine mysteries covered in silence within the secrets of your heart, and conceal them in stillness. Now, the intellect differs from the senses in this: our intellect reaches the point of understanding and discerning the quality of the world's sense through the focused intention of the mind. The intellect of the world, however, reaches toward eternity and the knowledge of the gods who are above it. And so it happens to us humans that we see the things of heaven as if through a mist, as much as is possible through the condition of human sense. This intention of ours is very narrow for perceiving such great goods; yet it becomes most broad when it perceives them with the happiness of a clear conscience.

v. The eleventh chapter reveals that only God is immobile and stable, while all other things are changeable. God is infinite, incomprehensible, and inestimable, and his true nature is unknown. Concerning the holy, incorruptible, and eternal sense of God. Concerning the sense of the world. Concerning the sense of humanity. Concerning the difference between intellect and sense. This is the summary of the eleventh part.

### Chapter XII

d. Regarding the **void** <note>original: "inane"</note>—which many people believe to be a great reality—this is my view: the void is nothing, nor could it have ever been, nor will it ever be. For all the parts of the world are completely full, so that the world itself is full and perfect with bodies that are diverse in quality, form, appearance, and size. One of these is greater or smaller than another, and they differ in density and thinness. For some of them, being denser, are more easily seen, just as the larger ones are; but the smaller and thinner ones either can hardly be seen or cannot be seen at all. We know these things exist only through the sense of touch. 

Hence it happens that many believe these are not bodies but are "empty spaces"—which is impossible. For just as that which is said to exist outside the world—if indeed there is anything there at all (a claim I do not believe)—must be full of <term>intelligible things<note>original: "intelligibilium rerum"; realities that can only be perceived by the mind or intellect, rather than physical eyes.</note></term>...

<summary>Hermes explains that human intellect perceives divine truths as if through a mist and argues that the universe is a "plenum," meaning it is entirely full of matter and has no such thing as a "void."</summary>
<keywords>intellect vs sense, divine mysteries, the void, plenum, perception of God, nature of the world</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech of Hermes Trismegistus to his disciple Asclepius. Having previously argued that the world is entirely full, Hermes now explains why certain beings remain invisible to us and deepens his rejection of the "void" by arguing that "place" cannot exist without a body to fill it.</meta>

[Marginal notes on the left are partially cut off]

                                                                                                    56
that is, of things similar to his own divinity, so that even this world, which is called the sensible world <note>The "sensible world" refers to the physical universe perceivable by the five senses.</note>, is most full of bodies and living beings suited to its nature and quality. We do not see all of their forms: some are excessively large, while others are very small—either because of the great distance of the space between us, or because the sharpness of our vision is dulled so that they appear to us as they do, or because, on account of their extreme smallness, many people do not believe they exist at all. I am speaking now of daemons <note>original: "demonas"; in this context, these are not "demons" in the modern sense, but intermediary spirits or guardian entities that inhabit the space between the human and the divine.</note>, whom I believe dwell with us, and heroes <note>original: "heroas"; semi-divine beings or elevated souls.</note>, who reside in the purest part of the air above us and the earth, where there is no place for clouds, nor any disturbance from the movement of any celestial signs. 

Because of this, O Asclepius, you should never say that the void is "nothing," unless you first specify *what* it is void of—the thing you call "void"—as in "void of fire," or "void of water," or things similar to these. For even if it should happen to seem that something could be void of such things—whether that which seems void is small or large—it can never be empty of spirit <note>original: "spiritu"; in ancient physics, "spiritus" or pneuma was a thin, life-giving substance that permeated all things.</note> and air. 

Similarly, we must speak of "place" <note>original: "loco"</note>, for the word alone lacks meaning. What a "place" is becomes apparent only from the object to which it belongs. For if the primary name of the object is removed, the meaning is mutilated. Therefore, we speak correctly of "the place of water," "the place of fire," or of things similar to these; for just as it is impossible for something to be a "void," so too a "place" on its own cannot be identified for what it is. 

For if you posit a "place" without that which it contains, it will appear to be a "void," which I do not believe exists in the world. But if the void is nothing, then "place" does not appear as anything by itself, unless you add to it lengths, breadths, or heights, like the markings used to measure human bodies. Since these things are so, O Asclepius—and you others who are present—know that the intelligible world (that is, God, who is discerned by the gaze of the mind alone) is incorporeal, nor is its nature...

<summary>Hermes explains that the universe is teeming with invisible beings like daemons and heroes, and argues that a "void" is a physical impossibility because space is always filled with air, spirit, or a specific body.</summary>
<keywords>fullness of the world, daemons, heroes, the void (inane), nature of place (locus), incorporeal God, sensible world, intelligible world</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues the Latin text of the *Asclepius* (part of the *Corpus Hermeticum*), picking up mid-sentence from the previous discussion on the impossibility of a vacuum. It transitions from the physical filling of the universe to the nature of "sense" (sensus) and its relationship to the soul and higher intelligence.</meta>

                                                                                                    57
...spirit and air <note>original: "spiritu et aëre." In the Hermetic view, even "empty" space is filled with a subtle, life-giving substance.</note>. Therefore, it must be said, O Asclepius, that nothing is void, but that all things are full of bodies, so that even "place" itself is not just an abstract idea, but is itself corporeal <note>The text argues that for "place" to exist, it must have a physical or substantial reality; there is no such thing as a "nothing" that contains "something."</note>.

Since these things are so, we must now speak of **sense**. Sense and the soul seem to follow a similar logic, for both appear to be forged from the same origins. But these two differ greatly from one another. It is manifest that sense cannot exist without a soul, yet the soul can indeed remain vital even without sense. 

->**On the Soul and Sense**<-

Sense only occurs when the soul has been placed within a body. Yet the soul can exist even without sense. For the soul’s sense is even that which belongs to the **mind** <note>original: "mentis"; referring to the higher rational and spiritual faculty.</note>, which is granted universally to the whole world and to everything that is within it. 

<margin>The soul is the source of sense, but the mind is the soul's highest faculty.</margin>

For sense is not like intelligence, nor is intelligence like sense. This is because sense deals with those things that are in the physical world, whereas intelligence <note>original: "intellegentia"; often used to translate the Greek *noesis*, or direct spiritual intuition.</note> deals with those things that are beyond the world—namely, the divine and the intelligible.

Sense is a thing of the body, whereas the mind is a thing of the soul. Therefore, the soul, which is incorporeal, uses the body as an instrument for sense so that it might perceive the things that are corporeal. 

<summary>Hermes explains that the universe is entirely full of substance and then distinguishes between "sense," which requires a physical body, and "intelligence," which allows the soul to perceive divine realities beyond the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>sense, intelligence, soul, corporeal place, Hermetica, Asclepius, mind, spiritual perception</keywords>

II                                                                    57

The **Form** therefore remains: producing from itself as many and as diverse images as there are moments in the rotation of the world. For while the world changes in its rotation, the **Form** <note>original: "species"; in this Platonic context, it refers to the eternal, unchanging archetypes of things.</note> neither changes nor rotates. Thus the forms of individual kinds are permanent, yet they appear dissimilar to one another within that same form. **ASCLEPIUS.** And does the world change its form, O Trismegistus? **TRISMEGISTUS.** You see then, O Asclepius, that all these things have been told to you as if you were dreaming. For what is the world, or of what does it consist, if not of all things that are born? **ASCLEPIUS.** Then you mean to speak of the heaven, the earth, and the elements. **TRISMEGISTUS.** Indeed, for other things change their appearance more frequently: the heaven becoming moist or dry, cold or fiery, clear or murky. These are the shifting appearances within the single form of the heaven. The earth, truly, always has many changes in its appearance: both when it brings forth fruits and when it nourishes its offspring; when it yields the various and diverse qualities and quantities of all fruits, as well as the positions and growths of all the qualities, scents, tastes, and types of trees, flowers, and berries. Fire also undergoes many and divine transformations; for the sun and the moon have images of every shape, which are like the likenesses in our mirrors, reflecting images with a rivaling splendor. But let enough have been said of such things.

The **Twelfth** <note>original: "Vodecimum," likely a printer's error for "Duodecimum" (12th).</note> discusses: that empty and void space has no place in reality; that **demons** <note>In the Greek sense, "daimones" are intermediary spirits, not necessarily evil.</note> dwell with us around the earth, but are imperceptible to us due to their extreme subtlety. That **Heroes** <note>Spirits of the deceased who have attained a higher state.</note> occupy a purer portion of the air. That the name of "place" is an empty abstraction when taken alone, because unless it exists in relation to something, the nature of "place" cannot be understood; and this is common to all relative terms. He affirms the **intelligible world** <note>original: "intelligibilem mundum"; the realm of pure thought and spirit.</note>, by which the **sensible world** (and all things within it) is covered as if by a garment, inasmuch as it is contained within the intelligible world. He states that the forms of each of the uniform kinds are dissimilar, so that man... <note>The sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

[Main text]
...of things, and this according to the moments and hours of the circle: over which the **Pantomorphos** <note>original: "pantomorphus"; literally "all-formed." In Hermetic thought, this is an entity or astrological power that gives shape to all things.</note> presides, whom he previously said was the god of horoscopes. We, however, are ignorant of that god; we leave him to the folly of the Gentiles, if there are any who still remain so clearly foolish and delirious. But for us, according to the sentiment of the most blessed Paul, *there is one God the Father, from whom are all things, and we in Him; and one Lord Jesus Christ, through whom are all things, and we through Him.* <note>The author is quoting 1 Corinthians 8:6 to provide a Christian correction to the Hermetic text.</note> He places the species and forms—that is, the **Ideas** (about which so much has been written by Plato and the Platonists)—as immutable and unvarying within the **Pantomorphos**, which takes its name from being, as it were, "all-formed" and "all-ideal." From these, at individual hours and moments, he shapes individual things; the form remains the same, but he forms them in various ways. He claims that all other things are mutable: the world, the sky, and the earth. Truly, there may be those who would relegate the **Pantomorphos** of Mercury and the **Idea** of Plato to the realm of fictions. This is the summary of the twelfth chapter.

But let us return to man and to reason, by which divine gift man is called a "rational animal." For while the things said about man are wonders (even if they are to be marveled at), the greatest of all wonders exceeds all admiration: that man was able to discover the divine nature and to make it. <note>This is the famous and controversial "god-making" passage of the *Asclepius*, where Hermes claims humans can create gods by animating statues.</note> Since our ancestors erred greatly concerning the nature of the gods, being incredulous and not paying heed to worship and divine religion, they discovered an art by which they could make gods. <meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and his disciple Asclepius. The previous page concluded Chapter 12 and began Chapter 13, discussing the nature of form and divinity. This section focuses on the "earthly gods"—statues or idols animated by human ritual—and the controversial Hermetic claim that humanity acts as a creator of gods.</meta>

II                                                                    58

...entirely in the sense of life, and having become better, he returned to heaven. Even now, he provides assistance to sick men through his divine power, providing the same aid by his divinity that he was previously accustomed to offer through the art of medicine. <note>Hermes is referring to the deified human ancestor Asclepius (Imhotep), the legendary physician who was believed to continue healing the sick from the divine realm.</note> Does not Hermes, whose ancestral name I bear, dwelling in his namesake home, <note>Referring to Hermopolis in Egypt.</note> help and preserve all mortals who come to him from everywhere? And Isis, the wife of Osiris—we know how many goods she provides when she is favorable, and how much she can harm when she is angry. For it is easy for earthly and worldly gods to be moved to anger, inasmuch as they are made and composed by men out of a nature that is external to them. <note>Hermes distinguishes between "celestial gods" (stars/planets) and "earthly gods" (statues inhabited by spirits).</note> This is why it happens that these are called "holy animals" by the Egyptians, and why the souls of those who consecrated them while they were living are worshipped throughout their individual cities, so that they live according to their laws and are called by their names. For this reason, O Asclepius, those things which seem worthy of worship and veneration to some are held differently by others; and because of this, the cities of the Egyptians are accustomed to provoke one another with wars. <note>A reference to the religious conflicts between Egyptian nomes/cities over local animal cults, a common theme in Roman-era critiques of Egypt.</note>

**ASCLEPIUS.** And of these gods, O Trismegistus, who are considered "earthly"—what is their nature?

**TRISMEGISTUS.** It consists, O Asclepius, of herbs, stones, and aromatics that possess a natural power of divinity within themselves. And for this reason, they are delighted by frequent sacrifices, hymns, and praises, with the sweetest sounds singing together in the manner of celestial harmony. The goal is that the celestial element—enticed by this celestial practice and frequent visitation—might endure happily within the idols, being patient with humanity, for a long period of time. **In this way, man is the maker of gods.** <note>original: "sic deorum fictor est homo." This is one of the most famous and controversial lines in the Hermetic corpus, suggesting that humans can "draw down" divine spirits into statues to create physical conduits for the divine.</note> 

And do not think, O Asclepius, that the effects of these earthly gods are merely accidental. The celestial gods inhabit the highest celestial realms, each fulfilling and guarding the order he has received. But these gods of ours help with human affairs as if by a friendly kinship: looking after certain things individually, predicting certain things by lots and divination, and foreseeing others, aiding us according to the proper measure.

                                                                      these

<summary>In this passage, Hermes Trismegistus explains the nature of "earthly gods" (statues or idols), famously claiming that "man is the maker of gods" by using rituals and natural materials to anchor divine spirits to earth. He contrasts these accessible, local deities—who can be moved to anger or kindness—with the distant and immutable celestial gods.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the Latin translation of the Corpus Hermeticum, specifically focusing on the transition between Chapters 13 and 14 of the Asclepius. The previous page discussed the animation of statues (earthly gods). This page opens with a sharp Christian critique of those practices—likely a commentary by the editor, Jacques Lefèvre d’Étaples—before returning to the dialogue to discuss the nature of Fate, Necessity, and Order.</meta>

HER.
This thirteenth chapter suffers from an impurity similar to that of the ninth chapter. In that which Hermes <note>The text uses "Mercurius," the Roman name for Hermes.</note> wishes for man to be most enduring and most admirable, in this very thing man is surely most impious and profane: namely, because he invented idolatry, because he placed summoned demonic spirits into images, and added rites and sacrifices. [This chapter speaks] of Asclepius, Mercury, Isis, and Osiris, who were worshipped by the Egyptians; of the animals which the Egyptians held sacred; of the herbs, stones, and aromatics, the harmonies and hymns, with which they might propitiate those spirits placed into statues and images. Some practitioners of the magical arts <note>Original: "phitonici," referring to those possessed by a "python" or divining spirit; soothsayers.</note> (O unhappy age!) still tend to do this, thinking they hold spirits enclosed in rings or small vessels—a most impure race of men, hostile to God and humanity, and established as an opposing enemy. Against this profane error of impiety, the blessed Augustine acts—and acts rightly—in his book *The City of God*, where he convicts it in the name of truth. <note>St. Augustine famously critiqued this specific passage of the *Asclepius* in *City of God*, Book VIII, condemning the "god-making" as demonic.</note> These things are for the thirteenth chapter.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;¶ Chapter XIIII

ASCLEPIUS. What part of reason, then, do the Destinies <note>Original Greek: "ειμαρμεναι" (heimarmenai), the personification of inescapable fate.</note> or Fates inhabit, O Trismegistus? If the celestial gods rule over the universe and the earthly gods inhabit individual things, what is this that we call Destiny? <note>Original Greek: "ειμαρμερην" (heimarmenen).</note>

TRISMEGISTUS. O Asclepius, it is the necessity of all things that are done, always joined to itself by chained links. This, therefore, is either the maker of things, or the supreme God, or the power from that very God who was made the "second god," <note>In Hermetic philosophy, the "second god" is often the Cosmos itself or the Sun.</note> or it is the discipline of all celestial and earthly things established by divine laws. This Destiny and Necessity, therefore, are both connected to each other by an inseparable glue; of these, the former, Destiny, brings forth the beginnings of all things, while Necessity compels toward their effect those things which hang from those origins. Order follows these—that is, the weaving together and the arrangement of time for the completion of things. For nothing exists without the composition of order. In all these things, this world is perfect. For the world itself is carried by order, or rather, it consists entirely of order. These three, therefore—Destiny (that is, Fate), Necessity, and Order—are especially brought about by the will of God, who...

<summary>This page contains a Christian condemnation of Hermetic "god-making" (idolatry) citing St. Augustine, followed by a philosophical dialogue defining Destiny, Necessity, and Order as the governing forces of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Idolatry, St. Augustine, Fate (Heimarmene), Necessity, Order, Earthly Gods, Celestial Gods, Hermeticism, Demonology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the cosmic order within the Asclepius. Having previously discussed the "earthly gods" (statues), the text now moves into Chapter 14, where Hermes explains the mechanical and divine laws that govern the universe: Fate, Necessity, and Order. The commentary identifies these with the Greek concepts of the Three Fates but notes Hermes’ more philosophical approach compared to common pagan myths.</meta>

[unclear]... celestial gods are in... ASCLEPIUS... through the books... divider... second... and of those... and so these... individual... of things... [effected]... follows... in per-... -sition... &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;II&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;59

[God] governs the world by his own law and divine reason. From these, therefore, all wishing and not-wishing is entirely turned away by divine power; for they are neither moved by anger nor bent by favor, but serve the necessity of eternal reason. This eternity is unswervable, immovable, and indissoluble. The first, therefore, is **Fate** <note>original Greek: "ειμαρμερη" (Heimarmene), the concept of ordained destiny or the "chain of causes."</note>, which, like a sown seed, contains the offspring of all things to come. Necessity follows, by which all things are driven to effect by force. The third order preserves the **fabric** <note>original: "textum," suggesting a woven structure or the "texture" of reality.</note> of those things which Fate and Necessity have arranged. This, therefore, is eternity: which neither begins to be nor shall cease, which is fixed by an immutable law of its course, and is preserved by everlasting motion. It rises and falls through its parts often and alternately, so that as the times are changed, it rises through those same parts in which it had fallen. For such is the nature of circular motion, with all things so tightened within themselves that you may not know what the beginning of the rotation is, since all things always seem both to precede and to follow themselves. Chance and luck, however, are mixed into all worldly things.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Concerning the fourteenth chapter, on the Fates: which he calls Fate <note>original: "imarmenen"</note>, Necessity, and Order; others call them Clotho, Lachesis, and Atropos <note>The three "Moirai" or Fates of Greek mythology who spin, measure, and cut the thread of life.</note>. Concerning these, the pagans used to be no less foolish than they were concerning the rest of the gods they imagined for themselves, inventing new and strangely powerful deities. But Hermes <note>The text uses "Mercurius."</note> speaks of these more cautiously and more sparingly, and as if ambiguously. It is as if these three were: the fore-definition and predestination of the divine mind; which is followed by the necessity of things so that they may exist; and finally, once they exist, order preserves them, just as it has been decreed by divine and eternal law. Thus, the first fate is divine fore-definition. The second is the sequence of things. The third is preservation. And these three are either the divine law itself, or the consequences following the sequence of the divine law. They contain the order of defined causes and those which act according to nature. Yet Fate also permits indefinite, unnatural, and undetermined causes in contingent things. But let others judge concerning these. And these things are for the present division.

->**Chapter XV**<-
h iij

It explains the circular nature of time and eternity, where the beginning and end of cosmic motion are indistinguishable.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the theoretical explanation of Fate and Necessity (the "Three Fates") into the conclusion of the dialogue. Hermes Trismegistus leads his students, Asclepius and Tat, from the sanctuary to offer a prayer of thanksgiving. The text highlights a core Hermetic principle: that God, being the source of all things, requires no material sacrifices like incense, but only the "incense" of human gratitude and intellectual recognition.</meta>

MERCURY.
We have spoken of individual matters as far as human nature was able, as the divine willed and permitted. This alone remains for us: that, blessing God and praying, we return to the care of the body. For having sufficiently discussed divine things, we have filled the mind as if with the food of the soul. 

<margin>The
Pious
Prayer
of
Hermes</margin>

Indeed, having come out from the inner sanctuary <note>original Latin: "adyto," the most sacred part of a temple where only priests could go.</note>, when they began to pray to God, they were looking toward the south. For at sunset, when anyone wishes to pray to God, they should face that way; just as at sunrise, one faces toward that which is called the east <term>Subsolanus</term> <note>An ancient term for the east wind or the direction of the rising sun.</note>. 

Now as they were already saying the prayer, Asclepius said in a low voice:
"O Tat, let us suggest to your father that he command us to say the prayer to God with the addition of incense and perfumes."

Trismegistus, hearing this and being moved, said:
"Better, speak better omens <note>original: "melius ominare," an expression used to correct someone who has said something spiritually inappropriate or inauspicious.</note>, O Asclepius! For this is similar to sacrilege: when you petition God, to burn incense and other things. For nothing is lacking to Him who is Himself all things, or in whom all things are. But let us worship by giving thanks. For these are the highest offerings <note>original: "incensiones," literally "burnings" or "incense," here used metaphorically.</note> to God: when thanks are given by mortals."

"We give thanks to You, O Most High and most Excellent. For by Your grace, we have attained so great a light of Your knowledge. A name holy and to be honored; the one name by which God alone is to be blessed according to the ancestral religion. 

Since You deign to offer to all a father’s piety, religion, and love—and whatever efficacy is even sweeter—when You gift us with sense, reason, and intelligence. Sense, that we may know You; reason, that we may track You through our inklings <note>original: "suspicionibus," suggesting following a trail or a scent toward a conclusion.</note>; intelligence, that knowing You through true cognition, we may rejoice. 

And saved by Your divine power, let us rejoice because You have shown Yourself to us in Your fullness. Let us rejoice that, while we are still situated in our bodies, You have deigned to consecrate us to eternity. For this is the only human thanksgiving: the knowledge of Your majesty. We have known You, the greatest light, perceived by the intellect alone..."

<summary>Hermes concludes his teaching by leading a prayer, rebuking Asclepius for suggesting the use of material incense, and asserting that the only true sacrifice to God is the light of human knowledge and gratitude.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, prayer, thanksgiving, ritual direction, incense, spiritual knowledge, intellect, salvation</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the "Asclepius" dialogue. The previous page ended with the group standing in the sanctuary, preparing to offer a prayer of thanks to God, having rejected the use of physical incense in favor of intellectual and emotional gratitude.</meta>

II                                                                                               60

...through our senses we have understood you: O true way of life, O fertile source of all natures, we have known you: you who are most full of the conception of all nature, we have known you: eternal perseverance. For in this whole prayer, as we adore the good of your goodness, we ask only this: that you wish to keep us persevering in the love of your knowledge, and that we may never be separated from this way of life. Having prayed for these things, we turn ourselves to a pure meal without animals <note>original Latin: "sine animalibus cenam." This refers to a vegetarian meal, which was a common practice among ancient spiritual initiates to maintain ritual and physical purity before and after sacred rites.</note>.

¶ The fifteenth chapter: it finishes the religious dialogue in the giving of thanks. With the adoration finished and thanks rendered to God: Mercury, Asclepius, Ammon, and Tat—the four men who had filled the sacred sanctuary <term>adytum</term> <note>The innermost, most sacred chamber of a temple, usually reserved for the highest priests or divine encounters.</note>—following the religious rite of the Egyptians, they turn themselves to a pure and meatless meal. And here is the end of the little books of Hermes on the will of God.

<meta>This page begins a new work, the "Crater Hermetis" (The Mixing Bowl of Hermes), by the 15th-century Christian Hermeticist Lodovico Lazzarelli. It follows the conclusion of the ancient "Pimander" and "Asclepius" dialogues from the previous page, signaling a transition from ancient Hermetic texts to a Renaissance Christian interpretation of those same mysteries.</meta>

# THE MIXING BOWL
## A dialogue by Lodovico Lazzarelli, a Christian Poet, to King Ferdinand, titled the Mixing Bowl of Hermes <term>Crater Hermetis</term> <note>original: "Crater Hermetis." The "Crater" or Mixing Bowl refers to a vessel sent down by God filled with Mind, into which seekers must dip themselves to achieve enlightenment, a concept found in the fourth book of the Corpus Hermeticum.</note>.

When I had long hesitated within myself—among so many diverse and discordant opinions of both the ancients and the moderns—about which way led to life, or by what reasoning a person's wavering and uncertain faith regarding the attainment of happiness could be strengthened in this life; and while, in this frequent anxiety, I poured out prayerful words to God, shedding frequent groans from my breast and true tears from my eyes: there was finally no lack of help from heaven. It came from him who stretched out a helping hand to the shipwrecked Peter <note>A reference to the Gospel of Matthew (14:31), where Jesus rescues the apostle Peter from drowning in the Sea of Galilee.</note>; who created the world before the ages began; who restored through his own offering the world that had been brought low by sins; who, as the Angel of Great Counsel <note>original: "magni consilij angelus." A traditional title for Christ derived from the Greek version of Isaiah 9:6.</note>, illuminated minds with true light; who, as the Prince of Peace, reconciled man to God; whom we profess as true God and true man, and whom we await as the Father of the age to come and as the Judge. 

He who was the Shepherd <term>Pimander</term> <note>original: "Pimander." This refers to the "Poimandres" or "Divine Mind" who reveals the secrets of the universe to Hermes Trismegistus in the first book of the Hermetic corpus. Lazzarelli explicitly identifies this figure with Jesus Christ.</note> in the mind of Hermes, in me is Christ Je...

<summary>This page introduces Lodovico Lazzarelli’s "Crater Hermetis," a Renaissance work that attempts to harmonize Christian theology with the ancient teachings of Hermes Trismegistus. The author describes a spiritual crisis of doubt that was resolved by a divine revelation, identifying the Hermetic "Pimander" as the Christian Christ.</summary>

<keywords>Lodovico Lazzarelli, King Ferdinand of Naples, Crater Hermetis, Pimander, Christian Hermeticism, Peter the Apostle, Angel of Great Counsel, spiritual anxiety, Renaissance philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory dialogue between Lodovico Lazzarelli and King Ferdinand. Lazzarelli has just introduced himself as a follower of the "Shepherd" (Pimander) and is now discussing the origins of wisdom and its movement through ancient civilizations.</meta>

HER: 61

...to one asking what the path to happiness might be, Delphic Apollo replied: "If you know yourself," and inscribed on the front of his <margin>Apollo.</margin> temple: "Know thyself" <note>original: "noſce teipſum." This famous Greek maxim, *gnōthi seauton*, was considered the foundation of all wisdom in the ancient world.</note>. Then the KING said: "So, you claim we should believe Delphic Apollo?" LAZARRELLI: "Not always; but when he expresses things consistent with the truth. For he brought forth many true things in his oracles. If you run through Porphyry's <margin>Porphyry</margin> <note>Porphyry (c. 234–305 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote a work titled *On the Philosophy of Oracles*.</note> *Philosophy of Oracles*, you will find for certain that this is so. But leaving other things aside, I will bring forward here an oracle he gave regarding the blessed life, which I could produce written as a Greek poem; but striving for brevity, I will express it in Latin [English]:

->That path is exceedingly hard, closed with bronze gates,<-
->Which opens life to us and grants a blessed one.<-
->Nor indeed can it be easily told in any words,<-
->Or explained: to where that path leads.<-
->Which the first among all, those who drink the waters of the Nile,<-
->Began to hand down; from there to the Phoenicians,<-
->And the Assyrians next had that care. But far more famous,<-
->The Hebrew race knew it and received what was known.<-

Is there anything of truth in this oracle?" THE KING: "So it seems. But tell me, I pray: did the Egyptians taste anything of the truth?" LAZARRELLI: "They did not just taste it; they were almost steeped in it. But setting others aside, what shall we say of Hermes? Who, having searched out every path of wisdom, left to posterity monuments regarding true wisdom—in a speech perhaps small in words, but immense in its meanings. From which some conjecture that wisdom migrated to the Hebrews. For they think that Moses, a Hebrew born among the Egyptians, transferred it from Egypt to the Hebrews through the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible, traditionally attributed to Moses.</note>. And we read of him in the Acts of the Apostles <note>Acts 7:22: "And Moses was learned in all the wisdom of the Egyptians, and was mighty in words and in deeds."</note> that he was most learned in all the teachings of the Egyptians." THE KING: "You seem to be a follower of Hermes, Lazzarelli, for you extol him with such praise that it would seem no one was wiser." LAZARRELLI: "I am a Christian, O King; and Her[mes]..."

<summary>Lazzarelli cites a pagan oracle and the philosopher Porphyry to argue that ancient wisdom—including the command to "Know Thyself"—originated with the Egyptians and was passed to the Hebrews via Moses.</summary>
<keywords>Apollo, Porphyry, Know Thyself, Egypt, Moses, Phoenicians, Assyrians, Pentateuch, Hermeticism, Christian Hermeticism</keywords>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous page, where Lazzarelli is convincing King Ferdinand of Naples that ancient Egyptian wisdom—specifically that of Hermes Trismegistus—is not only compatible with Christianity but actually anticipates its core doctrines, such as the Trinity.</meta>

LAZZARELLI <note>The heading 'CRA.' refers to the title of the work, *Crater Hermetis*, but the speaker is Lazzarelli.</note>
...and it is no shame to be a follower of Hermes at the same time. For if you consider his precepts, you will confirm that they do not shrink from Christian doctrine. This is that man, O best of kings, whom the ancient poets called the son of Maia <note>In mythology, Hermes (Mercury) was the son of Zeus and Maia. Here, Lazzarelli is blending the mythical god with the "historical" Egyptian sage Hermes Trismegistus.</note>, the interpreter of the gods, the god of eloquence, the inventor of the lyre, and one perfected in many duties. From him, all ancient theology drew its origin. For even if we remain silent about the many books of his that have perished, what can be found more divine than those little books we have in our hands? In these, he expressed the Trinity of God so perfectly that he who understands him rejoices to have found the truth. Therefore, I want this discourse of ours to be called the *Crater of Hermes* <term>Crater: Greek for a mixing bowl; here it refers to a vessel of spiritual transformation and the title of this book.</term>. For whatever we investigate here concerning true happiness, we shall draw both from Gospel doctrine and from the precepts of Hermes.

REX <note>The King: Ferdinand I of Naples.</note>
Proceed then, for I burn to understand that which you have already promised to bring forth. I hope that since I am a Christian, I may at the same time be made a follower of Hermes along with you.

LAZARRELLI
Now, O King, just ask: what do you most desire to hear first?

REX
We must hurry to the matter itself, casting aside all superfluities. Tell me, therefore, by what means I am able to know myself.

LAZARRELLI
To one who asked this, Pimander <note>Pimander, or Poimandres, is the "Shepherd of Men," a divine entity representing the Divine Mind who reveals secrets to Hermes in the *Corpus Hermeticum*.</note> replied: "Embrace me with your mind, and I will instruct you in all things that you desire." Truth itself [Christ] also said: "Without me you can do nothing." <note>original: "ſine me nihil poteſtis facere" (John 15:5).</note> It is also said by the prophet: "In your light we shall see light." <note>original: "in lumine tuo videbimus lumen" (Psalm 36:9).</note> So that this light may illuminate us, we shall first pray to God. For the ancients taught that one must pray at the beginning of every matter; but Dionysius the Areopagite <margin>Dionysius</margin> <note>Dionysius was a 5th-century Christian Neoplatonist whose works on mystical theology were highly influential in the Renaissance.</note> commanded that this must be carried out in theology, specifically in the book he published *On the Divine Names*. Since we must discuss the secrets of theology here, we will pray at the start. You, however, be present and favor us with your spirit while I invoke God thus:

->You who sit above the Cherubim,<-
->You who reside on the highest throne,<-

<summary>Lazzarelli defends the compatibility of Hermetic and Christian thought, proposing that his dialogue be titled the "Crater of Hermes," and prepares the King for a theological discussion on self-knowledge through prayer.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Trinity, Crater Hermetis, Pimander, self-knowledge, Dionysius the Areopagite, King Ferdinand, Christian theology</keywords>

[Header:] HER. <note>original: "HER." Likely an abbreviation for Hermes, indicating that these verses are spoken by the character representing Hermetic wisdom, or perhaps a section title for the "Hymn of Hermes."</note>

And you judge all things rightly,
You who dwell in the heavens:
I now direct my prayers to you
With word and heart submissive.
Invincible God of hosts,
Father of both gods and men,
Lend your holy ears to me.
Do not, I pray, move far away
The rays of your splendor.
You, King—you, God of Israel,
Descend: and this, your house,
Fill with white light.
Make it a worthy dwelling;
Purge the darkness; drive
From me the hellish plague: <note>original: "tartáream luem." In classical mythology, Tartarus was the deepest abyss of the underworld; here, it signifies the "plague" of sin or spiritual darkness.</note>
Cleanse this image <note>The "image" refers to the human soul, created in the *Imago Dei* (image of God), which the speaker seeks to restore to its original purity.</note> thoroughly. <margin><unclear>unclear handwritten text</unclear></margin>
As vapor is drawn up by the sun,
As the magnet draws steel:
So may I be snatched up by your flames.
Join yourself to me, Father:
Soon I shall be drawn deep into you,
And we shall be made one together.
As the moon shines with the sun’s rays,
Having drunk in their glittering light:
So may I glow by your countenance.
Take away the tree of good and evil,
Recall my wandering feet:
May I be nourished by the Tree of Life. <note>The poet contrasts the "Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil," which led to the Fall, with the "Tree of Life," representing the restoration of the soul through divine wisdom.</note>
So that with you I may enjoy
The inviolable and eternal good:
And never be turned back.
Indeed, let me enjoy you deep within:

<summary>This page contains a mystical hymn or prayer addressed to the "God of Israel," blending Biblical imagery with Hermetic themes of spiritual ascent and union with the Divine. It asks for the purification of the soul—the "image"—so that it might be drawn into God like steel to a magnet.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the poetic "Hymn of Hermes" from the previous page. While the first half of the hymn was a petition from the soul to God, this section shifts to a divine response. It reflects Ludovico Lazzarelli’s unique "Christian Hermeticism," where the Hermetic rebirth of the soul is described using the language of Christ’s baptism and cosmic sovereignty.</meta>

CRA. <note>original: "CRA." Abbreviation for *Crater* (The Mixing Bowl), the title of the work and a symbol of the divine vessel of wisdom, or perhaps indicating a shift to the "response" of the divine voice.</note>

You who alone are good, the Good
Beside which nothing is good,
Grant that by your light
I may see your light,
And reflect it back to your own.
My impiety terrifies me,
But your greater mercy <note>original: "pietas." While often translated as "piety" or "duty," in this context it refers to God's tender mercy or loving-kindness toward humanity.</note>
Cries out: "Do not fear; come,
I am here, justifying the wicked,
Pitying the human race,
Lifting the weight of sin."
To know God is the only salvation;
It is the only justification,
And the only path to the heavens.
Behold, you are my son:
In whom I am well-pleased. <note>This is a direct citation of the words spoken by God the Father at the Baptism of Jesus (Matthew 3:17), here applied to the soul that has achieved spiritual rebirth.</note>
Be bold and judge all things.
Behold, I give my words to you:
Which make new suns
And renew the vast sea.
Let the clouds obey you:
Let the fish and birds alike
Tremble at your voice.
Plant the starry dwellings:
Build the foundations of the earth;
Let Zion call you Lord.
And let the mountains of Sinai and Tabor,
And the swift waters of the Jordan:
Bear witness to the one born of God.
Behold, soon my kingdom comes,
With Christ as the sovereign leader: <note>original: "impéríta duce." Literally "un-ruled" or "sovereign," suggesting a leader who is not subject to any earthly power.</note>
Who hallows the remaining saints.

<summary>This page concludes the mystical hymn with a divine response, where God recognizes the initiate as a "son" and grants him sovereign power over creation. It blends Hermetic concepts of the "Divine Man" with the Christian imagery of the Baptism of Christ and the Transfiguration.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue within the *Crater Hermetis*. HER                                                                                                   63

Alas, may your own lot examine you;
Let a clinging faith suffice:
Which wisdom binds together.
I will rejoice in my Lord:
Who has established His servant 
As His King upon the throne of David.

The prayer is finished: now, best of Kings, repeat what you desire me to explain now. **THE KING**. This prayer of yours, so filled with sacred sentiments, has so captivated my mind that I wish first to understand many things said in it by you. **LAZZARELLI**. When we have reached the end of the discourse we have begun, you will clearly understand both those things and many others. But now let us press on with what we have started: so that you may be recalled to it, I will repeat what was touched upon earlier. But lest I seem to have taken my beginning from an oracle of Apollo <note>Lazzarelli is distancing himself from pagan "oracles" to emphasize that his teaching comes from a structured philosophical lineage—the "Hermetic doctrine"—which he sees as compatible with Christianity.</note>, but rather from the doctrine of Hermes: take it thus. All things having been created from the beginning: Hermes asserts that God cried out thus. <margin>Hermes</margin> "Sprout forth, grow, multiply: all my seeds and my works. You moreover, to whom a portion of Mind <note>original: "mentis." In Hermetic literature, this refers to *Nous* (Divine Intellect), which is granted only to some humans, enabling them to know God.</note> has been granted: recognize your race and consider your immortal nature. Know that the love of the body is the cause of death." This corresponds to the sentiment of Moses in the book of Genesis. <margin>Moses</margin> For in these words of Hermes, both the tree of life in which we live, and the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, which brings us destruction, are contained. The main point <note>original: "caput." Literally "the head" or "source."</note> of this is the precept (as is easily seen): that we should know ourselves. **THE KING**. I have been brought back to the question I had made. First indeed I desire to be taught by you, if the opportunity is there: what was the tree of life, and what the tree of knowledge of good and evil? For regarding this, either almost no explanation is found among the sacred doctors <note>The "sacred doctors" refers to the established theologians of the Church.</note>, or one that is very obscured. So much so that I am amazed that this—upon which the ruin of our whole race depends, and through which the entire tradition of sacred scripture is founded—remains completely unknown <note>The sentence cuts off here and continues on the next page.</note>

<summary>Lazzarelli links the creation account of Hermes Trismegistus with the Genesis account of Moses, arguing that the "love of the body" is the cause of death. The King asks for a deeper explanation of the Tree of Life and the Tree of Knowledge, noting that traditional theologians have not fully explained them.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Moses, Genesis, Tree of Life, Tree of Knowledge, Self-Knowledge, Mind (Nous), Original Sin, Prisca Theologia</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between King Ferdinand of Aragon (CRA.) and the initiate Ludovico Lazzarelli (LAZ.). They are debating the nature of the Garden of Eden and the Fall of Man, specifically whether the "Tree of Knowledge" was a physical object or an allegorical symbol, while reconciling Hermetic thought with Christian scripture.</meta>

->CRA.<-
...remains undefined. I add that the command given to Adam—in whom we existed by origin—is poured into us, his race, by derivation, so that the same danger of disobedience looms over us. For we cannot avoid what we do not know. Indeed, we read in the sacred writings that the wood of that tree (just like other trees) was physical. Yet, of what species that tree was, we do not read. Nor do we read that the command was revoked by God even after the transgression.

->LAZ.<-
I, however, have read an opinion of Philo of Alexandria <note>Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE) was a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who used allegory to fuse Greek philosophy with Hebrew scripture.</note> in his second book <margin>Philo.</margin> *On Agriculture*, O King, which is contrary to the one you just mentioned. It goes as follows: "Following what was said before is the cultivation of the plants of Paradise. For it is said: 'The Lord planted a paradise in Eden toward the East, and placed there the man whom he had formed.' Therefore, to think these were vines or olive trees, or pomegranates, or similar trees, is a great and incurable folly." 

Now, this Philo was a Hebrew, and most wise. And as Jerome <margin>Jerome.</margin> asserts in his book *On Illustrious Men*, he was dear through his close friendship in the times of the Apostles to Peter <margin>Peter.</margin>, the prince of the Apostles, and to Mark <margin>Mark.</margin> the Evangelist. Thus, this entire investigation concerning the First Philosophy <term>First Philosophy: A traditional term for Metaphysics—the study of the fundamental nature of reality and being.</term> is established by the oracles of the sacred scriptures. 

However, O King, since you compel me to the investigation of this matter which has remained undefined by the speakers of divine things (since human consideration is often deceived regarding divine matters), I wish only that to be asserted by us—in this matter and others that follow—which is approved by the Church and the choir of the faithful. For if Plato <margin>Plato.</margin> in the *Laws* commanded that nothing be changed of those things received from Delphi, or Dodona, or Ammon <note>These were famous oracular sites of the ancient world (Apollo at Delphi, Zeus at Dodona and Ammon) where people sought divine guidance.</note>—as certain ancient speeches brought forth through visions and inspirations of the gods—how much less should those things be changed which were handed down to us through the prophets and the elect of the true God, indeed through JESUS Himself, true God and man...

<summary>King Ferdinand and Lazzarelli debate the nature of the Tree of Knowledge, with Lazzarelli citing Philo of Alexandria to argue for an allegorical rather than literal interpretation. Lazzarelli emphasizes that his mystical investigations remain submissive to the authority of the Church.</summary>
<keywords>Philo of Alexandria, Garden of Eden, Tree of Knowledge, Allegory, Saint Jerome, First Philosophy, Plato, Oracles, Authority of the Church</keywords>
