[Main Text]
[...we] ought to [believe]. If he <note>Lazzarelli is referring to Plato here.</note> also advises in the *Timaeus* <note>original: "Thimeo"; Plato's famous dialogue on the nature of the physical world and the soul.</note>: that it is impossible not to have faith in those whom he calls the sons of the gods, even if their speech is confirmed neither by necessary nor by probable reasons: how much more should an unshaken and indubitable faith be held in the oracles of JESUS himself, the true God, and in the precepts of his prophets and his chosen ones.

->THE KING<-
Add also, if it pleases you: since we receive many good things from God through the Church and its ministers, it is right to subject all our words and works to Him. Now then, O Lazzarelli: proceed, and carry out what you intended. 

->LAZ.<-
Most often you have heard, and I believe you have read in the sacred oracles, O King: that God created all things for the sake of man, but man for the sake of Himself. 

->THE KING<-
We have heard it often, and we have read it. 

->LAZ.<-
Since, therefore, God had created man for His own sake, so that he might obey the divine will, He placed within him a portion of the divine intellect. This was so that through its discourse, man might contemplate divinity, and by contemplating, draw to himself the substantial rays of splendor. From this, he would obtain both wisdom and eternal life at once—that which is called the **Tree of Life** <note>original: "lignum vite"</note> by Moses, through a parable beloved by contemplatives. 

->THE KING<-
Therefore, O Lazzarelli, you assert that the **Tree of Life** is the contemplation of divine things and wisdom. 

->LAZ.<-
Indeed, I think so—or rather, I have no doubt of it.

->THE KING<-
What benefit then, concerning the happiness of the soul, did man receive from this contemplation? 

->LAZ.<-
No small amount, O King. For besides the happiness of the soul which comes through wisdom, he established himself as a pure and worthy temple in which the spirit of God might dwell, and he possessed the most radiant angels of God as companions of his life. For which reason, he was to have avoided forever, by divine gift, the death which he would have incurred by nature; and whatever he desired by his will, he would immediately attain, and other [things]...

<summary>Lazzarelli argues that the "Tree of Life" mentioned by Moses is an allegory for the contemplation of God and divine wisdom. He explains that through this contemplation, humanity was intended to achieve immortality and live in the company of angels.</summary>

->LAZARELLI<-

Since you understand what the Tree of Life is, you can easily understand for yourself what the tree of the knowledge of good and evil is. For when one of two opposites is explained, the other is also made clear. 

->THE KING<- 
I perceive it by a certain conjecture, but I expect to understand it more clearly from you. 

->LAZ.<- 
Listen then. Just as the love, contemplation, and wisdom of divine things is the "tree of life," so the attachment to and the obsessive study of fleeting and material things can be called the "tree of the knowledge of good and evil." 

->THE KING<- 
That does not quite sit right in my mind. For it is difficult for me to believe, nor can I in any way accept, that God would forbid us to consider the things that He Himself created. For there is no craftsman who would forbid his own work to be seen, considered, or appreciated. And it is even more difficult for me to believe that man would incur death simply because of such consideration. 

->LAZ.<- 
God the Craftsman did not forbid His works to be considered, O King, but rather he forbade us to dwell on them as if they were the ultimate goal. In this way, the ancients said that the heavens, the sun, the moon, the stars, the elements, and even certain animals were gods. But the Almighty wills and commands that all these things be considered through a process of reasoning, so that through certain steps, as it were, our mind might finally reflect back toward Him, and that the human soul may always find rest in the contemplation of His divinity. For the invisible things of God <margin>The Apostle</margin> <note>original: "Apostolus"; referring to St. Paul in Romans 1:20.</note> (as the Apostle says) are clearly seen since the creation of the world, being understood through the things that have been made; as are His eternal power and divinity. And Hermes <margin>Hermes.</margin> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom Lazzarelli attributes the Hermetic writings.</note> says: "Finally, when you wish to see God, look up..."

<summary>Lazzarelli explains that the "Tree of Knowledge" represents an unhealthy attachment to material things as ends in themselves, while the "Tree of Life" represents the contemplation of God. He argues that creation should be used as a ladder to reach the Creator rather than being worshipped as a god.</summary>
<keywords>Tree of Life, Tree of Knowledge, allegory, material world, contemplation, Romans 1:20, Hermes Trismegistus, Neoplatonism</keywords>

She, sent as a gift from Jove, opened a jar <note>original: "ollam." For what else is Pandora—which sounds like "the gift of all"—than the knowledge of material and sensible things? Which, opening the jar of our mind and dwelling in it, made all good things fly away, and only hope remained. For we continually hope that good things will come to us, yet we never achieve the things hoped for. For through her <note>Pandora/Material Knowledge.</note>, we are estranged from the tree of life.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between King Ferdinand, Craton, and Ludovico Lazzarelli. They are discussing the relationship between poetic myths and theological truth, moving from the specific myth of the giants to a broader defense of "sacred fictions" used by both ancient pagans and Christian prophets.</meta>

CRATON.
...servants; but I wished then to allude to the fiction of Hesiod. <note>Hesiod was an ancient Greek poet, often cited alongside Homer as a primary source for myths about the origins of the gods.</note>

THE KING. I cannot truly wonder enough at the way in which the fictions of the poets correspond theologically to the truth.

LAZZARELLI. Cease your wondering, O King. For even Hermes—the ancient prince of theology <note>original: "antique princeps theologie." Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary Egyptian sage believed by Renaissance thinkers to have predicted Christianity.</note>—wished for theological truth to be wrapped in these fictions. Now, however, these fables are presented by everyone in a merely naked fashion. Foreseeing this future, <margin>Hermes</margin> Hermes lamented in this prophecy: "O Egypt, Egypt! Of your religions, only fables shall remain, and these will be equally unbelievable to those who come after; and only words carved into stones shall remain, telling of your pious deeds." <note>This is a famous passage from the *Asclepius*, a text in the Hermetic corpus, mourning the eventual loss of ancient spiritual wisdom.</note> Even the sacred theologians of the Christian religion (whom they call prophets) made use of poetic fictions. You will find this to be the case if you ever run through the oracles of the prophets, about which <margin>Dionysius</margin> Dionysius speaks near the beginning of *The Celestial Hierarchy*: <note>Dionysius the Areopagite was an influential early Christian mystical author who explained why God and angels are described with physical attributes in the Bible.</note> "...lest we too, led astray by the error of the common people, should think everywhere that the celestial spirits endowed with divine appearance have many feet and many faces. Or that they are shaped after the dullness of oxen, or the ferocity of lions, or fashioned in the likeness of the curved beaks of eagles, or the hairy feathering of birds; or that we should stupidly begin to imagine fiery wheels and material thrones above heaven, necessary for the highest divinity to recline upon; or some multicolored horses, and armed attendants and captains, and other things of that kind. These things are handed down to us through most sacred fictions, with an evident and expressed variety of signs." Truly, theology makes abundant use of poetic fictions (to describe those spirits), being not ignorant of the weakness of the human mind (as we have said). Most kindly providing a path that is both suitable and known to the mind, through which it may be carried to higher things, theology lays out paths of this kind for such an ascent in the sacred scriptures, according to the mind's own capacity. Thus far Dionysius, and

<summary>Lazzarelli explains to King Ferdinand that both ancient Egyptian wisdom (Hermes) and Christian scripture (the Prophets) use poetic metaphors to hide and protect divine truths from the common people, citing Dionysius the Areopagite to justify the use of physical imagery for spiritual beings.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue regarding the fall of the soul and the interpretation of Genesis, moving from the nature of the senses to the allegorical meaning of Adam's exile.</meta>

CRAT.
...and desires through the eyes; and through the ears, it brings forth the allurements of voices. Thus, surrounded by a multitude of daughters, the soul is overwhelmed; and then the "sons of God," or the angels, enter unto them <note>A reference to Genesis 6:2, here interpreted allegorically as spiritual forces interacting with human passions</note>. For as long as the pure rays of wisdom shine into the soul—through which we behold God and His virtues—no lying messenger enters our thoughts; instead, those wandering outside are all driven away to the places of purification. But when the light of the mind is dulled and rendered weaker by departing from the "One" <note>The Neoplatonic concept of the absolute source of all being</note>, the companions of darkness act quickly, joining with the effeminate and broken disturbances which Moses called the "daughters of men." These daughters bear fruit for themselves and not for God. The mind, however, is commanded to bear fruit for God and not for itself; just as Abraham fathered his son Isaac for God and not for himself, being prepared to offer him to God as a burnt sacrifice. 

But Adam, abandoning the Tree of Life—that is, the contemplation of the Monad <note>original: "monadis"; the divine unity or the number one</note>—and descending to the Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil—that is, to the pursuit of things perceived by the senses—begot many daughters. Thus, having fallen from justice and dignity, he incurred the destruction of death. <margin>Moses.</margin> Whence Moses brings forth God saying of him: *Behold, Adam has become like one of us, knowing good and evil.* <margin>The Prophet.</margin> And it is said in the Psalms: *Man, when he was in honor, did not understand; he was compared to the foolish beasts and made like unto them.* <margin>Hermes.</margin> And Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the "Hermetica"</note> says in the *Poimandres*: *But man, when he had considered in his Father the procreation of all things, wished himself also to create; wherefore he fell from the contemplation of the Father into the sphere of generation.* And the same author says a little later: *Man existed superior to the Harmony <note>The celestial spheres or the cosmic order</note>; but having fallen into the Harmony, he was made a slave.* 

REX. This interpretation of yours is more pleasing to me than can be believed. For although many pious and excellent interpretations are given, this one, as I seem to see...

He supports this using a synthesis of the Bible, the Psalms, and the Hermetic tradition.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the King and Lazarus regarding the spiritual interpretation of the scriptures, following the discussion of the soul as the life of the text.</meta>

II [handwritten: 55 69]

The soul is the essence of the scriptures. <note>original: "scripturarū est aia [anima]"</note> And a great desire has arisen in me to ask about many things; but I shall refrain, lest we wander too far from our starting point. LAZARUS. You must refrain, O pious King, if we desire to reach the goal of our original intention. For this present discourse is like the Lernaean Hydra: <note>A multi-headed serpent from Greek mythology; when one head was cut off, others grew in its place.</note> when one head is cut off, many more arise and grow in its place. For here too there would be room for questioning: what is Adam? What is Eve? What is the serpent? And many other things of that kind. But setting all these aside, let us return, if you please, to where we left off. KING. That is certainly what must be done—if you will first explain just this one thing: namely, in what way man incurred death through the tasting of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil. LAZARUS. Accept my explanation as best I can give it, O King. Since man was a luminous temple in which the Spirit of the Lord dwelt—whose presence granted him immortality by grace, though not by nature (for he was composed of discordant elements)—the indwelling divine splendor imposed peace upon those elements. But when, through transgression, the Light withdrew in indignation, darkness took the place of light. And so the temple of luminous virtues became a lodging-place for shadows. From this, not only were the reins of elemental discord loosened, but destruction was accelerated by unclean powers. Then, innumerable diseases and old age arose. Hence it is described by Moses that shortly after the transgression, men lived long lives. But when, as time passed, transgression was heaped upon transgression (driven by the darkness), life was naturally shortened. Concerning this matter, Moses asserts that God said: "My spirit shall not remain with man forever, because he is flesh; and his days shall be one hundred and twenty years." <note>A reference to Genesis 6:3.</note> But now, O King—alas for miserable mortals!—the life of man is so contracted that hardly anyone reaches their hundredth year. Indeed—

For daily we all feed upon the forbidden wood <note>A reference to the Tree of Knowledge in Eden</note>. ¶ When men, through various pursuits,
Hinder their own labors in vain, having abandoned God;
When they are unmindful of their own beauty,
Which they previously possessed by the gift of the Father;
When they defile the divine image—once raised toward the heavens—
Beneath the prone shadow of Tartarus <note>The deepest abyss of the underworld in classical mythology</note>.
They consider the slanting shadow to be
Their parent, nor do they remember the nourishing light
Which truly brought them forth and was their nurse.
They have fallen from the dignity of the mind;
They no longer practice good, not even one of them.
No one, pious in mind, seeks God;
No one worships the lawful Parent.
But all, having acknowledged vain idols,
Have surrendered themselves to the vanity of the flesh.
And all, bound by the city of Babylon <note>Symbolizing worldly corruption and exile</note>,
Have deserted the city of holiness.
And as they have drunk the wine of the harlot,
They refuse to give their embrace to the Virgin.
The Virgin Zion cries out, seeking her own,
Mourning as a widow and one destitute:
"Why, enticed by the flattering mouth of the vain harlot,
And abandoning better things,
Have you placed your necks beneath the foot of Death?
Alas! Recognize the lurid poison
That lies hidden beneath this honey-sweet honeycomb;
That bold one pours out unmixed wine for you;"

See, I mix the wines of purity,
Which create virginal, pious foster-children
And sacred ministers of God,
To be placed on the throne of the Kingdom (which hastens near).
Let these be our final lessons:
I pray you, hold them fast within your mind.
By possessing light within the light of Christ,
Keep yourselves away from the light of the foul one.
But though she cries out again, and again, and again,
And like a mother offers her dutiful breasts
Full of milk: each man persists with a deaf ear,
Nor does he pull back his feet.
He lies wallowing in filth like a swine,

### CRA. 70
...by what means we are able to know ourselves. LAZARUS:
Pay attention, then. For just as we cannot recognize the image seen in a mirror or on a coin unless we first discern from whom it results or whom it represents: so too, unless we have known God, we cannot in any way know ourselves. For these things are connected to one another, so that if one is unknown, the other is unknown as well. For we are the image of God, as has been brought forth in the sacred oracles <note>original: "sacris oraculis," referring here to the Holy Scriptures</note>. And although many contend that "to be *at* the image" and "to be *the* image" differ, I nevertheless feel that they signify the same thing. THE KING: Bring forward some testimony on this matter, if you have any. LAZARUS: You command me to wander too far from our beginning; just as we return to the path, you bid us wander again. Yet it is right for you to assent to my speech, even if it be unadorned, if you wish to perceive the secret <note>original: "arcanum," a hidden or mystical truth</note> which I am about to bring forth. For I have worn these things down within myself for a long time; once worn down, I digested them; and once digested, I finally stored them in the treasury of my mind. It is necessary (as is commonly said) for a student to be a believer. THE KING: I do indeed believe. But the more I hear your discourse strengthened by the testimonies of the ancients, the more abundantly my soul is nourished. However, once you have completed this one point, we shall immediately turn our feet back to the path.
LAZARUS: Philo, in his second book *On Agriculture*, <margin>Philo</margin> explaining that saying of Moses, <margin>Moses</margin> "Let us make man in our image and likeness," says this: "But the great Moses did not call the species of human nature similar to anything created. Rather, he called it the image of the divine and invisible God." And Paul, truly, to the Corinthians: <margin>Paul</margin> "A man indeed ought not to cover his head, since he is the image and glory of God." Since, therefore, Moses and Paul spoke through the Holy Spirit, and the Holy Spirit cannot be at odds with itself—and since Moses said "at the image" and Paul said that "man is the image of God"—they undoubtedly wished to signify the same thing. It must also be asserted what Hermes asserts in the *Pymander*: <margin>Hermes</margin> "The Father of all things, the Mind..."

...chosen/life and existing radiance: he procreated a man similar to himself, and rejoiced in him as if in a son. And elsewhere, speaking of God, he says: "Whose name is every name; whose nature is every nature." <note>original: "cuius nomen est omne nomen: cuius natura est omnis natura." This reflects the Hermetic or Neoplatonic idea of God as the source of all things.</note> If, therefore, all of nature is an image of God, how much more so is man, for whose sake all things were made. THE KING. I think it is now right to pass over those things which are best known, and return instead to that which the previous question concerned. For it seems difficult, and beyond the powers of the human mind, to be able to know God, since Truth itself says: "No one knows the Son except the Father, and no one knows the Father except the Son, and anyone to whom the Son chooses to reveal him." <note>original: "nemo nouit filium nisi pater..." You, on the other hand, strengthened by sacred authority, assert that God cannot be known. I, however, do not sit now like a poet upon the tripod of the Muse <note>A reference to the Pythia at Delphi, who sat on a tripod to deliver oracles while in a trance.</note>, so that, lacking my senses, I might freely pour out whatever flows in; but as becomes a Christian, walking daily through the groves and shadows of Mount Zion and through sunny valleys, I have been so instructed by divine precepts that whatever I am about to relate here in your presence, I have first come to know through both reason and open experience. For it is not hidden from me that nothing of those things which we perceive by the senses can be absolutely affirmed of God. For whatever human intelligence defines must necessarily be beneath the infinite God. Therefore, I do not suggest here that we must know God in such a way that we discern what He is like in His separation from things, or what He is like in the total enclosure of Himself within Himself, or what He is like in that extreme and solitary withdrawal into the deepest retreat and darkness of His divinity, or what His substance may be. For this is too difficult—indeed, impossible. For the human intellect alone cannot ascend so high.

HERMES                                                                    71 74

Because Plato attempted this beyond the powers of the human mind, <margin>Plato.</margin> having finally gazed upon the inexpressible excellence of God, he fell into a destructive and deadly error: namely, asserting that God does not mingle with men. <note>Plato's "Timaeus" and "Symposium" suggest a distance between the divine and the mortal, which the speaker here critiques from a Christian perspective.</note> If this were so, unhappy human nature, abandoned to the power of darkness, would waste away. But our entire Christian profession is the opposite; for we profess that God is incarnate, shared with us, and joined to us. Therefore, it is necessary for us to know God in the manner that Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential Christian mystic and philosopher.</note> instructed in his book *On the Divine Names*. <margin>Dionysius</margin> Indeed, he says that all divine things—even those which have been clearly revealed to us—are known only through "participations." <note>original: "participationibus"; the idea that we don't know God's essence, only the ways He shares His attributes with us.</note> Furthermore, what these things are like in their own beginning and their own seat, no sense reaches, no substance or knowledge penetrates. Finally, whether we call that "hidden thing" beyond-substance, or God, or life, or substance, or light, or the Word: we understand nothing other than the participations and virtues emanating from Him into us, by which we are taken up into God, and which bestow upon us either substance, or life, or wisdom. For there are many divine matters (as the same author asserts in *The Ecclesiastical Hierarchy*) which, though unknown to men, have most worthy causes known to the more excellent orders <note>Referring to the ranks of angels.</note>. Likewise, many things are hidden from those highest substances which are manifest only to the one God Himself. For the steps and boundaries of the human condition have been set, which we cannot overstep.

KING. You speak the truth indeed; but I would wish you to teach me in what way it is necessary for us to know God.

LAZARUS. Since God is incomprehensible above all understanding (as Dionysius testifies in his *Mystical Theology*), <margin>Dionysius</margin> inexpressible above all speech, above all "position" and "removal," <note>original: "positionem/ablationem"; technical terms for cataphatic (affirming what God is) and apophatic (denying what God is not) theology.</note> and above all affirmation and negation: it is necessary for us firmly to believe...

->CRAT.<-
<note>Likely an abbreviation for a character name in the dialogue, such as Craton.</note>
...to give and simply profess that God is one in trinity and triune in unity: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. In this matter, even the doctors of the Hebrews agree with us, as is read in the *Bereshit Rabbah* <note>A midrashic commentary on Genesis</note> from Rabbi Moses Hadarshan <note>original: "rabi Mose Adera"; an 11th-century Jewish exegete</note>. God, the principle of all things, light, eternity, and the Holy One, is called *Ab*: that is, Father. God the One, the depth, the wisdom, the fountain from which all things were made, and who was begotten or went forth and is called the first principle, *Ben*: which is interpreted as Son. God, the root of the heart, and the perfection of the will, or the one will of both, is called the congregation of the vision of God <note>referring to the Holy Spirit</note>; and all are of the same perfection, and one with each other, nor do they differ from one another, but all are one. Consider how openly the divine trinity is asserted by these words. Some of the Hebrews, however—whether circumvented by ignorance or driven by malice—most stubbornly deny the trinity in divine matters. We, however, must hold to it most firmly if we wish to follow the truth and attain immortality. And it is necessary for us to believe all those things concerning the divinity which were instituted and defined by the holy fathers through the canons. If we wished to recount those now, we would protract our oration far too long. But what will make us fit and best disposed for grasping the mystery that I am laboring to bring forth in my mind? Surely this: if we meditate on how God is the cause of all things and the creator of all things, and that He created all things after Himself for the sake of that divine and chief animal, namely man himself. For (as Hermes says) <margin>Hermes.</margin> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the Hermetic corpus</note> when God formed man, being delighted by His own form, He granted all works to human use and manifested Himself to him alone among all living beings. Therefore, stunned with admiration, the prophet exclaims: "Lord, what is man that you have made yourself known to him, or the son of man, that you think of him?" <note>A paraphrase of Psalm 144:3</note> To such an extent did God magnify man, that from eternity He descended into time, [and...]

->HER.<-
Taking on the clothing of the flesh, God was made man. God therefore magnified man, the work of his own hands, for he is to be greatly honored; indeed, that supreme Creator is not ignorant of how highly his own handiwork ought to be valued. For just as the heavens, by their own image and light, perform those things which the power of fire cannot accomplish by its natural quality, so God, through his own image—that is, man—perfects those things which the world itself cannot do by its own innate virtue. Thus Moses, thus Joshua, thus Elijah, and thus the other prophets and saints of God shone with the greatest powers. REX. You tell this quite rightly; but come, I beseech you, tell me: what is the soul of man? LAZ. I will not answer now according to the opinion of Aristotle <note>original: "secundum Aristotelis sententiam"</note>—that it is the form of an organic body—nor according to the various pleasures of the philosophers: that it is an essence, or a self-moving number, or a harmony, or an idea, or the function of the five senses breathing together, or a thin spirit dispersed through the whole body, or a vapor, or a spark of stellar essence, or a spirit grown together with the body, or a spirit inserted with atoms, or fire, or air, or water, or blood, or that it consists of every essence, or of earth and fire together, or of earth and water, or of air and fire. Perhaps indeed these things are true according to various modes of proportions, adapted by metaphor. I, however (as I have found and hold for certain), answer you, illustrious King, according to the Hebrew *Mecubales* <note>A Latinization of the Hebrew 'Mekubbalim', meaning receivers of tradition or Kabbalists.</note>, or Kabbalists: <margin>Kabbalists.</margin> namely, that the soul of man is the light of God. Or, I answer you with the same words by which Poimandres defined it to Hermes: <margin>Hermes.</margin> That which sees and hears within you is the Word of the Lord, the Mind.

HER. 73 74

I feel just as Glaucus <note>A mythological fisherman who became a sea-god after eating a magical herb.</note> did in Euboean Anthedon, when he felt himself transformed into the nature of a sea deity by the taste of a wondrous grass—a story I used to hear among fables when I was a boy. It is necessary, O King, to pursue this truth through frequent acts of wonder, entreaties, praises, and contemplations, and by dwelling long upon divine matters. So that men might be better able to achieve this, Hermes says <margin>Hermes</margin> that the Muses descended to mankind. Turn here, O King, all the powers of your Muses—those you may have heard of from Pontano <note>Giovanni Pontano (1429–1503), a famous Italian humanist and poet.</note> or any other poet—and apply the strength of your genius to this. Beseech, wonder at, praise, and contemplate the divinity. For in this way, you will be suitably prepared for the greatest and deifying secret of the mystery which (provided God is present) I am about to bring forth. In these things also (as Hermes says), heaven and all the heavenly beings take delight. KING: It must be done as you advise, so that the deifying [influence] of the All may flow into us...

# CRATINUS.

He gave mind and word to man:
The true image of the Father:
He who is the mind and the word <note>original: "mens/sermoq;" - referring to the Logos or Christ as the Divine Reason and Speech</note> of the Father.
Blessed be he:
Both the Word and the Mind.
These two have established
The world.
Who, while he fills all things,
Elevates man alone:
He absorbs, transforms, and turns him into God,
Our spirit-bearing God.
Blessed be he:
The nourishing Spirit.
And let us sing of him:
Who fills the world.
Who purifies us from bodily filth
By his sacred breath:
Inhabiting the human form:
The God who dwells in the holy.
Blessed be he:
Whose temple we are.
Sing praises together:

Born of the Virgin.
O man, speak odes
To the offspring of the Virgin.

<meta>This page continues a philosophical dialogue between a King and a teacher named Lazarus, likely following a discussion on the divine nature of humanity and the power of sacred hymns.</meta>

->HERMES<-

<note>original: "est persequemur"</note> ...we shall pursue. **KING:** I have been inflamed with a great love toward God by this hymn of yours, which has extolled man with such great praises. And I am not only set on fire by love, but I am also placed almost outside of myself in amazement, just as usually happens to those who by chance have come upon a torpedo fish <note>The torpedo fish or electric ray was a common classical metaphor for being "stunned" or "numbed" by a powerful experience</note>. For such did your hymn appear: not one that proceeded from the inspiration of the Muses, as they say, but by the divine power of the Word of God. What wonder is it, then, if it inflames the mind, if it calls it forth, and snatches it outside itself?

**LAZARUS:** Effort must therefore be given in the future so that such love perseveres, grows warm, and increases day by day. For love itself, by turning lower things toward the heavenly, unites and connects them together in turn. Truly, contemplation first excites love; then love converts the human mind toward God. Once converted, the mind is so formed by God that—resuming its innate vigor (which it had lost through the affections for material things)—it may work, with its virtue made whole, things more admirable and greater than the very nature of heaven itself.

**KING:** We must dwell upon divine love as you advise; but tell me, I pray, what works does the human mind, thus informed, undergo?

**LAZARUS:** You ask prudently, best of Kings. For that which anyone is ignorant of, he will never rightly perform. Be present now, therefore, with the whole intention of your mind; so that you, who while remaining in the body are a King appointed by God, may also—by flying out from the body through separation and being surrounded by spiritual light—persevere as a King in perpetual dominion. We are now in the vestibule of the darkness in which God dwells. Pay attention, therefore, so that you may more readily be able to enter into the most lucid darkness of God <note>The "lucid darkness" is a mystical concept where God's light is so bright it appears as darkness to human senses</note>.

**KING:** I am indeed paying attention, desiring nothing more than that you might perhaps introduce me.

**LAZARUS:** It is necessary for us, O King, first among other things to understand that God is fruitful, since He is the author and progenitor of all things. For which reason, <margin>Hermes</margin> Hermes calls Him most full with the fecundity of both sexes. <margin>Orpheus</margin> Orpheus...

->CRAT.<-

Therefore, Hermes asserted that the mind and speech are of the same value as immortality. If anyone uses these in a way that is fitting, he judged that they would in no way differ from the immortals; rather, he asserted they would eventually be led by these into the choirs of the blessed. For these two, O King, joined together, give birth to a divine offspring.

KING. I would hardly doubt that to be true, O Lazarel, if perhaps you mean to imply that the sciences and the fine arts are the offspring of the mind—those things which are first conceived by the mind and, through the word, are brought forth to the external senses as if to a birth, and are preserved for posterity through writings.

LAZAREL. Both the sciences and the fine arts are indeed the offshoots of the mind, but they are produced through a generation that is, in a certain way, <term>homonymous</term> <note>original: "homonyma"; referring to things that share a name but have different essential natures</note>. However, I am discussing here a <term>univocal</term> <note>original: "vniuoca"; referring to things that share both a name and the same essential nature</note> and (so to speak) kindred generation of the mind, so that the one begotten is the same as the parent. For in a univocal birth, a thing is always produced by its own likeness.

KING. What is that, I beseech you? Do not keep the narrative in suspense any longer.

LAZAREL. Consider now, O King: if the body is of such power that it produces a body like itself, what could stand in the way of the mind generating a mind, which is more excellent than the body?

KING. Perhaps you are arguing, O Lazarel, that a begotten mind is produced from the mind of a parent?

LAZAREL. Our discourse is not presently engaged in that matter, but rather in that generation of the mind which, when Hermes had revealed it to Asclepius, the latter—turned toward ecstasy and an excess of the mind—exclaimed that he was vehemently confounded by the incredible magnitude of the thing, and that he judged that man would be most happy who had attained such a great gift.

KING. There is no need, Lazarel, to stretch my mind with such a great circuit of words. For like a jar full of new wine that has no vent, I am almost bursting from too much tension; digest more quickly what you are entrusting to me.

LAZAREL. Behold, O good King, beloved of God, I feel I must comply with your wish—moved not by a <term>Socratic demon</term> <note>original: "socratico demone"; a reference to the 'daimonion' or inner divine voice that guided Socrates</note>, but by the spirit of JESUS Christ, who dwells in his worshippers. Lo, I will...

->CRATYLUS<-

Before the ages, he begot his offspring; then, in his own time,
He was covered (as the fates granted) by a bodily house,
So that he might redeem the human race.
<margin>20</margin> Behold, love—or rather a madness—bursts within me,
Conceived by the torch with which your light radiates through me.
These secrets, known to me for but a short time,
Willingly open the heavens to me.
I now begin nothing humble or light;
I sing of nothing mortal or small,
But rather something remarkable to many souls, something fresh
And as yet unplayed by any other lyre.
That which was forbidden to the ancients, and which
Was scarcely permitted to be spoken in parables of old:
Now you grant me leave to bring it forth in plain verses,
So that men may become teachable.
For thus it is sung in the sacred prophecies:
Since in the final times, O Father,
You shall be manifest to all; then your teachable,
<margin>35</margin> Pious men shall reign.
Behold, now I begin. Let all things, hushed in silence,
Listen with attentive ears to my sounds,
Which are heavy with divine eloquence.
Behold, I touch the lyre with my fingers.
This is surely the new novelty of novelties,
And something greater than all wonders:
That man should have discovered the nature of God,
And that the wise man creates that very nature.
For just as the Lord or God the Father,
<margin>45</margin> Generating the heavenly beings, procreates the angels—
Who are the forms of things, the heads of all,
The patterns <note>original: "exemplaria," referring to the Platonic Ideas or archetypes</note>, and the first principles—
So the true man creates divine souls.

CRAT.
He is unrefined: let him hear this. THE KING. It must clearly be done as you advise. For if they say the goddesses of Eleusis <note>Demeter and Persephone, the central figures of the Eleusinian Mysteries</note> were angry with the philosopher Eumenius—a man curious about hidden things—because he had betrayed the sacred rites of Eleusis to the common people: it is much more right to believe that the supreme Creator of all things will be indignant if his secret mysteries are spread everywhere. LAZAREL. It is no wonder, O most happy King. For if any novelty of things (however small) changes the senses: much more and more excellently does this very thing work, which by its particular sublimity strikes back at and utterly blunts not only the senses, but even the very gaze of the mind.

->In the middle, the red and virgin earth—that is, *Adamah* <note>original: "adamā id est terrā rubram"; a reference to the Hebrew etymology of Adam, meaning "red earth"</note>—must be dug out. This, in my opinion, is to be understood as follows: the "desert mountains" are the divine sages, who are called "deserted" because they are despised by the common crowd. This aligns with that passage from the Book of Wisdom: *We fools esteemed their life to be madness.* <note>Wisdom 5:4</note> And Hermes <margin>Hermes</margin> says that those who dedicate themselves to this wisdom please neither the common people, nor do the common people please them. They are eventually thought to be insane and are met with laughter; sometimes they are even held in hatred, afflicted with insults, and deprived of life. From Plato <margin>Plato</margin> also, in the *Phaedrus*, we have this: "He who is separated from human pursuits and clings to the divine is rebuked by the multitude as if he were out of his mind; but being filled with God himself, he is hidden from the multitude." <note>original: "extra se positus"; literally "placed outside himself" or in a state of ecstasy</note>

I shall therefore defer this to another time, in a more hidden and solitary place, imitating the wise men of the Hebrews. For regarding that word in the Book of Genesis—*Abraham gave all that he possessed to Isaac, but to the sons of his concubines he bestowed gifts*—the Kabbalists <margin>Kabbalists</margin> <note>original: "Cabaliſte"; practitioners of Jewish mysticism</note> explain it thus: those things given to the sons of the concubines were *shemoth shel tumah* <note>original: "ſcemoth ſteltoma"; Hebrew for 'names of impurity'</note>, that is, names of uncleanness, namely the magical arts. However, those things given to Isaac were certain divine secrets which, because they were revealed from mouth to ear, they call "Kabbalah" <note>meaning 'received tradition'</note>. This name has begun to be known among certain people in our time; nevertheless, its actual operation (if I except only one person) remains entirely hidden from all. But you, O blessed King, preserve what we have said with a mindful and faithful spirit, and give thanks to God at the conclusion of this present discourse, for He has called us together for such a great gift.

**KING:** These are your duties, O Lazarello, you who excel in grace and eloquence.

**LAZARELLO:** Because you command it, O King, I shall perform it. But in this act of thanksgiving, I shall use not my own words, but those of Jesus Christ, so that our prayer may be more acceptable and more sacred to God Himself.

**KING:** Do as you please.

**LAZARELLO:** I give thanks to You, Lord Jesus Christ, because You have hidden these things from the prudent and the wise, and have revealed them to little ones. Yet it seems fitting to me, O King, that just as we have conducted almost this entire day's discourse through speech, so we should impose an end to the discourse with some Hymn of thanksgiving, especially in this matter. For no one will ever be able to sufficiently fulfill words of praise toward God. I shall therefore praise Christ Jesus under the name of *Poimandres* <margin>Hermes</margin> <note>original: "Pimadri"; referring to the 'Divine Pymander' or 'Shepherd of Men' from the Hermetic corpus</note>, which is interpreted by Hermes as both the Mind and the Word of divine power. But you, O King, be favorable in spirit, and with me, prostrate and suppliant, adore the Word itself, the author of all things.

Light of the Father, radiating Word, Poimandres <note>original: "Pimander"; the "Shepherd of Men," a divine title for the Mind of God in Hermetic literature</note>,
Mind that utterly decreed the fates from eternal ages,
And you are the craftsman-origin of all things:
Praise to you, virtue, honor, and triumph,
Glory and kingdom, beauty and power.
You roll the stars in a slanted curve <note>original: "sinu obliquo"; likely referring to the ecliptic path of the planets</note> beneath your feet,
In the manner of a serpent you press down and release
The dreadful shadows, roaring with a great noise.
Praise to you, virtue, honor, and triumph,
Glory and kingdom, beauty and power.
And you, like a bird, cherish moist nature
With the warmth of the Word, as if warming eggs;
From there, each of the elements flows forth.
Praise to you, virtue, honor, and triumph,
Glory and kingdom, beauty and power.
You perform every work for all things,
And whatever enjoys life, you bring forth
In fire, air, earth, or the sea.
Praise to you, virtue, honor, and triumph,
Glory and kingdom, beauty and power.
You command the race of men to be joined
With divine light, and you bind them back to yourself.
You turn the days, and you dissolve the darkness.
Praise to you, virtue, honor, and triumph,
Glory and kingdom, beauty and power.
When man had lost his own strengths,
You recall him to the stars, joined inwardly
To the Son of the Virgin, and made flesh. <note>The text here blends Hermetic philosophy with Christian theology regarding the Incarnation.</note>
Praise to you, virtue, honor, and triumph,
Glory and kingdom, beauty and power.
Behold, when he had fallen again
Among human limbs, you are seen once more <margin>S M N La G Se Se Lu La G Q Ce Di La G O E I L G Q Pa D La G T N S L</margin> <note>The marginalia appears to be a series of shorthand abbreviations or initials, possibly corresponding to the first letters of the lines or musical cues, though they are partially obscured.</note>

<summary>A rhythmic hymn of praise addressed to Poimandres (the Divine Mind), celebrating the creation of the elements, the governance of the stars, and the restoration of humanity through a "Son of a Virgin." It represents a synthesis of Hermetic mysticism and Christian doctrine typical of the Renaissance.</summary>
<keywords>Poimandres, Hermeticism, Creation, Elements, Incarnation, Liturgy, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the poetic or liturgical sequence, likely from a Neo-Latin drama or a collection of humanist hymns. The speaker "HER." likely refers to a character such as a Hermit or a Herald.</meta>

HER. <note>Likely an abbreviation for a character name, such as Heremita (The Hermit) or Heraldus (The Herald)</note> ->page 82<-

As the ancient prophets <note>original: "vates," meaning seers or poets</note> once sang,
Praise to you, virtue and honor and triumph,
Glory and kingship, beauty and power,
Just as it is read in the ancient oracles:
The great and the small shall be wise as one <note>A reference to universal divine knowledge</note>
The world has no need for frequent warnings,
Praise to you, virtue and honor and triumph,
Glory and kingship, beauty and power,
But the days yield to the servants of works,
A seventh rest of light has come upon the lands; <note>A reference to the Sabbath or the final age of rest</note>
Behold, the mind is joined to the human,
Praise to you, virtue and honor and triumph,
Glory and kingship, beauty and power,
Each one shall give voice with a divine mouth,
The world shall echo the praises of the heavenly beings,
The gods <note>original: "Dij," likely used here in a poetic or angelic sense within a Christian humanist context</note> will be present together on earth in great numbers,
Praise to you, virtue and honor and triumph,
Glory and kingship, beauty and power,
All things shall rise again into their original form,
Behold, the kingdom of God (which you now command
to be called forth by your prayer) hastens to come,
Praise to you, virtue and honor and triumph,
Glory and kingship, beauty and power,
Those two which were once separate are now one fold: <note>A reference to the unification of peoples or the soul and body</note>
Behold, one Shepherd rules, and leads them
to healthy pastures where the grass is soft,
Praise to you, virtue and honor and triumph,
Glory and kingship, beauty and power,
Then they shall drink living waters everywhere,
The wolf shall not harm there, nor the lion,
No thorn shall tear the white fleece,
Praise to you, virtue and honor and triumph,

<summary>A rhythmic hymn or choral ode celebrating the coming of a divine kingdom, the restoration of the world to its original state, and the arrival of a single Shepherd to guide humanity.</summary>
<keywords>prophecy, triumph, divine kingdom, Sabbath rest, Good Shepherd, restoration, golden age</keywords>

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dialogue between King Ferdinand and Ludovico Lazzarelli, following the previous discussion on divine mysteries.</meta>

# 83

->**CRATER**<-
<note>original: "CRAT." referring to the "Crater" or "Mixing Bowl," a title derived from the Hermetic tradition symbolizing divine wisdom poured out for humanity.</note>
**Glory and kingdom, honor and power.**
<note>original: "Gloria et regnum/decor et potestas." A formal doxology or closing prayer of praise.</note>

This thanksgiving having been fully performed, the King—made more vigorous by the disputation and more eager in mind—withdrew into the inner chambers of the palace, praying that the rising of another sun would bring a similar day. Lazzarelli, however, having greeted the King with due veneration (as was proper) and promising his efforts for the following day, departed late at night.

<column-break/>

->The End of the **Crater of Hermes**,<-
->added to the **Pimander** and **Asclepius**<-
->of **Hermes Trismegistus**.<-
<note>These titles refer to the "Corpus Hermeticum," a collection of Greek-Egyptian wisdom texts attributed to the legendary sage Hermes Trismegistus.</note>

->At Paris, in the workshop of **Henri Estienne**,<-
->with the proofreaders<-
->cleansing the errors from the workshop:<-
->**Johannes Solidus** of Krakow<-
->and **Volgacius Pratensis**.<-
->In the year of our Lord and Savior<-
->**1505**, on the Calends of **APRIL**.<-
<note>April 1st, 1505. Henri Estienne was a famous humanist printer in Paris.</note>
